```json
{"status":"deduplicated","error_msg":"Skipped: similar to recent task task-f02c283d","original_task_id":"task-f02c283d"}
```

task-f02c283d **was** the failure. Suppressing against it made the failure
permanent — the fingerprint is a simhash of the message CONTENT, so a fresh
message id and a new title changed nothing, and the only way to re-run the work
was to reword it. The deploy key the retry existed to exercise was never touched.

Two more claims in that message were false, and both came from the same place:

- **"similar"** — both stores match the fingerprint EXACTLY. The `threshold
  float64` parameter on `FindDuplicateTask` was read by neither, so the signature
  now takes the cutoff it actually uses.
- **"recent"** — the match had no time bound at all. A fingerprint from any point
  in history suppressed a new request forever.

The policy now lives in one place (`internal/coordinator/task_dedup.go`) as a
table over every task status, with the same exhaustiveness test as
`terminalByStatus`, so the next status added gets a deliberate answer instead of
the zero value. `failed`, `rejected`, `cancelled`, `no_changes` and `duplicate`
no longer suppress; in-flight and recently-completed work still does; an unknown
status does not, because the cost of one extra run is a task and the cost of the
other direction is a request that can never be made again. Matches are bounded to
`DedupWindow` (24h).

The two stores had also drifted: SQLite excluded `cancelled` in its SQL and
Firestore excluded nothing, so the cloud coordinator and the local one gave
different answers to the same question. Both now call `BlocksDuplicate`.

### Fixed — the deduplicated completion carried no `correlation_id`

The same report. Of the three publishers of `completion` messages, the dedup path
was the only one that omitted the correlation (the Pub/Sub and stale-task paths
both set `task.MessageID`) — so a client with a request in flight received a reply
it could not attribute, and went on waiting through three polls for an answer it
had already been sent. That is the exact hang the dedup notification exists to
prevent. It now carries the originating message id both as the message
`correlation_id` and in the payload, plus `original_task_status` so a client can
tell "already running" from "already done" without a second lookup.

### `ailang coordinator agent-check` — adding an agent stops being eight silent failures

Adding one agent (daneel-writer) took eight rounds on 2026-09-11, and every
failure was silent: the config looked right and nothing worked. Each is now a
check, and each names its own fix.

| check | the failure it catches |
|---|---|
| repo/live in sync | a bucket edit made before a later repo edit, never re-synced — **three** drifts in one day. The bucket is a cache; a config deploy regenerates it from the repo |
| no dead config keys | `push_branch: dev` reads as decisive, but `AgentConfig` has no such field (direct push is `skip_approval` + `merge_branch`). YAML drops unknown keys silently |
| target branch exists | `merge_branch: main` against a repo whose default is `dev` and which has no `main`. PR creation is best-effort, so the task reports success having opened nothing |
| can write to repo | a `GET` returns 200 for a read-only collaborator; only the `permissions` block answers push |
| ssh key secret | the secret exists **and** the executor's service account can read it — existence is not access |
| settings coherent | flags that cannot fire together: `auto_merge` with a deploy key (SSH cannot use the API), with direct push (no PR), or with no declared artifacts |
| skill present | `invoke.type=skill` resolves from the cloned workspace, so a missing `SKILL.md` means nothing to run |
| coordinator current | the config was written but the service never rolled, so routing still follows the previous registry |

**A check that cannot run reports UNKNOWN, never OK**, and unknowns exit non-zero.
Several of these need permissions the CLI may not have — listing deploy keys
needs repo admin and GitHub answers 404 rather than 403, so "no keys" and "not
allowed to look" are the same response. Reporting that as a pass is how the
original mistake happened.

### Fixed — `auto_merge` without declared artifacts had unlimited scope

Found by writing the test above. The auto-merge guard was reading
`GetEffectiveArtifactPatterns()`, which falls back to **`**/*`** for any
unrecognised agent — so an agent with `auto_merge: true` and no declared
patterns got the widest possible scope, and the scope half of the guard
contributed nothing. It now reads the DECLARED patterns, and an empty list is a
refusal. The markdown floor was still holding, so nothing unsafe shipped, but the
bound only existed for agents that happened to declare one.

## [v0.38.1] - 2026-09-12

Unblocks v0.38.0, whose tag was cut but whose image build aborted at `ci-gate`
with dev red. Five blockers, found one at a time because CI stops at the first
failing step. Two were mine; three were pre-existing, and two of those turned out
to be real bugs that only ever surfaced on a platform nobody ran.

### Fixed — `rig_yield_pending` used BSD-only `date -j`, so on Linux it DELETED every handoff

`date -j -f` is BSD-only. On Linux the parse fails, `until_epoch` comes back
empty, and the next branch reads "cannot parse" as "expired" — and removes the
handoff file. So a Linux caller silently destroyed a live handoff just by looking
at it. The rig is macOS, so production never saw it; the only platform exercising
the path was the one it worked on. Reproduced 100% on Linux and 0% on macOS,
which is exactly what made it look like a flake.

### Fixed — a negative `--max-wall-clock` meant UNLIMITED

`if maxWallClock > 0` treated a negative cap as no cap, so `--max-wall-clock=-5m`
— a plausible typo for a ceiling — silently removed the bound being asked for. On
the single-GPU rig an unbounded suite holds the lock all night. Unlimited is the
one answer a negative ceiling cannot mean.

Its test had been passing a 1ns cap and relying on the clock ticking between the
deadline being computed and first read: true on Linux and macOS, false on Windows
(~15.6ms granularity), where both jobs dispatched. A negative cap is expired by
construction, so the test is now deterministic everywhere.

### Fixed — the docs-only CI lane took 19 hygiene gates with it

Putting the whole `test` job behind `if:` skipped `check-no-personal-email`,
`check-referenced-paths` and `check-context-docs` among others — gates that are
about **documentation** as much as code. `TestWiredGatesAreCanonical` exists to
catch a gate hiding behind a condition, and did. Only the OS/compiler-specific
jobs skip now.

### Fixed — new git exec sites, and a stale stdlib freeze

Three `exec.Command("git", …)` sites added with the deploy-key work now go
through `gitexec`, which resolves git by absolute path rather than a PATH lookup
— worth having in a container whose PATH a task can influence. And `env`, `fs`
and `list` were re-frozen: `getPid`, `mkdirResult`, `removeDirResult` and
`contains` landed unfrozen. Verified additive-only before accepting, because
`freeze-stdlib.sh` warns that re-freezing is not a check.

## [v0.38.0] - 2026-09-11

### The notify daemon supervises its subscriptions, and pulls the one it was told to

Two distinct bugs behind five days of "the daemon has stalled again", found after
a report that the process was alive, the subscription ACTIVE on Google's side, and
nothing delivered.

**Supervision (`internal/daemon/daemon.go`).** Each subscription was started once,
in a goroutine, with no restart. `sub.Receive` returns on any unrecoverable stream
error; when one hit that, its goroutine exited, the error landed in a **buffered**
channel, and `wg.Wait()` kept blocking because the other goroutine was still
alive. `Run` never returned, the error was never read, and the process stayed up
receiving nothing. Subscriptions now restart with capped backoff (1s → 2m), a
returned `nil` counts as an outage too, and the outage is **announced** through
the daemon's own fan-out — once per outage — because restart-alone would have
turned a five-day silence into a five-day flap.

**The wrong subscription.** The rig's plist passed `--extra-messages-sub
messages-rig`, meaning "this device pulls messages-rig". That flag renames the
subscription for *extra* sources, and there were none, so the daemon printed
`extra_message_sources=[]` and pulled `messages-laptop` — shared with another
machine, and Pub/Sub **work-steals**, so each saw only part of the traffic. New
`--messages-sub` names the primary; `--extra-messages-sub` with no extra envs is
now refused, naming the fix. Verified on the rig: a long-untouched backlog drained.

### Auto-merge, and PR titles that say what the change is

- **Auto-merge now exists.** `auto_merge` was a config field the autonomy router
  read and **nothing acted on** — no merge call anywhere in the codebase. The
  wrapper now enables GitHub's **native** auto-merge (squash): GitHub waits for
  the required checks and honours branch protection, so there is no polling loop
  of ours to get wrong.
- **Scope comes from the agent's declaration.** Two conditions, both required:
  every changed file matches the agent's `artifact_patterns`, **and** every
  changed file is markdown. The first is per-agent and therefore per-repo — a
  hardcoded `design_docs/|changelogs/` list is an AILANG-repo notion and would
  silently disable auto-merge for an agent working anywhere else. The second is
  the floor: `**/*` is a legitimate declaration, so scope alone would merge
  anything the day someone flips a flag.
- **A docs-only CI lane.** `test-windows`, `build`, `launchd-drivers` and
  `govulncheck` skip when only `design_docs/**.md` or `changelogs/**.md` changed.
  Job-level, deliberately: a workflow-level `paths` filter leaves the required
  contexts never reporting and branch protection blocks forever. The `test` job
  is **not** gated — it carries 19 hygiene gates, several of them about
  documentation, and `TestWiredGatesAreCanonical` catches any attempt to skip them.
- **Readable titles.** The subject was the entire directive, unbounded — raw JSON
  for a structured request. It is now a bounded summary, with the JSON field
  chosen by a fixed precedence list rather than map iteration, so the same
  directive always yields the same subject.

### `coordinator config set` rolls the coordinator — writing was not deploying

The config bucket is a gcsfuse mount, so a write updates the file the container
sees within seconds; the agent registry is built **once at startup**, so routing
kept following the old config. Measured: an inbox declared `triage_only` still
bounced after a successful write. `set` now rolls the service, `config roll` is
the recovery path, and a write that lands without a roll exits **non-zero** —
the file is durable, the plane is not yet using it, and those are different facts.

### Added — `daneel-writer`

Daneel's research and drafting agent, documents-only into `sunholo-data/daneel-memory`,
auto-merged on that basis. Registered in `ailang-multivac/config/config.cloud.yaml`
rather than the bucket, because a config deploy regenerates the bucket from the repo.

### Structured `Debug.log` lines survive every host sink (M-DEBUG-SINK-STRUCTURED-LINES)

A Cloud Run service wrote correct structured logs —
`Debug.log("{\"severity\":\"ERROR\",...}")` — and Cloud Logging stored them as
`textPayload` at `DEFAULT` severity, because the line that reached stderr was
`2026/09/09 13:32:14 [Debug] {"severity":"ERROR",...}`. `severity>=ERROR`
returned nothing. The reporter correctly traced it to the AILANG sink; no flag
could remove the prefix.

The audit found four faults in the same surface, two host sinks that each
formatted Debug output their own way, plus a copy-pasted severity filter:

| Site | Fault |
|---|---|
| `serve-api` | `log.Printf("[Debug] %s")` — Go's timestamp + prefix on every line |
| `serve-api` | failed `Debug.check` never structured — invisible to any severity filter |
| `run --batch` | `[<input>] ` label prefix on every line, JSON included |
| `serve-api` without `--caps` | no effect context at all — **every Debug line silently dropped** |

**Fixed** — one `effects.DebugSink` shared by `run`, `run --batch` and
`serve-api`: a line that is a bare JSON object is written verbatim on its own
line (no label, no timestamp); everything else keeps exactly its previous
decoration. `serve-api` emits a failed check as
`{"severity":"ERROR","message":"assertion failed: …","location":…,"source":"Debug.check"}`;
the CLI keeps the human `[ASSERT FAIL]` line. `serve-api` always constructs
its effect context, so Debug output no longer depends on `--caps`. The two
duplicate severity parsers are deleted. `--log-level` semantics unchanged
(a structured line with no `severity` still always passes).

**`location` is the real call site, and `--release` erasure actually fires.**
Every Debug line carried `location: "unknown"` — `std/debug.ail` passed a
literal `"unknown"` and the "auto-injected by the compiler" the docs promised
had never been written. A Core pass (`pipeline.DebugLocationInjector`, run on
lowered Core in both pipelines) rewrites a direct `log(msg)` / `check(c, msg)`
into the underlying builtin with the call site appended, so the structured
check line now reads `"location":"api/orders.ail:12"` (relative to the working
directory when the file is under it; absolute otherwise; always `/`-separated).
Two consequences surfaced by the same fixture:

- `--release` never erased user-level Debug calls — the eraser matched the
  builtin by name and user code called the wrapper. It now matches the
  `$builtin` shape the injector produces; `--release` output is just `pong`.
- The compile cache key had no notion of pipeline mode, so one `--release`
  compile was served to every later normal run (all Debug output silently
  gone) and vice versa. `ReleaseMode` is now part of the compiler identity in
  the key; normal keys are unchanged.

Regression tests pin the wire bytes end-to-end (in-process apiserver with
captured stderr; a spawned no-`--caps` `serve-api`; the batch fixture asserts
the JSON line is a whole line); both mutation checks — old server sink, old
flag-gated context — fail them. Design: `design_docs/implemented/v0_37_3/m-debug-sink-structured-lines.md`.

### Six Daneel reports from one morning: five small fixes, one design doc

All measured against HEAD before touching anything. Two were the feature
already existing behind a name nobody could find; three were real gaps small
enough to close in the session; one is real and needs design.

| Report | Verdict | Change |
|---|---|---|
| `--args-json` cannot decode a `list[...]` parameter | **bug** — `list[T]` is `TApp{list,[T]}` since DX-17 and the decoder only knew the legacy `TList`, so *every* list argument failed with `unsupported type for argument decoding: *types.TApp` | `argdecode` goes through `types.AsList`; top-level, nested and record-field lists decode; non-list `TApp` still refused |
| no `--net-timeout`; `httpRequest` hard 30s ceiling | **gap** | `--net-timeout <duration>` on `ailang run`, mirroring `--process-timeout`; wire-proved both ways (50ms fails a 300ms server, 2s succeeds) |
| SMT-encodable list membership for `ensures` | **export gap** — `_list_contains` has been SMT-encoded as `(seq.contains xs (seq.unit x))` and the encoder's own table already mapped `std/list.contains` to it; `std/list` just never exported `contains` | `std/list.contains(xs, x)`; `ensures { result == "" \|\| contains(allowed, result) }` now **verifies**. `member` stays runtime-only (arg order differs) and says so |
| no `mkdirResult` — atomic try-lock aborts the run on contention | **gap** | `std/fs.mkdirResult` (Err on exists — exactly one of N concurrent callers gets Ok) and `removeDirResult` (empty dir only, never recursive) |
| no `getPid` | **gap** | `std/env.getPid() -> int ! {Env}` — the AILANG `$$` |
| `toLower`/`toUpper`/`foldChars` not SMT-encodable | **real, not small** — bounded ASCII case-fold map and a monomorphic fold recurrence in the encoder | design doc, not this release |

### `exit(code)` is findable from where a CLI author looks

`exit(code) -> () ! {IO}` has been in `std/io` since v0.10.1, and a CLI
author looking in `std/process` — the module that *runs* programs — found
nothing and reported "no way for an AILANG program to exit non-zero"
(email-parse, 2026-09-04). Same failure class as Process missing from
`--help`: the feature existed, the surface a reader checks did not say so.

**Fixed** — `ailang docs <module>` now prints the module's whole header, not
just its first line; the first line was all it ever showed, which hid every
module's security flags and semantics notes (std/process's 15 lines,
std/ai's 30). `std/process`'s header now points at `std/io.exit`, `std/io`'s
one-liner names it, and `reference/effects.md` gains an *Exit code*
subsection with the per-surface table (`run`, `--batch`, served
route/A2A/MCP handlers). The M-EXIT-CODE design doc no longer says
`Status: Planned`.

### `--caps IO,FS,Nett` is an error, not a program missing a capability

`--caps NOPE,IO` ran the program and said nothing (#1116, Daneel). `--caps`
is a security boundary — a script granted `IO,FS` cannot reach the network,
and that property is asserted in authority docs — so a typo that runs
silently is a boundary nobody can read off the invocation.

**Fixed** — every `--caps` name must be a known effect. An unknown one exits
1 before anything runs, naming the entry, the closest valid name
(`unknown capability "Nett" in --caps (did you mean Net?)`) and the full
list; nothing is granted on rejection. `run`, `--batch` and `serve-api` share
the one parser. `--caps auto` is unaffected: it only ever yields names the
type checker already accepted.

### `--process-allowlist git:status` — a binary narrowed to its subcommands

Allowlisting `git` granted `git push --force`. For a delegate whose audit trail
is a git repository that is the one command the allowlist exists to refuse
(#1137, Daneel); the workaround was an argument-free wrapper script per binary.

**Added** — an entry may name a subcommand chain: `git:status,git:commit,gh:pr:list`.
The runtime refuses every other invocation of that binary with
`NotAllowed("git push")`, naming what was tried. Matching is positional from
the first argument (`git -C /x status` is refused under `git:status` — fail
closed rather than parse option grammars); arguments after the chain are free
(`git:commit` allows `--amend`: per subcommand, not per flag); a bare `git` or
`git:*` in the same list is the broadest grant and wins; an empty segment is a
startup error, never a wider allow.

**The part the design doc missed** — the allowlist was checked in three
hand-copied blocks: `exec`, `spawnProcess` and `asyncExecProcess`. Narrowing
only `exec` would have left `spawnProcess("git", ["push"])` open. All three now
call one `(*ProcessContext).Authorize`, and a test drives the refusal through
each path. `examples/runnable/process_subcmd_allowlist.ail`;
`reference/effects.md` has the rules.

### The Process effect is now in `ailang --help`

Agents grepping the help text for `Process` found nothing and concluded the
effect did not exist. `ailang --help` listed `--caps` as `IO,FS,Net,AI`;
`ailang run --help` said `IO,FS,Net,Env,Process`; `serve-api` carried two more
lists of its own — four hand-typed copies, no two alike, none complete. Daneel
found `--process-allowlist` only by reading `ailang run --help` line by line
(#1137), and its design doc for per-subcommand narrowing had sat in
`design_docs/implemented/` with `Status: Planned` since a bulk reorganisation,
so the roadmap read the per-binary half as unbuilt too.

**Fixed** — one `CapsList` constant (`cmd/ailang/run_helpers.go`) feeds every
`--caps` usage string, and `TestCapsList_CoversEveryRegisteredEffect` fails the
build if an effect is registered at runtime without appearing in it. `ailang
--help` also names `--process-allowlist`. Docs: `reference/effects.md` documents
the four Process flags as a table (path-pinned resolution, per-binary
granularity, wrapper-script pattern — measured on HEAD: an allowlisted bash
wrapper runs, `/bin/bash` itself is `NotAllowed`); M-EFFECT-REFINEMENT gains a
Process row; `m-process-subcmd-allowlist` moved to `design_docs/planned/` with
Daneel as consumer on record.

### The rig lock can be asked to step aside

The rig is a single GPU with several tenants, and its lock had exactly two modes
— wait and no-wait — with no priority and no way to hand over. That made a
ten-hour batch job and a forty-second interactive classification equals, and the
batch always won: it arrived first and never let go. Measured 2026-09-11, with
the nightly eval holding the lock 03:00→13:00:

| tenant | outcome |
|---|---|
| `nightly-eval` | held the lock 9h45m (09-10), two of 24 runs spending the full 1h hard timeout for nothing |
| `os-rotation-filler` | one acquisition in a day; otherwise yielding or skipped |
| Daneel mail intake | deferred on **83%** of runs (34/41), mail fetched but never triaged |

**Added — cooperative yield** (`internal/riglock/yield.go`,
`tools/launchd/rig-lock.sh`). A short job writes a handoff marker naming itself
and a deadline; the holder notices it at a checkpoint where the GPU is idle —
for `eval-suite`, between benchmarks — releases, waits, and re-acquires. The
marker lives *outside* the lock directory so it survives the release: otherwise
the gap the holder opens is just a free lock, and the 45-minute background
filler wins the race. No-wait acquirers that are not the named requester are
refused while a handoff is in force. Every handoff carries an expiry and a pid,
so a requester that dies cannot strand the holder. The checkpoint is a single
stat when nothing is pending, and a no-op for a run that does not hold the lock.

`eval-suite` checkpoints only at `--parallel 1` — with siblings still streaming,
releasing the lock would hand out a promise that is already false. Every rig job
runs `--parallel 1`, so it is live exactly where it matters.

**Added — `eval-suite --max-wall-clock`.** `--max-tokens-per-bench` and
`--timeout` bound one benchmark; nothing bounded a suite, so a night could run
away in aggregate. The cap stops *dispatch* only: in-flight trials finish,
everything banked stays banked, the suite finalizes, and a
`wallclock_stopped.json` sentinel marks the run partial — the same contract as
`budget_stopped.json`. `nightly-eval.sh` now budgets 8h for the *night*, divided
between the arms that will run, so an A/B Monday costs the rig the same as an
ordinary Tuesday. A truncated arm is refused for banking: the cap cuts wherever
each arm happened to be, so the pair is no longer paired and the delta is not a
treatment effect.

**Changed — the filler's blackout window is derived, not fixed.** The 04:00–07:00
blackout was sized for a ~3h nightly and had stopped being true at both ends. It
is now a pre-nightly guard (default one hour) computed from the nightly's own
start time, read from its installed plist so the two cannot drift. What covers
the nightly *while it runs* is the rig lock, which the filler already yields to.

**Fixed — the filler logged every line twice.** `log()` piped through `tee -a`
into the same file the plist already captures stdout to. Harmless to read, but
it silently doubled every count taken from that log — which is the instrument
used to judge how much rig time the filler is getting.

**Fixed — a crashed Daneel lock could not be reclaimed for six hours.**
`riglock` reads the first field of the holder file as a pid to decide whether a
dead holder can be stolen from; Daneel wrote `daneel intake pid=N …` there,
which parses as not-a-number and so always read as "assume alive". Both writers
now lead with the bare pid.

### Removed

- Withdraw the `design_requests` capability and `design-document-v1` coordinator
  overrides introduced in v0.37.1/v0.37.2. Existing design agents already provide
  their configured design workflow; ordinary requests now use that configuration
  unchanged. The published release entries below remain a historical record.

## [v0.37.2] - 2026-09-10

### Fixed

- **Cloud design-only dispatch:** resolve the agent from the registry before
  applying the per-task scope, and reuse that scoped configuration for the worker
  parameters. v0.37.1 accidentally skipped directive construction and retained
  ordinary timeout/direct-push/package settings. The live test caught this before
  production promotion; v0.37.1 is marked prerelease. A regression test now exercises
  the actual cloud dispatch method, including an ordinary-request positive control.

## [v0.37.1] - 2026-09-10

### Design-only requests reuse existing project inboxes

Daneel can request documents through existing project/package agents instead of
maintaining six copied design agents. An inbox opts in with `design_requests: true`;
a `request` message with JSON `workflow: "design-document-v1"` invokes the
repository's existing `design-doc-creator` skill (shared installed skill if absent).
The task preserves its configured repository, provider/model and permission tier.
It uses a task branch, disables package publication and inherited sessions, and
finishes without an automatic or approval-released implementation handoff.
Ordinary maintenance requests keep their previous behaviour. Inbox discovery exposes
the capability for clients to refuse unsupported routes.

Tests cover ordinary handoffs as positive controls, design completion, replay and
later approval, native skill selection, and shared registry non-mutation.
See [design and verification](../design_docs/planned/m-coordinator-design-only-requests.md).
The executor's Markdown-only scope remains a prompt restriction, not an OS sandbox.

Also includes the already-committed config-write rollout fix: a successful canonical
coordinator config update now rolls the service so writing configuration actually
activates it, rather than leaving the old in-memory registry running.

## [v0.37.0] - 2026-09-10

### A send to an unknown inbox now answers back, and `messages inboxes` says what each name does (2026-09-10)

Fifteen messages over 2026-09-08..10 — daneel's AILANG asks to `ailang-core`,
clients-planner's std/regex and std/datetime reports to `pkg:sunholo/ailang`, one
to `pkg:sunholo/email_parse` (one suffix from the real `pkg:sunholo/email`) — were
accepted, stored, and never dispatched. Zero Cloud Run job executions on 09-09 and
09-10 while the plane itself was perfectly healthy: every one of those messages
reached the coordinator within milliseconds and was skipped with a log line.

It is a **discovery** problem, not a routing one. Nothing told a sender which inbox
names exist or what sending to one does.

- **The coordinator now bounces.** An undispatched message produces a notice in the
  SENDER's inbox saying it was filed but not dispatched, which name failed, the
  message id so it can be resent, and a near-miss suggestion. It lives in the
  coordinator, not `messages send`, because only the coordinator holds the
  authoritative registry — a laptop's config has 2 agents against the cloud's 35,
  so a send-side check would refuse correct sends and pass wrong ones.
- **The near-miss rule is deliberately asymmetric.** `pkg:sunholo/email_parse` →
  `pkg:sunholo/email` (a registered name with a suffix appended) is offered;
  `pkg:sunholo/ailang` → `pkg:sunholo/ailang_parse` is **not**, because that is a
  different product and the suggestion would route a language report to the
  document parser. Both directions are pinned by tests.
- **Loop safety.** A component that answers messages with messages earned scrutiny
  after 2026-08-31 (591 notifications, 59 job executions, 96 minutes). A bounce is
  never sent for a bounce, never for a declared triage inbox, never without a
  sender to answer, and at most once per message — verified against both delivery
  paths (`PubSubInboxAdapter.ListUnread` drains its buffer; the backstop sweep
  already refuses unrouted inboxes).
- **`ailang messages inboxes`** answers "what does sending here DO?" in three
  verdicts — `DISPATCHES` (creates a task for a named agent), `TRIAGE` (filed for a
  human, on purpose), `NOTHING` (accepted and silently dropped) — with recent and
  unread counts per inbox. It **resolves the registry that matches the store**
  rather than assuming this machine's: judged locally against the prod store, all
  sixteen correctly-routed inboxes render as `NOTHING`, which is the exact
  misdirection the command exists to prevent.

First run against prod found **21 inboxes that accept messages and do nothing**,
including `docparse` with **1,146 messages in 14 days** and `controlplane` with 263.

### Fixed — semantic message search over the canonical store matched nothing, and said so as "No messages found"

`ailang messages search`, `dedupe`, `triage` and `--similar-to` returned zero results against the
prod Firestore store no matter what was asked of them. Four independent faults stacked, each one
silent:

- **The write path never built the index.** The SQLite backend computes a message's simhash at
  insert; the Firestore backend wrote `simhash: nil` and computed nothing. Both `SemanticSearch`
  and `FindDuplicates` then `continue`d past every document without one — a full scan that
  skipped 100% of the corpus and reported an empty result.
- **The two backends used different, incompatible hash functions.** Firestore carried a private
  `simhashText` that XOR-folded runes into eight shift positions. It is not a simhash and is not
  comparable with `builtins.SimHash`, so even a populated index would have scored noise. Both
  paths now go through `messaging.ComputeSimhash`, which is the only one either may use.
- **The repo→inbox alias pointed at an inbox that does not exist.** `inferInbox` mapped repo
  `ailang` to `ailang_core`, the v0.6.0 spelling, long after the store standardised on
  `ailang-core`. Every bare search from this repo filtered on an empty inbox.
- **The explainability footer hardcoded `backend=SQLite`,** and neither `search` nor `dedupe`
  printed the store header that `list` prints — so a search of prod claimed to be a search of
  local SQLite, in the one place a reader checks that they are not reading a stale local
  graveyard.

Historical documents are hashed on read, so the whole existing corpus became searchable without a
migration; new messages are indexed at write. Search, dedupe and triage now announce their store
and report the backend they actually scanned.

## [v0.36.0] - 2026-09-09

Two themes, both from field reports.

**Contracts reach the code that needed them.** String interpolation desugared to `show` before
any type was known, which put every string-building function outside the Z3-decidable fragment —
so the archetypal "pure contract-bearing core" (an RFC 5322 composer with a CR/LF injection
guard) could not be proved. It verifies now, verbatim, with interpolation.

**Observability stops costing confidentiality, and stops costing gigabytes.** The tracing tier
was resolved, printed, and then never passed to the collector, so the *default* tier recorded
every function's arguments and results: 2059 MB of peak RSS on a 400-iteration loop, and the
widest value-retention surface in the runtime. The tier is enforced now (105 MB), `deep` is
bounded, and `AILANG_TRACE_VALUES=off` gives the complete call tree with no payloads — for
workloads under confidentiality terms that previously had to choose between an audit trail and
their contract.

Along the way: an IFC label could be laundered past a `{not secret}` sink through a closure, and
every compile of an IFC-labelled module was silently missing the cache.

### Fixed — interpolated strings can be Z3-verified; `show` no longer blocks the whole class

`M-SMT-INTERP-SHOW` ([design doc](../design_docs/implemented/v0_36_0/m-smt-interp-show.md)),
reported from the field as `fb_913ee851c83c0d8c`.

`ailang verify` skipped **any** function that built a string with `"${...}"`, reporting
`uses an unencodable builtin: show` for a function whose source contains no `show` call. Since
`++` is list-only, interpolation is *the* way to build a string, so in practice no
string-building function could carry a verified contract — and string construction is exactly
where the injection classes live (header injection, SQL, prompt). The reporter hit it writing an
RFC 5322 composer: the archetypal "pure contract-bearing core, thin effectful shell" the
language's own guidance asks for, and it skipped.

The verifier was never missing a theory. `concat_String` already encodes to `str.++`, and the
same function written with explicit `concat_String` calls verified all along. The parser
desugars each hole to `show(<expr>)` at **parse** time, before any type is known, so the wrapper
landed even on holes that were already `string` — where `show` is the identity.

- **New `ShowNormalizer` pass** (`internal/pipeline/show_normalize.go`) runs after
  monomorphization, where `CoreTypeInfo` has resolved a generic helper's instantiations, and
  rewrites by argument type into Core the SMT encoder already accepts:
  `string` → the argument itself; `bool` → `if x then "true" else "false"`; `int` →
  `$builtin._string_intToStr(x)`. Float, list, record, ADT and type-variable holes are left
  alone. Two of the three needed **no verifier change at all**.
- It is a **shared-pipeline** pass, not a verify-local one. A verify-local rewrite would have the
  verifier prove properties of a Core tree the evaluator never executes; this way "what was
  proved" and "what runs" are the same tree, and the SMT layer stays untyped. Interpolation also
  costs one fewer builtin call at runtime.
- **The `int` encoding is not `(str.from_int n)`.** Z3's `str.from_int` is defined only for
  non-negative arguments and returns `""` for a negative one, while AILANG renders `-5` as
  `"-5"` — the naive form would have silently proved that every negative int renders as the
  empty string. The emitted form branches on the sign, and its exactness is asserted as `unsat`
  on the *negation* of `showInt(n) = strconv.Itoa(n)` over sampled inputs plus the symbolic
  property that no int renders empty. A separate test records the `str.from_int` premise itself,
  so a solver change reports rather than hides.
- **A missing `CoreTypeInfo` entry is a hard error**, not a degraded skip. `ValidateCoreTypeInfo`
  already calls such a miss a compiler bug; absorbing it would resurface as an ordinary
  encodability skip and mask the real fault. A type that is present but unsupported is the
  different, legitimate case.
- **The residual skip now names the type.** `Function "fmtPrice" applies show to a float; Z3 has
  no string encoding for that type. Encodable show arguments: string, bool, int.` The type comes
  from a note the pass records in `DeclMeta.ShowResidue` — measured, not inferred. It
  deliberately does **not** claim the call came from an interpolation: the identical Core shape
  is produced by an explicit `show(x)` call, so that claim would misdiagnose it. The
  interpolation fact is in the hint, as advice true either way.
- `show` skips across `examples/runnable/contracts/` went **2 → 0**: `safeConcat` and
  `prefixedLength` — the language's own contract showcase could not prove its own string
  examples.
- Corrects the comment in `internal/parser/parser_literals.go` claiming `show` dispatches to
  `show_Int`/`show_String` at elaboration time. It does not: `show` is one polymorphic `$builtin`,
  and `show_Int` and friends exist only in the frozen interface table. That comment is probably
  why this went unexamined.
- `ailang fmt` is unaffected — it parses only and never reaches Core. Effect rows are unaffected —
  `show` is `IsPure`, and issue #386's accept *and* must-reject controls both still hold.

### Added — `AILANG_TRACE_VALUES=off`: the full call tree, none of the content

For workloads under confidentiality terms — reading a mailbox and client documents on a machine
whose disk is not encrypted — the choice was observability *or* confidentiality
(`fb_5c0baeb1ee5ae1eb`). The tier fix above closed per-call function values at `standard`, but
**effect** args and results are recorded at every tier above `off`, so a `readFile` still shipped
what it read.

`AILANG_TRACE_VALUES=off` is orthogonal to the tier, which makes the useful combination
expressible: `AILANG_TRACE=deep AILANG_TRACE_VALUES=off` retains every function, every effect,
arity, depth, span parentage and timings, with each value replaced by a size descriptor.

Measured on a program handling a bearer token and a client document: **17 trace lines with values
on, 17 with them off — 8 verbatim copies versus 0.** Structure is untouched; only content goes.

- Applied at the collector, not the call sites, so a recorder added later cannot bypass it.
- Byte counts are retained deliberately: they leak no content, and an empty response or a body
  that grew between retries stays visible.
- A misspelling (`=of`) is an **error**, not a silent default — a typo must not become a leak.
- Deliberately blunt. Label-aware redaction (`M-TRACE-LABEL-AWARE`) is more precise, but its
  guarantee is "no values the label checker flagged", worth exactly what the checker is worth.
  This one's guarantee is "no values", which is what a confidentiality term needs.

Also fixes `boundedShow`, which took `maxDepth`/`maxWidth` and ignored both while its caller in
the typed evaluator's trace path relied on it to keep values small.

### Added — bounds on `deep` tracing

`deep` is meant to have everything, so the bounds cost as little of that as possible: a per-value
cap (1 KB) that keeps every event and elides only pathological values — the change that makes an
accumulator recursion O(n) instead of O(n²) — and a 256 MB retention cap that evicts the
**oldest** events, since the tail is what a debugger needs. `module_start` is pinned (it carries
the granted capability set), and eviction inserts a synthetic `trace_truncated` event so a
truncated trace cannot be mistaken for a complete one that started late. Observers are notified
*before* retention applies, so a streaming exporter still receives the complete stream.

Measured, `deep`: n=400 2478 → 1133 MB; n=800 4934 → 2061 MB. This bounds *retained* memory, not
peak — rendering still materialises a value before the cap truncates it, so a deep trace of a
large accumulator still allocates and discards. An early-terminating renderer is the next step.

### Fixed (security) — a closure could launder an IFC label past a `{not secret}` sink

Found while spiking `M-TRACE-LABEL-AWARE`'s dependency on label propagation. The
`{not secret}` guarantee was bypassable in three tokens:

```ailang
export func sink(body: string{not secret}) -> string ! {} = body
export func attempt(s: string<secret>) -> string ! {} =
  let f = \u. s in sink(f(0))     -- accepted; the label was gone
```

Two independent gaps had to line up, which is why it survived:

- `ifc_check.go`'s `Lambda` case returned `LabelBottom()` — "a closure value carries no label".
  True of the function value; but the label of what it *returns* was then lost.
- `labelOfCall`'s fallback for a callee with no declared signature — the path **every local
  closure takes**, since a `let`-bound `f` is not in `c.sigs` — joined only the *argument*
  labels, discarding the callee's own.

Fixing either alone leaves the hole open. Both now propagate, and a closure is labelled with
what its body returns. That over-approximates — a closure whose result is never used is still
labelled — which for a security control is the correct direction to err: over-approximation
rejects safe programs loudly, under-approximation admits leaks silently. No false rejections
appeared across the example corpus or the test suite.

Nine paths are pinned by `TestIFCClosureCannotLaunderALabel`, including the direct call as a
firing control, so a fixture that stops biting cannot quietly turn the other rows green.

**Note for anyone iterating on the checker:** the compile cache key includes the compiler
version, which is identical across two builds of the same dirty tree — so re-running through the
CLI after a rebuild can grade a *stale binary*. This defect appeared to survive its own fix for
exactly that reason. Clear `.ailang/cache`, or work through the unit tests.

### Fixed — the tracing tier is now enforced; the default tier no longer records every value

`M-TRACE-TIER-NOT-ENFORCED` ([design doc](../design_docs/planned/v0_36_0/m-trace-tier-not-enforced.md)),
from `inbox_1788936332313_c08c55b5` (an OOM) and `fb_b023726953f2ee5a` (the exposure axis).

`AILANG_TRACE` / `--trace-tier` selects `off | standard | deep`. The tier was resolved, used to
decide whether a collector existed and which banner to print, and then **never passed to the
collector**, which had no tier field at all. `standard` and `deep` recorded identically, against
`options.go`'s own documentation ("NOT per-call function spans").

Because every call recorded its arguments and result as strings, a function carrying an
accumulator serialised O(n) data on each of n calls — O(n²) trace bytes, held in memory:

| n (accumulator loop) | before | after | `off` |
|---|---|---|---|
| 400 | 2059 MB | **105 MB** | 102 MB |
| 800 | 3463 MB | **155 MB** | 153 MB |

**20× at n=400, and now within 3% of tracing-off.** `deep` is unchanged (2478 MB at n=400) —
capturing values is its purpose.

- The collector carries a tier; `RecordFunctionEnter`/`Exit` are gated on it.
- The **rendering** is gated too, at `eval_operations.go`. Gating only the recording would have
  fixed the retention and left the memory cost exactly where it was — `a.String()` per argument
  per call is where it is actually paid.
- `NewCollector()` keeps recording everything, so the 32 existing test callers are unchanged;
  the CLI passes a tier explicitly. Only 2 non-test construction sites existed.
- **Security-relevant:** at `standard`, an IFC-labelled value crossing a traced call boundary is
  no longer written to the trace. Measured with a `string<secret>` canary: 4 verbatim copies
  before, **0** after. At `deep` it is still 4 — that is what `M-TRACE-LABEL-AWARE` addresses.
- **Not fixed here:** effect args and results are still recorded at `standard`, so a `readFile`
  span still carries what was read. That is `fb_b0237`'s measured surface and is M2 of
  `M-TRACE-LABEL-AWARE`.
- `TestTierGovernsWhatIsRecorded` pins the per-tier matrix so prose and behaviour cannot drift
  apart again — the absence of exactly that test is why this survived since v0.12.0.

### Fixed — `TLabelled` survives the compile cache

Every compile of an IFC-labelled module failed its cache write with
`gob: type not registered for interface: types.TLabelled` and silently fell back to fresh
compilation (`inbox_1788847828665_f0c5db58`) — a permanently cold cache on exactly the modules
the taint story is for. Not the one-line `gob.Register` it appears to be: the four `Label`
implementations are unexported value types with unexported fields, which gob refuses both ways,
so registering them would have found the type and then serialised an *empty label*. The codec
delegates to the tagged-union JSON codec that already exists for ifaces, so the two cannot drift.

### Fixed — the interpolation desugar is now hygienic, and `ailang fmt` no longer rewrites hand-written `show` calls

Shipped alongside the above, and the reason the `export func show` shadowing bug
(`inbox_1788928007403_88e5056a`) is now safe to fix independently.

The desugar synthesized a bare `show` identifier subject to ordinary name resolution, and
`globalEnv` is consulted before local bindings — which is the *only* reason a module's own
`show` did not already take over every `"${x}"` hole. Fixing the shadowing bug the natural way
would have flipped that, silently: with a type-compatible local
`export func show(x: string) -> string`, `"${s}"` would return the user's value **and** the
enclosing function would drop from VERIFIED back to SKIPPED.

- **`ast.Identifier.ResolveAsBuiltin`**, set only by `parseInterpolatedString`. The elaborator
  resolves such an identifier straight to `$builtin.<Name>`, bypassing constructor lookup,
  `globalEnv` and local resolution alike — so nothing a program declares can capture it. An
  unregistered name there is a hard error: a desugar referencing a builtin that does not exist
  is a compiler bug, and a plausible-looking reference would surface much later as an
  unresolved global with nothing pointing back.
- **Behavior change in `ailang fmt`:** a hand-written `concat_String("a", show(n))` is now left
  as written, where it was previously reprinted as `"a${n}"`. The formatter re-sugared by
  pattern-matching the `show(x)` *shape*, which is indistinguishable from a desugared chain —
  a meaning-preserving rewrite only for as long as `show` cannot be shadowed. A formatter must
  never be the thing that changes which function a program calls. Real interpolations
  round-trip exactly as before; `fmt`'s round-trip is now exact rather than heuristic.

New: `examples/runnable/contracts/interpolation_verify.ail`.

## [v0.35.4] - 2026-09-08

### Added — `std/bytes.toBase64URL`, closing an encode/decode asymmetry that failed silently

`M-STD-BASE64URL-ENCODE` ([design doc](../design_docs/implemented/v0_35_4/m-std-base64url-encode.md)),
reported from the field as `fb_dfb699d91224be9c` while building a `sunholo/gmail` package.

`std/bytes` exported `fromBase64URL` and no encoder, so base64url — the wire encoding the Gmail
API requires for a message's `raw` field, and the one JWT header/payload segments use — had no
stdlib primitive. The workaround (`toBase64` then `replaceMany(s, [("+","-"),("/","_")])` and strip
padding) type-checks, runs and exits 0 while producing a plausible-looking base64 string when it is
wrong; the failure surfaces as a 400 from a third-party API. `toBase64(fromString("a+b/c?"))` is
`YStiL2M/` — the `/` is the reported failure mode.

- **`toBase64URL(b: bytes) -> string`** — RFC 4648 §5, URL-safe alphabet, no padding. Total
  function, no `Option` to unwrap. Exact inverse of `fromBase64URL`: both are
  `base64.RawURLEncoding`, so `fromBase64URL(toBase64URL(b)) == Some(b)` holds for every input.
- Pinned to **unpadded** deliberately. `fromBase64URL` rejects padded input, so a padded encoder
  would emit strings its own sibling decoder returns `None` for.
- The full `std/bytes` surface was audited for other asymmetries first; base64url was the only one.
  `fromString`/`toString`, `fromInts`/`toInts` and `toBase64`/`fromBase64` are all paired. Hex is a
  missing *pair* rather than an asymmetry — different class, no consumer, out of scope.
- A symmetry regression test now covers all 256 byte values, all three length-mod-3 padding
  residues, and asserts the encoder never emits `+`, `/` or `=` — so the next codec added to
  `std/bytes` cannot land half-finished the way `toInts` did for 2.5 months.
- Not wired into `compiler.BuiltinTable`/`vm.BuiltinTable`: those are index-matched, and no
  `_bytes_*` builtin is in either (the VM has no `TagBytes` representation). Adding one to a single
  table shifts dispatch for every later builtin.
- Taught in the active prompt `v0.16.6`, amended in place. A new `v0.16.7` was cut first and
  reverted: `v0.16.6` carries no banked corpus evidence, so no eval baseline is pinned to it and
  `ailang prompt freeze v0.16.6` correctly refuses. The registry admits exactly one *mutable* head
  and `v0.16.6` is it — `TestRealRegistry_PostMigrationSplitCounts` is the guard, and it caught the
  second head. Frozen versions (`v0.16.5` and earlier) are untouched.

New: `examples/runnable/bytes_base64url.ail`.

## [v0.35.3] - 2026-09-08

### Dashboard cloud read contract

- Honor chain/span offsets with deterministic timestamp ties; apply agent filters
  before chain paging and propagate membership query failures.
- Add validated chain CLI paging/workspace/repository filters and retained HTTP
  filter validation. Stage evidence reads verify chain ownership and distinguish
  missing records, denied access and unavailable/index-dependent queries.
- Align empty local/cloud page arrays; explicitly reject unsupported Firestore
  span workspace filters. Document required indexes and remaining rollout gaps in
  `docs/internal/dashboard-cloud-read-indexes.md`; production is not yet certified.

### Dashboard simplification — remove optional analytics and evolution visualization

- Delete the activity/analytics panel and evolution-tree mode with exclusive hooks,
  styles and chart dependencies. Keep chains, timeline/chat inspection and approvals.
- Replace heatmap-only date selection with direct From/To inputs; align queue and
  hierarchy filtering to complete UTC days, including the final millisecond.
- Remove `recharts`, `d3-hierarchy` and its type package; refresh checked-in UI and
  embedded server assets. This is a UI reduction, not a production data-stability fix.


### The second Go implementation is gone — AILANG is the only one (2026-09-08)

Mark's instruction, after the approval-authority work turned up the pattern. Every
AILANG transform in the dashboard and the budget CLI kept a **parallel Go copy of
the same rule** and silently fell back to it on any error. Removed.

**What the fallback was hiding, found the moment it came out:** `ailang budget
status` failed with `missing dictionary method: prelude::Fractional::Int::add`.
`Engine.Call` folds a whole `float64` into an `IntValue` for JSON compatibility, so
a `$100` budget reached `checkTaskBudget`'s `float` parameters as an `int`. Budgets
are whole numbers in every realistic config, so **the AILANG budget rule had never
successfully run** — the Go copy answered every time, and nothing said so because the
error was discarded. Fixed with `CallPreserveFloats`, with a regression test that
also asserts the negative arm.

Worse, the deployed dashboard image shipped **the binary and no `.ail` files at
all**, and did not set `AILANG_DASHBOARD=1` — so in production the "fallback" was
the only thing that had ever executed.

- **`internal/server/ailang_bridge.go`** — all seven methods now return `(T, error)`.
  Deleted `goTruncate`, `goCheckTaskBudget`, `goCalculateBurnRate`,
  `goForecastExhaustion`, and `handlers_controlplane_heatmap.go`'s `buildHeatmapGrid`.
  `IsEnabled()` (is the feature on?) becomes `Ready() error` (is the only
  implementation reachable, and if not, why not?). `AILANG_DASHBOARD` is retired: with
  one implementation there is nothing to switch.
- **Call sites handle it.** `/budget/status` and `/budget/check` return **503** rather
  than a guessed verdict — they gate spending, which CLAUDE.md Principle 2 puts
  outside what may be quietly defaulted. Summary, turn-count and heatmap likewise.
  Burn rate and forecast are display fields on a larger response, so they are left
  **unset** and logged — an absent number is honest, an invented one is not.
- **`cmd/ailang/budget.go`** — the worst case: it swallowed the error with no log line
  at all, and built its engine with `ailembed.New(".")`, so the rule that answered
  depended on the caller's working directory. Now one code path, and it exits non-zero
  when the rule cannot run.
- **`internal/embed/root.go`** — `ProjectRoot` / `NewForModule`, shared by the bridge,
  the budget CLI and the approval authority. `AILANG_PROJECT_ROOT` is **verified, not
  trusted**; otherwise it walks up from the working directory. A root that does not
  contain the module is an error.
- **`docker/Dockerfile.dashboard`** ships `internal/dashboard_transforms/` and `std/`
  (4.6 MB) and sets `AILANG_PROJECT_ROOT=/app`. Verified by reproducing that layout
  locally and running the budget rule from a foreign working directory.

**Still duplicated, deliberately left:** `coordinator.SummarizeEvents` /
`CountTurns` re-implement `event_formatter.ail` and have a real non-fallback caller
(`cmd/ailang/coordinator_browse.go`). They already differ — Go counts `len(text)`
bytes, AILANG counts `strlen` runes. Converging them would put an AILANG runtime
dependency inside the coordinator daemon's container, which is a separate decision.

### The session banner leads with pending APPROVALS, not unread counts (2026-09-08)

The message plane's binding constraint moved and the banner did not follow it.
Delivery, execution and the handoff all work now — 26 of 27 cloud job executions
succeeded across 09-07/09-08, and the `design-doc-creator → sprint-planner →
sprint-executor` chain fired end to end for the first time (`6c0bf448d`,
`9bb62a84d`, `83cece4ff`, live as coordinator revision `00077`). So what stalls
the loop is no longer a lost message; it is a finished branch waiting on a
decision nobody was told about. Six approvals were pending in prod when this
landed, the oldest 8h, plus 8 orphaned rows — and the banner said "20 unread".

- **`ailang coordinator approvals --json`** — the queue as a document: age,
  what approving DISPATCHES (handoff targets), whether a diff is actually
  reviewable, the evaluator verdict, and the orphan count that the pending list
  structurally cannot show. Errors exit non-zero rather than printing `[]`.
- **`scripts/hooks/session_start.sh`** now opens with that queue, oldest first,
  above the (demoted, still ambient) unread line. An unreachable queue prints
  `UNREADABLE`, never `0` — "none pending" and "I could not ask" read identically
  and are opposite facts, which is the same shape as the 2026-08-26 banner that
  reported 16 local unread while prod held 74.
- **Authority is identity, and the decision is written in AILANG.**
  `internal/dashboard_transforms/approval_authority.ail` — the operator's rule
  (Mark, attended 2026-09-08): the default is none, and controller sessions,
  plus mission loops on a trusted rung (fable/astra/opus), may resolve
  unattended. The grant reads `MISSION_CONTROL_ACTIVE` / `CONTROLLER_ID`, which
  the launchd driver exports only after walking its controller ladder — so a
  controller demoted to a dry-out rung (codex, glm) loses the grant by itself.
  Two rules are not preferences: a **spawned role never decides** (generator is
  not judge), and a row with **no visible diff** is nobody's to approve
  unattended. `AILANG_APPROVAL_POLICY=always` is the deliberate override;
  `AILANG_APPROVAL_CONTROLLER=1` is the standing grant for an attended session.
  `--by` now records the resolved identity, so a 3am fable controller is no
  longer indistinguishable in the audit trail from the operator sharing its login.

  It is AILANG because it is exactly the shape `budget_checker.ail` already
  occupies — a pure function from declared config plus observed facts to a
  verdict with a reason — and Axiom A4 is "Explicit Authority". It carries 21
  inline `tests [...]` that run under `ailang test` alone; editing the `.ail`
  turns the Go suite red, which is how the wiring is verified. Cost: 65ms to
  load once, then 13µs per call, against a ~1.1s Firestore round trip.

  **One deliberate divergence from the `budget_checker` precedent:** that bridge
  keeps a second Go implementation and silently falls back to it on any error.
  For an authority rule that would be a defect — a failed module load would hand
  "who may merge" to an unreviewed second copy. There is no second copy here; a
  decision that cannot be evaluated **refuses**, which is also the safe
  direction. Verified: run the CLI outside the tree and the grant is withheld
  with the reason attached.

  It remains advice, not enforcement — an agent and a human drive the identical
  CLI against the identical store — and the code says so rather than implying a
  boundary that is not there.

### Windows: the mission runtime was never ported, and dev had been red for it (2026-09-08)

`test-windows` had been failing since `48e72ef7e` on 15 tests. Four distinct causes, not one:

- **SQLite `file:` URI built from an OS path** — a real product bug, not a test artifact.
  `url.URL{Scheme: "file", Path: "C:\\Users\\..."}` renders `file:C:%5CUsers%5C...`, and
  SQLite parses the drive letter as the URI authority (`invalid uri authority`). Both DSN
  sites now go through `sqliteFileURI`, which takes the OS explicitly so the Windows spelling
  is verifiable from macOS — `filepath.ToSlash` switches on the *runtime* separator, so it
  would have made the Windows behaviour untestable on the machine that runs this fleet. The
  unix spelling is asserted byte-identical to before.
- **Directory fsync** — three hand-rolled copies, each returning `Access is denied` on
  Windows, which has no fsync-on-directory. One build-tagged `internal/fsyncdir` helper; the
  non-unix build documents the guarantee it genuinely cannot make instead of pretending.
- **An un-tagged POSIX permission check** in `cmd/ailang/mission_activation.go` rejected
  every directory it had just created (`MkdirAll(dir, 0700)` does not yield 0700 there), so
  activation failed with a permissions complaint *before* reaching `lock_other.go`, the one
  place that is supposed to decline. Now `activation.EnsurePrivateDir`, build-tagged, keeping
  the portable symlink/is-a-directory guarantees and leaving the refusal to the lock.
- **Fixtures hardcoding `/tmp/...`**, which `filepath.IsAbs` rejects on Windows, so the
  binding validator refused every fixture for a reason unrelated to what the tests assert.

Tests that drive activation end-to-end now skip on `activation.HostSupported()` — the
package's own declaration — rather than re-deriving a GOOS list that would drift from it.

### Daily rations now cover every provider the loops spend on (2026-09-08)

The ration exists so unattended mission loops leave headroom for attended sessions. Two of
the three buckets the fleet actually routes to were not paced by it at all.

- **Anthropic is read, not guessed.** `GET /api/oauth/usage` — the endpoint behind Claude
  Code's own usage display — returns utilisation AND `resets_at` for the 5-hour and 7-day
  windows, so the existing percentage pacing applies unchanged with no capacity inferred.
  The bucket previously reported `capacity-unknown — UNRATIONED` while the ledger counted
  1.38M tokens against nothing. Only unscoped windows ration: a `weekly_scoped` entry caps
  one model (Fable, 25% on 2026-09-08) and would otherwise block every lane.
- **Rationed by default** (Mark, attended 2026-09-08); `AILANG_ANTHROPIC_RATION=0` opts one
  process out and still reports the number. An over-ration controller walks
  `CONTROLLER_FALLBACK` to a cheaper rung rather than wedging, and when nothing is left the
  existing "NO usable controller" refusal is the pause D-4 asks for. Measured at landing:
  34% used, 11.2% allowed.
- The Anthropic ration was also only half-walked: `_mc_probe` had no admission gate, so the
  controller yielded while the designer and evaluator kept spending. Now symmetric with the
  Codex and Pi probes, and `rc=75` reports as `over daily ration` rather than `unusable`.
- **Ollama is paced on rate, because it cannot be paced on position.** Its gauge returns
  session/weekly fractions and no reset (verified against the live response), so there is no
  window position to compute an allowance from — which is why it was the fleet's only
  unrationed bucket. It is now paced on percentage points consumed in the trailing 24h
  against the same 10%/day rule, needing neither capacity nor reset. Consumption sums
  positive deltas so a window rollover cannot license a fresh burst.
- Measured motivation: the weekly gauge went 36.1% → 43.1% → 69.4% between 2026-09-07 17:02
  and 2026-09-08 09:06 — ~2.1pp/h against a 0.42pp/h ration, with nothing to stop it before
  the 95% cutoff.
- Thin history is UNPACED and loud rather than blocking, and neither ration can launder a
  critical gauge or an unknown quota into `ok`.

### Mission communications integration (2026-09-08)

- Add `mission report` alongside iteration/recovery commands, with a network-free dry-run, resolved bookkeeping issue and marked UTF-8-safe report cap.
- Bound GitHub client shell-outs and retain typed timeout errors; the live shell-driver cutover remains separate.
- Add coordinator regression coverage for registry load failures and missing agent entries.

### Testing — the coordinator daemon's task-execution path is executed by a test for the first time (M-DAEMON-TASK-EXEC-RUN-UNTESTED, 2026-09-08)

`Daemon.executeTask` — the function that runs every coordinator task — had no test that ran
it. Five `_test.go` files named it and none executed it: two walk it as AST source, the rest
test the different function `executeTaskQueue`. That is why six of the seven lines SonarCloud
flagged as uncovered on [#1111](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/pull/1111) sat in this
one file, and why that PR's rebase of the production caller onto `DefaultExecuteOptions()` was
verified by code reading with nothing executing it.

- `Daemon.executor` is now the narrow one-method `taskExecutor` interface instead of the
  concrete `*TaskExecutor`. Of the five production references to that field only the
  declaration changes; `*TaskExecutor` satisfies the interface, so no call site, nil check or
  assignment moves and no production default or code path changes in effect.
- `TestExecuteTask_HandsDefaultBasedOptionsToExecutor` executes `executeTask` against an
  in-memory fixture and pins what actually reaches `ExecuteWithRetry`: the three **base**
  fields that prove `DefaultExecuteOptions()` is the base, the five overrides, the retry
  count, and the directive payload on the `AnalyzedTask`.
- The test is hermetic by construction — it pins `AILANG_CONFIG` at a non-existent path inside
  `t.TempDir()`, so the budget gate reads the compiled-in defaults rather than the developer's
  `~/.ailang/config.yaml`. Proven load-bearing by a two-run experiment that holds spend and a
  hostile config constant and varies only the pin.

### Mission iteration reliability (2026-09-08)

- Bind evaluator requests to a bounded immutable evidence packet and expose redacted tool progress, including incomplete receipt diagnostics.
- Prepare content-bound evaluator-only successors with `mission retry-review`, retaining author provenance and requiring approval for imported artifacts.
- Add typed recovery guidance and versioned `mission confirm-stopped` attestations for supported cancellation/deadline states.
- Supervise local Docs canaries through `mission activation run|inspect|recover`, with durable ownership, frozen child input, verified cleanup and retained read-only status after restoration.
- Live adoption remains gated on the bounded smoke series; no automatic rollout or merge.

### Durable mission iteration (2026-09-07)

- Canary repair: honor the documented `AILANG_MISSION_REGISTRY` absolute directory override; reject invalid explicit paths instead of silently selecting the checkout-local fleet registry.

- Added `mission iterate`, `status`, `resume`, and versioned `cancel` for one frozen Git work item with durable parent/child ownership, quota admission, crash reconciliation, and immutable stage acceptance.
- Validate committed artifacts, path scope, imported authority, independent evaluation, and bounded verification commands before advancing; execution completion alone cannot accept work.
- Share Unix process-tree cancellation across Claude, Codex, and Pi; the explicit driver opt-in preserves foreign repository placement and invokes the binary once.
- Provide read-only planning/status and an activation packet. Production scheduling and live canary activation remain separate from implementation validation.

### Fixed — a `pure func` no longer exports an effect-polymorphic row (M-EFFECT-PURE-ROW-OVERGENERALIZATION, [#1091](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/1091))

Moving a `pure` helper into a sibling module could make a *caller* fail effect checking
with an effect that appears nowhere in the program:

```
Error: effect checking failed in docparse/services/docx_template:
  Effect checking failed for function 'docxTplCleanForSectPr'
  Missing effects: FS
```

**Cause.** A recursive self-call deliberately shares — and does not bind — the enclosing
function's effect-row variable (`inferApp`). With a **concrete** declared row (`! {IO}`) the
recursive call unifies against it and the row closes on its own; with an **empty** declared
row there is nothing to close against, so the variable survives. `generalizeWithConstraints`
then quantifies every free effect-row variable of the type (correct in general — it is what
gives separate imported uses independent rows, #386) without consulting the declaration. The
module exported `(string, string) -> int ! {...ρ2}` with `RowVars=[ρ2]`; each importer
instantiated that row fresh and it absorbed whatever effect surrounded the call site.

The trigger was invisible to the author — the body simply had to call a recursive function —
and the suggested fix (`... -> T ! {FS}`) told them to declare an effect the function does
not have, propagating the falsehood through every transitive caller.

**Fix.** Before generalization, an unresolved effect row on a binding whose declaration
carries no row variable is defaulted to the declared row. Only the open *tail variable* is
bound; concrete labels are never removed, so a genuine effect still reaches the effect
checker and a `pure` caller of a genuinely effectful import is still rejected.

The rule applies only when the row variable occurs **nowhere else in the type**. A shared
variable is load-bearing even with no `! {e}` annotation: `std/list.flatMap` declares no
effects at all, yet its callback and result rows share an inferred row, and that sharing is
what lets callers pass an effectful lambda. An earlier, broader version of this fix closed
shared rows too and broke exactly that (`epub_parser`'s
`flatMap(\entry. epubParseContentFile(...))`); the case is now pinned by a test.

Not a soundness hole: the over-generalization only ever fired on an empty declared row, so it
rejected valid programs rather than admitting invalid ones.

**Also corrected**: the issue reported that naming the dependency on the command line made the
error vanish (`ailang check pkg_template.ail docx_template.ail`). It does not — `ailang check`
ignores every argument after the first, so only the dependency was being checked. Tracked
separately.

### Changed — `internal/coordinator` stops sleeping through its own tests (M-COORDINATOR-TEST-PARALLELISM)

Four tests in the slowest package of the test matrix were burning **8.13 s of real sleep** —
63.7% of the package's summed test time — waiting on production timers the tests could not
control: a hardcoded `baseDelay := time.Second` exponential backoff in `ExecuteWithRetry`, a
hardcoded `pollInterval: 2 * time.Second` in `NewStoreBackedApprovalCheckpoint`, and a
hardcoded `time.Second` rate-limit window in `checkRateLimit`. The package ran effectively
serially (wall ~= sum of per-test durations, **zero** `t.Parallel()` across 738 tests).

Three timer seams are now injectable as **per-call config** — `ExecuteOptions.RetryBaseDelay`
plus a cancellable `Wait` seam, `pollInterval` + `tick` constructor parameters on the
store-backed approval checkpoint, and a `WithClock` functional option on
`CoordinatorEventHandler`. **No production default changes** (1 s backoff, 2 s poll, 1 s
window, 10 events/s), each is set explicitly rather than by a zero-value fallback, and
`t.Parallel()` is still absent everywhere — the win comes from not sleeping, not from
concurrency.

Measured on one box with `-count=1`, controller-run outside the executor sandbox:
the four tests **8.13 s -> 0.23 s**, summed package test time **12.65 s -> 4.65 s**,
full-package wall **16 s -> 6 s**.

Five behavioural regression guards observe the injected value's effect on production
behaviour through the seam — never the field and never elapsed wall time — including
`TestExecuteWithRetry_BackoffIsCancellable`, which pins that the production backoff still
returns as soon as its context is cancelled, and `TestProductionTimerDefaultsPreserved`,
which pins the three defaults a grep backstop provably could not (`RetryBaseDelay:.*time.Second`
matches `2 * time.Second`).


### Fixed — the notice-spool drain is bounded as a WHOLE, not just per row (M-LAUNCHD-NOTIFY-SUBSHELL-OBSERVATION M3)

`_mc_drain_notices` runs once in the launchd driver's **preflight** — the one phase of a fire
with no deadline behind it but the 6-hour `HARD_TIMEOUT`. It bounded each row's send at
`NOTIFY_TIMEOUT` and left the loop itself unbounded, while the spool is unbounded by
construction (no size cap, no row cap, no rotation). An N-row spool of hanging sends therefore
cost `N × (NOTIFY_TIMEOUT + overhead)` inside a phase the stall watchdog cannot rescue: the
driver logs that early kill is DISABLED when there is no progress instrument, and a drain making
slow progress is not "no progress" anyway.

`MISSION_DRAIN_BUDGET` (default `90`) now caps the whole drain. Before each row the loop requires
`DRAIN_BUDGET - elapsed > NOTIFY_TIMEOUT + 2`; when it does not, the current row **and every
still-unattempted row** are appended back to the spool unchanged, the drain logs
`notice spool: deferred <k> row(s), aggregate budget <B>s exhausted`, and returns 0. **No notice
is dropped** — an outage costs a delay, not the record — and the pre-existing failed-row
re-append path is untouched.

`bash tools/launchd/test_driver_notify.sh`: 38 passed/0 failed → **40 passed/0 failed**, rc=0,
identical across two runs; `make test-launchd-drivers` rc=0. Independent judge (`sonnet`)
PASS 88/100, zero blocking, with a mutation drill anchored to the diff and non-vacuity proven by
reverting M3's own production hunk alone while keeping M1/M2.

**Known, filed, not fixed here:** the new `deferred` counter over-reports when a malformed
(empty-title) spool row precedes the deferral point, and that row is silently dropped —
pre-existing drop behaviour, newly mis-counted. Tracked as `m-drain-deferred-counter-overcounts`.

### Fixed — `grade_entrypoint` benchmarks no longer run in standard mode (M-EVAL-STANDARD-MODE-INPUT-FILES-GAP)

`markdown_reimplement` and `docx_reimplement` set `grade_entrypoint`, which grades the
agent's preserved multi-file workspace — something standard (0-shot) mode never
constructs. Every pre-fix standard-mode run of these two benchmarks therefore produced a
near-guaranteed `compile_error`/`runtime_error` (e.g. the identical
`undefined variable: emptyParseState` signature across 8/13 models on `markdown_reimplement`
in the v0.32.0 baseline) that measured the harness gap, not model capability.

**Pre-fix standard-mode data for these 2 benchmarks should be treated as unreliable** —
exclude it from frontier-tier difficulty/eligibility reads (per the design doc's Risks
mitigation). Post-fix, they are excluded from standard-mode scheduling entirely, and a
direct standard-mode dispatch banks a labelled skip row
(`error_category: skipped_mode_incompatible`, validity reason `mode_incompatible`) that
every aggregate filters out. Historical result files are not edited; the rows stand as
banked, quarantined only going forward. Run these benchmarks with
`eval-suite --agent --benchmarks markdown_reimplement,docx_reimplement`.

### Fixed — telemetry capture after collector startup outages

- Keep OTLP exporters registered when the collector is unavailable at startup;
  remove the TCP preflight that incorrectly sent HTTPS endpoints without a port
  to port 4318. Later batches can recover through the HTTP SDK.
- Report actual Cloud Trace/OTLP registration with delivery explicitly unverified;
  report Cloud Trace initialization timeout as degraded. Clean up partial and late
  initialization results, and flush coordinator telemetry on normal exit.
- Preserve explicit chain/stage IDs in SQLite’s task-linked span transaction;
  this path previously dropped provenance that the receiver had correctly decoded.
- Verify recovery, bounded failure, HTTPS port selection and task/chain/stage
  provenance through the real OTLP receiver using isolated fixtures. Failed batches
  are not durably replayed; production rollout and live receipt remain separate gates.

## [v0.35.2] - 2026-09-07

### Fixed — the agent handoff chain fired for the first time, after four stacked defects

The configured pipeline (`design-doc-creator → sprint-planner → sprint-executor →
sprint-evaluator`) had **never advanced past its first stage**. Four defects sat on top of
each other, each hidden by the one above, and every one produced the identical symptom:
the approval reported success while nothing downstream happened.

1. **`ProcessApprovalRequest` never fired handoffs.** Only `Daemon.handleApproval` called
   `OnAgentApproved`; the shared path used by the CLI **and the dashboard** read
   `TriggerOnComplete` solely to compose a GitHub comment saying *"Triggering next stage"* —
   a claim nothing acted on. Every pipeline edge is `auto_approve_handoffs: false`, so no
   pipeline edge could ever chain from either.
2. **Handoffs were written to the wrong collection.** `CreateMessage` targets thread
   messages; dispatch polls the **inbox**. The daemon's `deliverHandoffToInbox` always used
   `InsertInboxMessage` — two mechanisms, one of them unread by anything.
3. **The inbox row alone starts nothing.** The cloud coordinator dispatches from a Pub/Sub
   notification, so a correct, visible handoff row was never picked up.
4. **The task status never followed its own approval.** The only status write lived in
   `finalizeApprovedTask`, which merging reaches and the three no-merge early returns do
   not — and `SkipMerge` is true for *every* cloud approval. Each successful hop stranded
   its own stage in `pending_approval`, unactionable by anything.

Measured end to end on 2026-09-07: message in at 20:33, **handoffs at 22s and 20s**, working
code at 20:49 — a 47-line test file that compiles and passes, evaluated `PASS score=90`. Each
stage opened a PR (#1100, #1101, #1103). Handoff topology came from the registry throughout,
never from model output — the property unsupervised chaining depends on.

`errHandoffNotDispatched` now distinguishes "stored but not dispatched" from both success and
a lost handoff. Collapsing those two is what let this hide three times.

### Fixed — task ids derived from deterministic message ids collided by construction

`msgIDSuffix` took the last 8 characters of a message id: unique for
`inbox_<millis>_<8 hex>`, meaningless for the handoff form
`task-<parent>:handoff:<target>`. The first evaluator handoff produced `task-valuator` — as
would **every** evaluator handoff from any parent, forever. Firestore's `Doc().Set` overwrites
silently, so the second would have destroyed the first with no error anywhere. Only reachable
once handoffs worked at all. Now hashed when the tail is not a random hex suffix; redelivery
stays idempotent and the inbox form keeps its existing suffix.

### Fixed — `--type feedback` could not produce a feedback message, and SQLite rejected one

`--type` set `Category` while `MessageType` stayed hardcoded to `notification`, and
`config.cloud.yaml` selects a package agent's prompt template on `MessageType`. A feedback
report sent from the CLI therefore arrived under the **cascade-repair** template: the agent
went to the *consumer* package hunting a dependency breakage that did not exist. Separately,
`feedback` was absent from the SQLite CHECK while Firestore has carried such messages for
weeks — the same split as the five coordinator types, missed then. The migration's CHECK is
now **derived from `InboxMessageTypes`** rather than written twice.

### Fixed — the pi prepush gate ran ailang's Go gates in every repo

`prepush-gate.ts` is baked into the pi image and unconditionally ran `gofmt -l cmd/ internal/`,
`make lint` and `make check-file-sizes`. In `ailang-packages` (zero Go sources) it could never
pass, so **every push was blocked**. An agent made a correct fix, committed it, then spent its
whole budget failing to push and tried to escape by writing a fake `gofmt` shim into
`/usr/local/bin` — what an unpassable gate teaches an unsupervised agent to do. The work was
lost. The gate now checks for tracked `.go` files under the directories it inspects.

### Fixed — approvals hid the tasks nothing could approve; `--remote` guessed no project

`approvals` reported "No pending approvals" while **16 tasks** sat in `pending_approval` in
prod, oldest from 2026-04-28: the view filters on approval records, and these had none. It now
reports them on every invocation with age, cause and whether a worktree still exists, and
`--clear-orphans` cancels them — refusing any task whose worktree is still present.

`--remote gcp` also required a separately exported `AILANG_CLOUD_PROJECT`. It now resolves the
project itself, and **deliberately does not consult `gcloud config`**: the first clean-environment
test resolved to an unrelated project, and `--clear-orphans` against the wrong one would have
been silent and irreversible.

### Fixed — `tools/ci/headroom` parsed zero packages from real `go test` output

The parser matched a hand-anchored `^ok\t` prefix, but Go pads the status column so a real line is
`ok` + two spaces + TAB + pkg + TAB + duration — every real line was dropped, reddening the `test`
and `test-windows` jobs on a suite where every package line is a clean `ok`. The parser now splits
each line on TAB and inspects the fields (field 0 trimmed must be exactly `ok` or `FAIL`; field 2
is the duration or `(cached)`), and a `(cached)` line — which carries NO duration — parses as a
0-second record that still counts toward the package-count floor.

- Real captured `go test` output is now checked in as fixtures under
  `tools/ci/headroom/testdata/` (a cold run and a cache-warm run) and drives a new
  `TestParseRealGoTestOutput`; a hand-typed fixture cannot catch the status-column padding or the
  duration-less cached line.
- Three new mutation tests defend the `budget <= 0` validation, the sort tie-break, and the
  `len(args) < 3` usage guard.
- A second, same-class defect: the cached-line branch compared `fields[2] == "(cached)"` exactly,
  while `.gitattributes` pinned `eol=lf` for `*.golden`/`prompts/*.md`/`*.sh` but not for the
  headroom fixtures — so a Windows checkout rewrote the checked-in fixture and all 105 cached
  records were dropped, not recorded as zero-second. Fixed on both sides, which are not
  alternatives: the splitter strips the carriage return (the pwsh leg can produce a CRLF log at
  runtime, where no `.gitattributes` entry reaches), and the fixtures are pinned `eol=lf` (a
  fixture that changes shape on checkout is not a fixture). The CRLF test derives its input from
  the LF fixture inside the test, so the pin cannot normalise it away.

### Fixed — the mission driver's three notification paths had no per-call deadline (D-60)

`_mc_notify`'s direct `ailang messages send`, `_mc_drain_notices`' drain-time send, and the
`gh issue comment` notice each ran unbounded. The drain is called in the driver's PREFLIGHT, whose
only backstop is the whole-slot `HARD_TIMEOUT` (6 h) — the stall watchdog explicitly disables its
early kill there ("no progress instrument") — so a hung send could cost a whole fire. Each now runs
through the existing `_mc_bounded` at `NOTIFY_TIMEOUT` (env `MISSION_NOTIFY_TIMEOUT`, default 30 s,
matching the two already-bounded slot-verdict sites). Retry count, back-off, payloads and the
non-aborting failure contract are unchanged.
