- `_mc_bounded` runs `( exec "$@" )`, and `exec` cannot parse a `VAR=val` prefix, so the two
  canonical message-store variables are carried by a leading `env`; scoping is unchanged and
  `AILANG_STORAGE` is untouched.
- Bounding would otherwise have deleted a diagnostic: a cut-off command produces no output, so
  `MC_BOUNDED_OUT` is empty and the failure WARNING degraded to `FAILED … after 3 attempts:` with
  nothing after it. The reason is now synthesised whenever the tail is empty and the rc is non-zero,
  which also covers a `mktemp` failure (`rc=125`).

### Fixed — the launchd notification test suite could not observe a send at all

`63a0d2b32` moved the direct send into a command substitution, so the fixture's `ailang()` stub
recorded the call by assigning to `TRACE` inside a subshell and the record died with it — seven
assertions in `launchd drivers (bash 3.2)` had been red on `dev` since.

- `ailang`/`gh` become PATH-executable stubs (required in any case: `exec` bypasses shell
  functions), `log()` appends to the same `MC_TRACE_FILE`, and the teardown loads that file into
  `TRACE` before the assertions run — one medium, so the two negative `checkno` assertions cannot
  pass vacuously.
- `run()` now captures the sourced block's real status instead of `printf`'s, and a dedicated arm
  drives `run()` itself with a failing block so that fix cannot be reverted silently.
- The suite `unset`s `AILANG_MESSAGES_STORE`/`AILANG_MESSAGES_PROJECT`: a subshell inherits the
  caller's exports, so with the session-start exports CLAUDE.md mandates, the store/project guard
  would have stayed green with the production `env` prefix deleted.

`bash tools/launchd/test_driver_notify.sh`: 20 passed/7 failed → **38 passed, 0 failed**. Note the
`launchd drivers (bash 3.2)` job remains red on a *different*, pre-existing set: clearing this red
lets the ordered target reach `test_mission_heartbeat.sh`, which fails 9 arms identically before and
after this change.

### Added — `ailang docs search` GitHub code-search fallback

- When local `design_docs/`/`docs/` aren't found (an installed binary run outside a source
  checkout), `ailang docs search` can now fall back to authenticated GitHub code search. Set
  `GITHUB_TOKEN` or use `gh auth login`; disable the fallback with `--no-github` or
  `AILANG_NO_GITHUB_SEARCH`. Results are cached for one hour under
  `~/.ailang/cache/docsearch/github/` without storing credentials. GitHub-specific logic lives
  entirely outside `internal/docsearch/search.go`'s local search path (zero diff), selected once
  at the CLI boundary via a new `SearchBackend` interface.

### Fixed — `cmd/ailang/exec.go` crossed the 800-line gate and reddened a REQUIRED context

`8c41d41d4` ("chore: checkpoint existing z.ai provider and benchmark work") added a net 9 lines to
`cmd/ailang/exec.go` (10 insertions, 1 deletion), taking it 798 → **807** and failing
`make check-file-sizes` (>800). That step
lives in CI's `test` job, which is a REQUIRED context, so a file-size overrun on an unrelated
checkpoint blocked every open pull request in the repo.

- `spanningEventHandler` and its streaming callbacks (`newSpanningEventHandler`, `SetContext`,
  `OnTurnStart`, `OnText`, `OnToolUse`, `OnToolResult`, `OnTurnEnd`, `OnError`) move **byte-for-byte**
  into `cmd/ailang/exec_events.go`. `exec.go` 807 → 614, new file 209; the diff of `exec.go` is 193
  deletions and **zero** additions. Same package, no signature, visibility or import-path change.
### Added — mission configuration can be inspected, staged, and applied from one registry

`M-MISSION-LOOP-WORKBENCH`, Phases 1–2. Mission wiring had lived across six surfaces, with a
hand-maintained reach table that had already hidden a live fork and configuration drift.

- One TOML entry per mission in `missions/` is parsed with the in-tree BurntSushi/toml. Validation
  rejects colliding `boot_offset` values and deliberately rejects `[roles]`, naming
  `M-MODEL-REGISTRY-SINGLE-SOURCE` (`models.yml` via `ailang models role`) as the sole owner of
  model assignment.
- **`ailang mission list|doctor|install|apply`** covers the four registered missions. `list`
  computes reach from the registry; the prose reach table in `mission-control.sh` was deleted and
  tests prevent either it or registry/driver boot-offset drift from returning.
- **`install` writes both artifacts only to `*.staged`.** This is a safety boundary: the driver
  re-sources its installed env on every fire, so an in-place write would change a running schedule
  without a reload. Env rendering authors only mission name, repo, doc and workdir; role chains,
  allowlists, PATH, comments and blank lines pass through verbatim.
- **`doctor` was accepted only after reproducing live-rig failures:** reviewed and installed docs
  env files disagreed on `MISSION_PLANNER_ALLOWLIST`, two plists supplied a PATH without
  `/usr/sbin`, and world did not source `pin-root.sh`.
- **`apply` is the only mutating verb.** It refuses a live pidfile unless forced, never retries on
  its own, and gives bootout settle, bootstrap and readiness verification 10s, 10s and 15s
  respectively; a test bounds the worst case at 35s.
- All four missions were adopted only after staged-versus-installed diffs caught two defects: both
  launchd streams targeted the driver's own `$LOG`, and an appended bare `MISSION_WORKDIR` would
  have overwritten the pinned worktree with the source clone. Their discovery on first use is why
  staging and applying remain separate operations.

### Fixed — the mission workbench repair restored every check left red by the attended landing

The follow-up repair in PR #1055 corrected the Windows, shell, ratchet and test defects from
`M-MISSION-LOOP-WORKBENCH`; all six originally-red checks were green at its final commit.

- Workdir validation now accepts both POSIX-absolute registry paths and host-absolute fixture paths
  while rejecting relative paths. The Bash 3.2 launchd job remains shell-only, and its explanation
  is a make comment rather than a shell no-op; the Go mission suite remains available to `make test`
  and the explicit `test-mission-registry` hand-run target.
- `kill_unix.go` had shipped without a build constraint or Windows counterpart. Go does not treat
  `_unix` as a GOOS suffix, so the package did not compile on Windows. This was found by direct
  measurement, not a CI log: fixing workdir validation alone would still have left both Windows
  checks red while appearing to address them. The Windows stub now compiles and fails closed.
- The repair corrected a test comment that overstated local mutation coverage, skips the driver-copy
  down-ratchet when sibling rig checkouts are absent, and replaced a slash-only backup `baseName`
  with `filepath.Base`/`filepath.Join` after Windows exposed the final failure.

### Added — the fleet can now see how much of a subscription bucket it has spent

`M-QUOTA-RATIONING-ROUTING` M2. Routing could ask "is this lane up?" and never "can it afford to
be used?" — a 1-token probe answers the first, nothing answered the second, and on 2026-09-06 that
cost half a codex bucket in a day.

- **`ailang mission quota`** reports fleet-wide consumption per (bucket, window) against a 10%/day
  ration (`--bucket`, `--json`, `--consolidate`). Fleet-wide because that is what the subscription
  physically is: four missions drawing on one bucket, where per-mission accounting would let every
  loop believe it was within budget while the bucket emptied.
- **`quota_tokens` is a new field end to end** — `chain_stages` migration v20, `IterationStage`,
  `ChainStage`, `Backend.UpdateStageQuotaTokens` (all five backends, Firestore included), and
  `MissionRollup.QuotaTokensByBucket`. It is deliberately NOT `tokens_in`/`tokens_out`: `tokens > 0`
  is the cost estimator's only structural marker for "metered and priceable", so the 4,979 quota
  stages recording zero do so by design, and reusing those fields would have invoiced subscription
  runs in order to fix the ration. `Validate` rejects `quota_tokens` without a `quota_bucket`,
  which would otherwise be counted once by each accounting system as if it were two runs.
- **A held lock cannot wedge a mission fire.** The write path is an append journal taking no lock
  at all; the journal is the sole authority for spend and `~/.ailang/state/quota-ledger.json` is a
  cache of totals plus provider-supplied capacities, which any reader folds in memory.
  Consolidation is the only step needing exclusion and gives up instantly when contended.
- **Window boundaries come from the provider** when a reset instant is known, snapping our window
  to the one actually being enforced. With none pinned, `BoundarySource()` reports `local` rather
  than implying an authority it does not have. A bucket with unknown capacity is reported
  UNRATIONED and loud — never rationed on a guess.

### Fixed — a probe that passed could strand the fleet on a spent bucket

`M-QUOTA-RATIONING-ROUTING` M4. A start-probe cannot see a spent bucket: the probe is one
tiny request and the limit is on the agent-sized request behind it. The rung therefore
probes green, dies on its first real call, and because the walk stopped there, every rung
below it is never reached.

Measured 2026-09-07: `pi:ollama/glm-5.3:cloud` probed rc=0 then returned `429: you have
reached your session usage limit`, and the fleet crash-looped for hours while
`pi:openrouter/z-ai/glm-5.3` — the next rung, healthy, with $93.33 of credit — was never
tried. Before that, an Anthropic lane returning `rc=2` had discarded its own error text,
so the cascade that moved every role onto codex (13M → 713M tokens in eighteen hours,
spending a weekly bucket in under fifteen) was never explained anywhere.

- A runtime quota signal now demotes the rung for that fire and re-walks the chain,
  skipping demotions in the preference list, the fallback chain, and both pin paths.
- With nothing left to walk to, the fire PAUSES with its own `PAUSED-NO-CAPACITY`
  verdict and a notice on both channels — reporting it as `CRASHED` would send someone
  debugging a mission that did the right thing.
- `_mc_probe` logs the captured output on `rc=2`, the one outcome where the fleet loses
  a lane and previously the only one that said nothing.
- `ailang mission quota --over` lets the driver skip a rung whose bucket is over its
  10%/day ration. It lists only PROVEN exceedances, so it is inert until capacities are
  known — and the driver says so when it cannot read the ledger, rather than proceeding
  quietly unrationed.

The quota signature is anchored to the emitters actually observed, not to words like
"quota" or "rate limit": the driver log carries mission prose about quota routinely, and
a loose pattern would demote a healthy controller for writing about limits.

### Fixed — the compile cache executed artifacts it never verified, and serve-api published a tool surface the source contradicted

`M-COMPILE-CACHE-UNVERIFIED-ARTIFACTS`, all four milestones (mission iterations 330–333),
reported at [#1046](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/1046). A cached module could be
loaded with no check that its artifacts belonged to the key authorising them, so `ailang run` could
silently execute a stale program — measured end to end: source says `99`, the manifest key is the
correct key for the `99` source, the blobs are from the `42` compile, and the run prints `42` with
no diagnostic.

- **M1** — artifact loads are verified against the expected key, version and module ID, with
  per-blob SHA-256 checks before any decode and byte ceilings on blobs, the stamp and the module
  aggregate. Publication failures are visible (`CACHE_WRITE_FAILED`) instead of discarded.
- **M2** — the cache key is computed from the exact bytes the lexer parsed. The loader retains the
  source as an immutable snapshot rather than re-reading disk at key time, and a module with no
  snapshot bypasses both lookup and publication with `CACHE_SOURCE_UNAVAILABLE` instead of hashing
  as the empty string.
- **M3** — `ailang cache compile-clear` now removes the artifacts the manifest authorised, not just
  the manifest, and returns an error rather than printing success over a failed deletion.
- **M4** — `serve-api` refuses to start when a module's cached interface contradicts its source. An
  exported `@route` function with no non-nil interface export is reported as
  `CACHE_ROUTE_IFACE_MISMATCH`, naming the source, module and function, with the compile-clear
  remedy — where previously the route was dropped in silence and the MCP tool surface came up short.

Known limitation, filed separately: the compile artifact cache does not publish on **Windows** —
`sanitizeModuleID` leaves the drive-letter colon in the artifact directory name, which Windows
forbids as a path component. Pre-existing; M1's diagnostic is what made it visible.

### Mission recovery
### Mission Ollama quota correction (2026-09-07)
`ailang mission quota` now reuses Pi's legacy fractional usage gauge: warns at 80%
and blocks new Ollama Cloud admission at 95% in either window. Basic admission no
longer requires manually supplied reset metadata; verified metadata optionally adds
pacing. Unknown provider data still blocks. Local Ollama routing is unaffected.
- Preserve a foreign mission repository when re-executing a pinned fleet driver; reject ambiguous origins loudly (World #1075).
- Add opt-in `mission role-run --state-db FILE` with persisted stage admission, lease fencing and cancellation. New `mission attempt status|reconcile|cancel` commands expose recovery state. No automatic rerun, acceptance or live fleet migration.
### Mission role dispatch
- Add opt-in `ailang mission role-run --request FILE --receipt NEW_FILE [--dry-run]` with explicit model routing, origin-vendor evaluator independence, bounded adapter execution and exclusive receipts. Execution success does not imply artifact acceptance; live mission drivers are unchanged.
- Enforce cumulative token limits in the Pi adapter; retain the existing cost guard.

## [v0.35.1] - 2026-09-05

### Fixed — codex had the key in its environment and looked for it in a file

Every codex Cloud Run turn 401'd against `wss://api.openai.com/v1/responses`. The
credential was never the problem: the same Secret Manager key returns **200** from
`/v1/models` (133 models) and from `/v1/responses` on `gpt-5.6-sol`. `codex`
authenticates from `~/.codex/auth.json` and **does not consult `OPENAI_API_KEY`** —
which this repo already documented, in [`cost.go`](../internal/executor/codex/cost.go)
and in `codex.go`, because `authLane` reads that file rather than the environment.
The jobs were handed the env var and no `auth.json`, so codex had no credential at
all.

`EnsureAPIKeyAuth` writes the `{"auth_mode":"apikey", ...}` file the CLI expects,
from `OPENAI_API_KEY`, **only when no `auth.json` already exists** — with `O_EXCL`
and mode `0600`. It is called from the cloud job's entry point, not from the codex
constructor: writing a credential is a deployment action, and a library that
materialises one as a side effect of being constructed would do it during `go test`
too. The never-overwrite rule is not caution, it is the point: the rig
authenticates codex with a ChatGPT-subscription `auth.json` *and* keeps
`OPENAI_API_KEY` exported for other tools, so overwriting would silently move
flat-rate runs onto a metered key with nothing in the output to say so. An existing
file wins even when unparseable — "unreadable" is not "absent", and guessing wrong
costs real money.

This keeps the metered lane on an API key, which OpenAI documents as the right way
to authenticate automation. It needs no subscription token, and so carries neither
the *"only one machine or serialized job stream"* condition nor the ~8-day refresh
that a ChatGPT `auth.json` in Secret Manager would (sunholo-data/ailang#903).

### Fixed — the fleet had no memory admission control, and it stampeded every boot

Capping ollama on 2026-09-03 held: across the three `JetsamEvent` OOMs that followed
(09-04 05:08, 09-05 08:29, 09-05 09:23) its physical footprint was **25.77 GB at
every one, identical to two decimals** — flat, under load, days apart, against a
90.39 GiB peak before the caps. It was no longer the growth term.

The box filled anyway: ~60 MB free, 46 GB wired, and a compressor holding **131 GB**
of logical pages while only 37 GB was resident across 566 processes. The largest
identifiable population was ours — **22 concurrent Claude Code processes**, 12.7
CPU-hours. Every mission plist carries `RunAtLoad=true` (deliberately: it is what
restores the cadence after a reboot), so a boot or GUI login fires all four missions
within seconds. `dev.ailang.mission-motoko.plist` recorded that alignment on
2026-08-17 and fixed only the steady-state half with non-harmonic `StartInterval`s;
the boot half fired again on 09-05, 33 `claude` processes inside ten minutes.

[`mission-control.sh`](../tools/launchd/mission-control.sh) gains two gates, placed
after the kill switch, the overlap yield and the dry run — so a disabled, yielding or
wiring-checked fire stays instant — and before the model probes, so a gated fire
spends nothing:

- **Boot stagger.** v1 0s · world 420s · docs 840s · motoko 1260s, taken only inside
  `MISSION_BOOT_WINDOW` (900s). Outside that window the offset is not applied at all,
  so the tuned non-harmonic phase is untouched. The 7-minute spacing exceeds the worst
  measured controller preamble (240s of opus probes on one v1 slot).
- **Memory admission.** Refuses to start below `MISSION_MIN_AVAIL_GB` (16) available
  or above `MISSION_MAX_COMPRESSED_GB` (48) compressed, waiting up to
  `MISSION_MEM_WAIT` (600s) before yielding the slot — a transient spike should delay
  a fire, not cancel it, since a skipped slot costs motoko 13h. Where `vm_stat` cannot
  answer the gate disables itself **loudly** rather than wedging every mission.

Available is `free + inactive + speculative + purgeable`, never `free` alone: at the
09-23 event free was 66 MB while 7.7 GB sat reclaimable in `inactive`. The full
expression separates the two observed states by two orders of magnitude (7.8 GB at
each OOM, 104 GB idle). Not `memoryPressure` — the kernel's own flag read `false`
throughout the 09-03 panic.

Thresholds are **starting values**; nobody has profiled an iteration's peak, so every
fire logs the live numbers for the log to correct them.
[`test_mission_memgate.sh`](../tools/launchd/test_mission_memgate.sh) (24 arms) pins
the parsers, the offset spacing and both refusal arms — including the greedy-`sed`
bug this change shipped with for an hour: `s/.*sec *= *\([0-9]*\).*/\1/` on
`kern.boottime` captures **usec**, not sec, and reported 1788394233s of uptime on a
box 20 minutes old, silently disabling the stagger in exactly the window it exists
for.


### Fixed — the container verified nothing, so a codex image ran opencode

`execute-job` decided which executor to run by reading `AILANG_PROVIDER` and, when
that was empty, assuming `"claude"`. That is a second, independent answer to a
question the dispatcher had already answered from the image — and two answers with
nothing forcing agreement is the bug that
[`dispatch_provider.go`](../internal/coordinator/dispatch_provider.go) removed on the
dispatcher side in v0.35.0. Removing it there left it here, which is why it kept
coming back.

Three prod runs died on it (2026-08-27 x2, 2026-08-28): the `agent-codex` and
`agent-codex-go` images were told to run `opencode`, which they do not install. Each
cloned the repo (**24,032 files**), created a branch and resolved pi extensions
before `exec` failed on the missing binary — about a minute spent discovering
something knowable at startup.

Two guards now, failing for different reasons:

- **The image declares what it installs.** `ENV AILANG_IMAGE_PROVIDER` sits beside
  the `npm install` line in each agent Dockerfile (`multi` for `agent-eval`, which
  genuinely carries several). `resolveContainerProvider` refuses a mismatch and
  names both sides. There is no default: a provider this process invented is
  precisely what shipped the bug.
- **The executor proves its binary is there.** `preflightExecutor` calls the
  executor's own `HealthCheck` before the clone. `HealthCheck` is not new —
  `eval_harness`, `doctor`, `smoke-motoko` and motoko's canary all call it, and
  `TestHealthCheck_MissingBinary` has covered exactly this for codex and opencode
  all along. This path simply never called it. It alone would have caught all three
  failures with no declaration at all.

`TestDockerfilesDeclareTheDispatchersProvider` ties the Dockerfiles to
`coordinator.VariantProviders()` so the two statements of one fact cannot drift,
resolving `FROM` up the image chain so the `-go` variants are proven to inherit
rather than assumed to.

### Measured — OpenAI prompt caching is time-gated, so a FAST eval suite gets none of it

Investigating gpt-6-astra's cost turned up a property of OpenAI's automatic prompt
cache that changes how standard-mode eval spend should be read. The harness already
splits the prompt correctly for caching (`splitCacheablePrefix` in
[repair.go](../internal/eval_harness/repair.go) hands the teaching prompt to
`GenerateCodeSplit` as a stable prefix, and for AILANG that prefix is one global
teaching prompt, byte-identical across every benchmark). Caching still delivered
**nothing** on a real suite run:

| run | benchmarks | wall time | input tokens | cache reads | banked | real |
|-----|-----------:|----------:|-------------:|------------:|-------:|-----:|
| core+frontier, COLD | 31 | 2m18s | 843,908 | **0 (0%)** | $9.76 | $9.76 |
| same 2 benchmarks, re-run 10min later | 2 | — | 53,878 | **53,872 (100%)** | — | — |
| core+frontier, WARM (repeat 10min later) | 31 | — | 882,187 | **816,821 (93%)**, 30/31 rows | $10.63 | **$3.28** |

The warm repeat scored **identically** — 29/31, the same two failures
(`docx_reimplement` runtime_error, `markdown_reimplement` compile_error, both
finish_reason=stop) — for **$3.28 of real spend against $10.63 banked, a $7.35
discount the banked figure does not show**. Cold vs warm real cost: $9.76 → $3.28, 3x.

The cause is OpenAI's documented ~5-10 minute cache **write-availability latency**,
not our prompt shape or parallelism: the whole 31-benchmark run finished inside that
window, so every call missed. Re-running `cli_args` and `float_eq` afterwards — same
model, same prompt, only elapsed time different — hit 100%.

Consequences for reading cost data:

- A fast suite's banked cost is the **real** cost; there is no hidden discount to net off.
- A *repeat* of the same suite (N>=3 de-flake, re-gates, longitudinal tracking) costs
  ~3x less in real money — measured, not projected: $9.76 cold vs $3.28 warm for the
  identical 31-benchmark suite at identical quality. That run is 87% input-bound
  ($8.44 input vs $1.32 output), which is why the discount is so large.
- **The banked number does not fall when the cache hits.** Standard-mode CostUSD is the
  uncached list rate by design (a ceiling that never understates spend), so the warm run
  banked $10.63 — HIGHER than the cold $9.76, purely because it emitted more output
  tokens. Read `cache_read_input_tokens` before comparing two runs' costs, or a cheaper
  run will look more expensive.
- The lever is **elapsed time, not concurrency**. Warming does not help a suite that
  finishes in two minutes.
- Cache savings are shape-dependent: an output-bound run barely moves. Fable 5's last
  banked run (2026-07-09, `gauntlet_passk`) was 538,349 output vs 293,655 input tokens,
  so perfect caching would cut at most ~9% of it.

**`prompt_cache_key` was tried and rejected.** Deriving OpenAI's cache-routing key from
the stable prefix looked like the fix for cold runs. Measured: it opens a NEW cache
namespace, and the two benchmarks that had just hit 100% dropped straight back to 0%.
Default prefix-hash routing already reaches 100% once the cache has had time to fill, so
the key added a guaranteed cold-start cost for no demonstrated benefit. Reverted.

Do not read a pre-v0.31.0 row's absent cache fields as "no caching happened" — cache
fields were first persisted in v0.31.0 (2026-07-29), so every earlier row (including all
27 banked Fable rows) simply never recorded them.


### Fixed — every GPT-6+ model was unusable: the token-parameter gate was a `gpt-5` literal

`usesMaxCompletionTokens` ([internal/ai/openai/client.go](../internal/ai/openai/client.go))
routed OpenAI requests to `max_completion_tokens` via
`strings.HasPrefix(lower, "gpt-5")`. That is a *literal*, not a generation test, so
**gpt-6-astra** (released 2026-08-27) fell through to the legacy `max_tokens` and OpenAI
rejected every call:

```
openai error (400): Unsupported parameter: 'max_tokens' is not supported with this
model. Use 'max_completion_tokens' instead.
```

The failure is generation-gated, not model-specific — GPT-7 would have hit it too, and the
400 arrives at *eval* time, i.e. after a benchmark run has been paid for and queued. The
gate now **parses** the generation (`^gpt-(\d+)` → `>= 5`), so the next OpenAI generation
needs no code change to be evaluated. `gpt-4`/`gpt-4o`/`gpt-3.5-turbo` still get
`max_tokens`; the o-series substring rules are unchanged. Both call sites (`chat.go`,
`step.go`) share the one helper, so the fix is complete by construction.
Regression cases added for `gpt-6-astra`, `gpt-6`, `gpt-7-whatever`, `gpt-4o` and
`gpt-3.5-turbo` in `client_test.go`.

### Added — `gpt6-astra` promoted to `benchmark_suite` on a clean win over `gpt5-6-sol`

Measured against sol's **banked** v0.32.0 baseline rows — the same corpus the ELO anchor
was fitted from, so no comparator was re-run (M-EVAL-ROLLING-ELO / model-manager §5.5):

| tier | astra | gpt5-6-sol |
|------|------:|-----------:|
| frontier | **6/8** | 3/8 |
| core (comparable cells) | **7/7** | 6/7 |

Frontier is a **strict superset**, not a trade: astra additionally passes
`contract_sorted_merge`, `gauntlet_10` and `quine`, and loses nothing sol wins (both fail
only `docx_reimplement` and `markdown_reimplement`). On core it passes
`explicit_dataflow_ssa`, the benchmark this file long recorded as one "every OpenAI model
fails". Across all 15 comparable cells: **astra 4 wins, sol 0**. Astra's own full panel is
core 23/23, frontier 6/8, anchored standard ELO 2554.4, N=2 with identical results.

`gpt5-6-sol` is **kept** alongside rather than retired: astra costs 2x ($10/$50 vs $5/$30),
so sol remains the cost-effective OpenAI reference and longitudinal anchor — the
claude-sonnet-5 / claude-sonnet-4-6 precedent. Astra is also in `extended_suite`, and is
standard-eval only (never `agent_suite`, on cost).

⚠️ **Process note.** An earlier pass in this same session withheld this promotion, calling
it a "tie at 2x the price". That verdict was reached by reading models.yml PROSE (a
historical "core 26/26" annotation) instead of the banked per-benchmark rows in
`docs/static/benchmarks/latest.json`, which were available the whole time and show sol
losing 4 cells to 0. The prose was not wrong; it was answering a different question (an
older 26-benchmark core panel, now 23). Compare banked rows, not summaries of them.

### Added — `gpt6-astra` in the model registry

GPT-6 Astra ($10/$50 per 1M, 1.05M context, 128K max output, knowledge cutoff
2026-04-30, `reasoning.effort` low→max with no `none`/`minimal`). API access and a
fizzbuzz round-trip verified 2026-09-05. **Deliberately absent from every suite** — it is
reachable only via `--models gpt6-astra` until it clears `--tier smoke` and is placed on
the anchored ELO series. At 2× gpt-5.6-sol's price the promotion bar is a clear core-tier
win, not a tie (model-manager §5.5).


### Docs — stale references to the retired branch-push deployment model

The deployment model was unified on 2026-09-03 (push → dev, `v*` tag → test, promote by
version → prod; design `design_docs/planned/v0_35_0/m-unified-release-model.md`, model
written down once in `ailang-multivac/.claude/skills/release/SKILL.md`). Documents in this
repo still described the old one — a branch push rebuilding every image via
`ailang-multivac/cloudbuild.yaml`, the `cloudbuild.yaml` ⇄ `cloudbuild-images.yaml`
"lockstep" rule, `promote all`, and a tag auto-promoting to prod:

- **`docs/internal/EXECUTOR_SHAPE.md`** §6 rewritten. A new executor variant now needs a
  build step in *this* repo's `cloudbuild-dev.yaml` **and** `cloudbuild-release.yaml`
  (release adds `:$TAG_NAME` and has no `allowFailure`), a bootstrap mirror in
  `ailang-multivac/cloudbuild-images.yaml`, the job in `terraform/cloud_run_jobs.tf`, and
  its `job:image` pair in `scripts/cloudbuild-lib.sh` `AGENT_JOBS` — which `make test-lib`
  cross-checks against terraform. Pillar 2 checklist and the intro touchpoint list follow.
- **`.claude/rules/cloud-endpoints.md`** — added the missing **test** row
  (`ailang-test-dashboard` / `ailang-multivac-test`) and replaced "Deploy is Terraform-only"
  with how a route actually reaches each environment. A merged dashboard change is public on
  **dev only** until it is tagged and promoted.
- **`design_docs/planned/m-coordinator-execution-trust.md`** — the Rollout section's
  two-pipeline split is superseded: one push to ailang `dev` now carries both the
  coordinator and the `agent-pi` gate, and `promote` has `core`/`agents`/`agent-pi` sets, so
  V26/V31/V32 are marked as expired evidence rather than current state.
- **`design_docs/planned/m-completion-path-parity-sprint-plan.md`** — trigger table

- **`design_docs/planned/docparse-billing/responsibility-multivac.md`**,
  **`docker/agent-motoko-multivac-prs.md`** (marked HISTORICAL),
  **`docker/resident/test-image.sh`** (names all three pipelines that run it), and both
  **`docs/sprint-retros/m-ci-build-speed*.md`** (retirement note; the measurements stand).

## [v0.35.0] - 2026-09-03

### Fixed — the eval lane was talking to an UNCAPPED ollama the whole time

Two ollama servers can hold port 11434 simultaneously, because they bind different
stacks: `dev.ollama.serve` (ours, `127.0.0.1`, IPv4, carrying `OLLAMA_GPU_OVERHEAD` and
`OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH`) and the **Ollama.app GUI**, which auto-starts at login and binds
`*:11434` over IPv6 with `OLLAMA_HOST=0.0.0.0` and **no memory caps at all**.

The harness default was `http://localhost:11434`. `localhost` resolves to both `::1` and
`127.0.0.1`, and Go tries IPv6 first — so requests went to the **uncapped GUI server**.
Measured 2026-09-03: a request to `localhost:11434` was logged by the GUI server with
client address `::1` while the capped server's log did not move. Every eval request in the
overnight log carries the same `::1` address, so the plist tuning this repo has maintained
for months — `KV_CACHE_TYPE`, `FLASH_ATTENTION`, `NUM_PARALLEL` — plausibly never applied
to evals, and neither would the memory caps added earlier today.

Every endpoint in the eval lane is now pinned to `127.0.0.1`: `defaultEndpoint` in
`internal/ai/ollama/client.go`, the provider default in `cmd/ailang/exec.go`, the
`/api/ps` probe in `chains_live.go`, and the liveness gates in `nightly-lang-eval.sh` and
`os-rotation-filler.sh` (a gate that the uncapped server answered would pass while the
capped server was down). Verified after the fix: `curl` and a Go client using the
harness's own resolver path both reach the capped server, logged as `127.0.0.1`.

Diagnostic: `lsof -nP -iTCP:11434 -sTCP:LISTEN` — **more than one listener means the GUI
app is serving** and its server must be stopped.

### Fixed — dev CI was red for ~24 hours on FIVE defects stacked in one sequential job

`dev` went red at `327db37cd` (2026-09-02 13:19) and stayed red. Five separate causes,
found by walking the check set back to the last green (`7668ed9df`). Only the first was
visible; each fix revealed the next, because CI's `test` and `lint` jobs are long ordered
step lists and a failing step `skip`s everything behind it:

1. **A dangling reference the intentional fmt A/B removal left behind.** `c8c841e24`
   deliberately removed the Wednesday fmt A/B from `tools/launchd/nightly-eval.sh` — and
   with it the `# BEGIN/END FMT_AB_TESTABLE_FUNCTIONS` markers. Its message says *"its
   schedule test is deleted"*, which was true only because a concurrent docs commit had
   deleted `tools/launchd/test_fmt_ab_schedule.sh` **by accident**; `ce05af862` then
   correctly reverted that accident, restoring a test whose subject was legitimately gone.
   Two callers went red: `TestFmtDriverScheduleSatisfiesOrderIntegrity` and
   `make test-launchd-drivers`. The removal is now complete — the fixture script, the Go
   test and the `make/test.mk` line are gone. No analyzer coverage is lost:
   `CheckFmtOrderIntegrity` is still exercised by `censored_integrity_test.go`.

2. **`lint` red on formatting.** Seven files arriving with the coordinator merges were not
   gofmt-canonical.

3. **Two files over the 800-line gate.** `internal/observatory/backend_gcp.go` (811) and
   `internal/messaging/inbox.go` (850). Split as pure moves into `backend_gcp_chains.go`
   and `inbox_flow.go`.

4. **`lint` red on `golangci-lint unused`, one step behind defect 2.** Fixing the formatting
   step let step 6 run for the first time in a day. Two findings, handled differently on
   purpose: a never-assigned `cancel` field in the coordinator finalize test harness was
   removed, and `diffResultFromEvidence` — whose consumer is M-COMPLETION-PATH-PARITY's
   deliberately-outstanding M1b — was annotated `//nolint:unused` with that reason rather
   than deleted. Deleting a symbol because a linter calls it unused, when half its contract
   is already shipped, is the failure this repo's coding standards name explicitly.

5. **`Build windows-latest`: 21 `TestFinalize_*` tests.** `newFinalizeHarness` registered a
   `t.Cleanup` closer for two of its three stores and none for the observatory backend, so
   `observatory.db` stayed open. POSIX unlinks open files; Windows refuses, and `t.TempDir()`
   cleanup took every test in the file down. The Windows leg reads `cancelled` on every
   recent `dev` commit — the matrix fails fast and ubuntu was dying first — so this was
   invisible from `dev` entirely.

**The shape is the finding, not any one defect.** `check-file-sizes` is step 15 of the `test`
job and the job was dying at step 11, so steps 12-60 — 45 gates — read `skipped` for a day.
At the last green those two files were 788 and 773 lines and step 15 passed; they crossed 800
inside the window where nothing could see them. A red that fails EARLY in a long sequential
job silently suspends every gate behind it, and the check set reports ONE failure where there
are five. The same mechanism hid defect 4 behind defect 2 (steps in the `lint` job) and defect
5 behind defect 1 (fail-fast across a build matrix). Each fix is cheap; discovering that there
was another one behind it cost the whole day.

### Added — `make check-referenced-paths`: refuse a merge that deletes a file make/CI still calls

`scripts/check_referenced_paths.sh` enumerates every `tools/…`/`scripts/…` `.sh`/`.py` path
referenced from `Makefile`, `make/*.mk` and `.github/workflows/*.yml`, and refuses when one is
missing or untracked. It carries an anti-vacuity floor (refuses below 20 enumerated paths) so a
broken enumerator fails loudly rather than passing empty. Wired into `ci:`, `code-health.mk` and
`ci.yml`, with a 6-arm self-test (`make test-check-referenced-paths`) covering the missing path,
the workflow-sourced path, substring anchoring, the floor, and the untracked-but-present case.
This gate catches the *deletion* half of defect 1 above; it does not catch the marker-removal
half, and does not claim to.

Its first version did not have the teeth it claimed. An independent evaluator got it to return
rc=0 on a fixture carrying four dangling references written in forms the matcher did not
recognise (`.bash`, `.pl`, uppercase `.SH`, and a make-variable-composed path). Three are now
matched — the extension test is a case-insensitive set membership rather than a hardcoded
alternation — and the fourth cannot be resolved without evaluating make variables, so the
script's header now discloses the full scope and ends with: a green here means *no LITERAL
`tools/`/`scripts/` script reference dangles*, never *no reference dangles*. Self-test 6 arms
→ 10, and one pre-existing arm was found not to pin the branch it named (it was redundant with
the untracked-path arm, and survived that branch being neutered).
### Added — the production motoko probe lane's process-group kill is pinned by a harness

Row 6g made both timeout wrappers in `tools/eval/motoko_connection_probe.sh` kill the
child process group rather than the wrapper PID, but only the self-test wrapper was
pinned: reverting the production `run_lane` hunk left the 40-arm suite green, because
`bash -n` cannot tell a group kill from a PID kill. A surviving driver descendant on the
rig then reads as "the local model declined to act" — the very conclusion the probe
exists to prevent. `run_lane` now has the same fixture-cwd + `lsof -c sleep` survivor
oracle the self-test wrapper had (PR #985, evaluator 87/100). Design doc:
`design_docs/implemented/v0_35_0/m-motoko-connection-probe-run-lane-harness.md`.

### Changed — motoko probe self-tests pin group KILL and contain the lsof oracle

The production `run_lane` group-SIGKILL escalation now has its own TERM-immune
wrapper-grandchild arm, while the existing arm continues to pin group TERM. A bounded
Darwin startup gate also refuses an ambient `lsof` outside `getconf PATH`, with hostile
and benign PATH re-exec arms pinning both outcomes. The suite grows from 43 to 46 arms
and by about 11 seconds on this rig. The production
`tools/eval/motoko_connection_probe.sh` was not modified.

### Changed — mission-control/SKILL.md split 4,201 → 2,854 lines via `resources/`

The Gate-2 **verification protocol** — 18 rules, 1,378 lines — moved verbatim to
`.claude/skills/mission-control/resources/verification-protocol.md`, the `resources/`
layout every other multi-file skill here already uses. It was the right section to move
because of its shape: each rule's *title states the rule* while its *body carries the
measured evidence* (the war story, the discriminating commands, the tell). The titles stay
in `SKILL.md` as a numbered index — the rules are cited by number across the later gates
and the mission log, and that numbering is unchanged — with a mandatory pointer to the
full text at the point Gate 2 needs it. The other five gates no longer carry ~33k tokens
of Gate-2 epistemics they never use.

Verified as a **move, not a rewrite**: the extracted block is byte-identical to
`HEAD:SKILL.md` lines 1047–2424, lines 1–1046 are untouched, Gate 3 onward is
byte-identical, and all 18 rule anchors resolve in the new file.

### Added — the context-doc gate now checks pointers in `SKILL.md` too

Moving 1,378 lines behind a single `resources/` link makes that link load-bearing, so
`check_context_docs.sh` gained link verification for every `SKILL.md` (it already covered
`CLAUDE.md` and the rules). It immediately found skills promising a second layer that was
never written — **`parser-developer` advertises five `resources/*.md` files that do not
exist**, plus one each in `agent-inbox` and `local-ollama-eval`. Agents sent there for the
discriminating detail found nothing and carried on without it.

Fifteen known-broken links are ratcheted in `scripts/context_docs_links_baseline.txt` as a
burn-down list: no new dead pointer may land, and an entry whose link starts resolving must
be removed. Placeholder links inside worked examples (`path/to/doc.md`) are excluded.
Self-test now 19 arms.

### Added — `make check-context-docs`: progressive disclosure is now a CI gate

Context documents (`CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/rules/*.md`, `.claude/skills/*/SKILL.md`) load
**wholesale** into every session that triggers them, so an unscoped or bloated one is a
permanent tax on every future session. Three failure modes were live in the tree, none of
which produced any symptom:

- **A `paths:` glob matching nothing is a rule that never loads, and never fails.**
  `ailang-syntax.md` was scoped to `stdlib/**` while the tree has `std/` — the AILANG syntax
  rule had silently stopped loading for every stdlib edit. Fixed, and the scope widened to the
  `.ail` trees that actually exist (`benchmarks/`, `eval_projects/`, `mcp_tools/`).
- **Two rules had no `paths:` block at all**, so `coding-standards.md` and `dev-workflow.md`
  load in every session by accident rather than by decision. Both are defensible always-on
  rules; each now carries an `<!-- always-on: … -->` marker stating why, which the gate
  requires.
- **`SKILL.md` files grow without bound because appending is easier than filing.**
  `mission-control/SKILL.md` had reached **4,201 lines (~96k tokens) with zero reference
  files** — a quarter of a small context window spent before the skill does anything.

`scripts/check_context_docs.sh` asserts: every rule is either path-scoped or explicitly
marked always-on; every glob matches a tracked file; every relative link resolves; and line
caps (300 `CLAUDE.md` / 200 rule / 500 `SKILL.md`, the last being Anthropic's own guidance).
The eight oversize skills are grandfathered in `scripts/context_docs_baseline.txt` as a
ratchet — they may shrink, never grow, and the gate also fails a stale entry once a doc drops
back under its cap, so an exemption nobody removes cannot silently re-authorise the next
regression. 14 self-test arms in `scripts/test_check_context_docs.sh`, including a vacuity arm.

The convention itself is documented where it is needed rather than always-on: a new
path-scoped `.claude/rules/context-docs.md` (`CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/rules/**`,
`.claude/skills/**`, `scripts/hooks/**`) — the rule surfaces exactly when someone edits a
context document. Wired into `make ci` **and** its own CI step, because `make ci` is a local
aggregate that CI never invokes.

### Documented — the ollama memory budget that panicked the rig on 2026-09-03

The rig kernel-panicked at 02:23 (`watchdog timeout: no checkins from watchdogd in 91 seconds`)
with **15 MB free of 128 GB**, compressor at 53.6 GB, file cache evicted to 238 pages, and the
pager reclaiming **20 of 3,088 pages wanted**. Not an upgrade — the OS build is identical either
side — and not generated code: `AILANG_EVAL_MAX_RSS` bounds only the eval subject, so it never
observed the thing that actually filled the machine.

Ranked by `residentMemoryBytes` from the panic report, **ollama held 195 GB across 4 processes**
against ~11.5 GB for all 44 Claude Code sessions. The chain is entirely configuration defaults:
ollama claims 84% of unified memory as VRAM (`total="107.5 GiB"`), reserves nothing
(`overhead="0 B"`), and therefore auto-selects the model's full native context
(`default_num_ctx=262144`). At 256k that runner peaked at **90.39 GiB**, leaving ~38 GB for the
desktop, the agent fleet and the harness.

Two server-side variables now bound it in `dev.ollama.serve.plist` —
`OLLAMA_GPU_OVERHEAD=32 GiB` (**verified**: `available` dropped 107.0 → 75.0 GiB) and
`OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH=131072` (**applied but unproven**; ollama still logged
`default_num_ctx=262144` against the reduced budget, and small-prompt probes cannot
discriminate). The distinction that matters: `OLLAMA_GPU_OVERHEAD` is *admission control* and a
loaded model can still grow past it as KV fills, so the context bound is not redundant.

Surfacing follows the repo's own mechanism rather than the always-loaded files: a new
**path-scoped** `.claude/rules/local-models.md` (`internal/ai/ollama/**`,
`tools/launchd/dev.ollama.serve.plist`, the nightly-eval drivers) loads the budget rules exactly
when someone touches the local-model lane, and `dev-workflow.md` — which loads in *every* session
— keeps only a one-line pointer.

The repo plist had also drifted from the running config and was the **more** dangerous of the two:
it carried `OLLAMA_KEEP_ALIVE=-1` (models never unload) against the live `60m`, so a reinstall
would have restored the pre-incident config plus never-release. `tools/launchd/dev.ollama.serve.plist`
now matches the installed copy exactly.

New section **"Ollama Memory Budget on the Rig"** in `docs/docs/guides/debugging.md` records the
numbers, the `free` vs `available` vs `overhead` distinction, the sizing inequality
`weights + KV(context) < available`, and two standing traps: the rig has **no swap**
(`free_swap="0 B"`, `/private/var/vm` empty) so there is no thrash-and-notice phase, and the
kernel's own `memoryPressure` flag read **false** throughout — never key a guard to it.


### Fixed — the mission stall watchdog was killing live iterations, not wedged ones

Measured 2026-09-02: the stall watchdog killed **4 V1 and 3 world iterations in one day**. The
21:13 kill landed on a V1 slot that was at **Gate 5 committing its own iteration-321 record** —
609 transcript records, tool calls 8 seconds before the SIGKILL — which is why iteration 321 has
no record.

The predicate was a single arm: instantaneous `ps %cpu` summed over the process tree, "idle"
below 2%. The comment above it claimed that arm errs safe — *"a session doing real work reads
non-idle and is NOT flagged ... we miss late stalls, never kill live work"*. Sampled against a
LIVE controller whose transcript was growing 15-45KB per 30s, the same expression read
**0.10 / 0.30 / 0.80 / 1.40**. An agent spends its wall-clock blocked on the model API, not on
CPU, so the safety claim was exactly inverted — and it was a claim in a comment, which cannot red.

- **`_mc_stalled` now requires four arms to agree**, sample over sample, before it will kill: a
  descendant past `STALL_CHILD_AGE`, a flat progress counter, a flat gate heartbeat, and a tree
  under `STALL_CPU_PCT`. Any one of them moving resets the hit counter.
- **New `_mc_progress_bytes`** — the progress counter. For `claude` it is the session transcript
  (`~/.claude/projects/<cwd-slug>/*.jsonl`), the one signal a controller cannot forget to emit,
  since the harness appends to it on every assistant message and tool result. For `codex`/`pi` it
  is the driver log, which both stream into. The heartbeat cannot be the primary arm: it is
  stamped by the *agent* at gate boundaries, and the killed V1 slot reached Gate 5 having stamped
  only `gate-0`.
- **Fails open, loudly.** With no progress instrument the driver refuses to guess, logs a warning
  and disables the early kill for that attempt; `HARD_TIMEOUT` still bounds the slot.
- `STALL_SAMPLES` 3 → **5** (10 minutes of proven no-progress), new `MISSION_STALL_CPU_PCT` knob,
  and the kill line now names which arms were flat.
- **`tools/launchd/test_mission_stall.sh`** — 11 arms, extracted from the driver so the suite
  cannot drift green against an edited one. Five mutation drills, each restored byte-identical:
  reinstating the CPU-only predicate reds 5 arms, deleting the heartbeat arm reds 1, failing
  closed reds 1, dropping the `.`-to-`-` mapping in the cwd slug reds 2, and removing the
  heartbeat path derivation reds 1. Wired into `make test-launchd-drivers`.
- The **world** mission carried the identical defect in its own copy of the driver and had **zero
  driver coverage**; it gets the same predicate, the same suite, and a first
  `launchd drivers (bash 3.2)` CI job.

Still open, and not fixed here: the mission skill stops stamping gate heartbeats after `gate-0`
(V1 and docs both show `fired, gate-0, gate-0` for slots that ran to Gate 5), so the heartbeat arm
is weaker than designed. The transcript arm removes the dependence, but the stamping gap is real.

### Changed — CLAUDE.md principle 4: read the control before refusing on its behalf

Written from a measured failure on 2026-09-02. Asked to record an attended ruling with
`mission_answer.sh`, the agent refused on the grounds that a loop-authored ruling would be
indistinguishable from a human one — then spent an hour elsewhere before the decision still had
to be made. The guarantee it was defending did not exist: that script overrides the git identity
for *every* caller, and this rig's git identity is the fleet bot in Mark's own sessions too, so
the two commits are byte-identical. A second error compounded it — a rule addressed to the
*unattended loop* (mission-control's SKILL.md) was treated as a rule about an attended session
that happened to contain an agent.

- **CLAUDE.md** gains principle 4: if the artifact is identical whether you or the user performs
  the step, refusing buys no integrity. If a control is genuinely weak, say so and offer to fix
  it rather than simulating enforcement that is not there.
- **`mission_answer.sh`** documents the three traps that actually cost time: the answer must be
  ONE LINE (a ledger row is a single markdown table row — the validator catches this and refuses
  to commit, and it is not a false alarm); do not start the answer with `ANSWERED —` (the script
  prepends its own, and the first attempt recorded `ANSWERED — ANSWERED —`); and who may run it.
  Its `--help` now prints the whole header rather than truncating at line 32.
- **SKILL.md** rule (c) now states its scope: it binds the unattended loop resolving a row nobody
  ruled on, not an attended session recording a ruling Mark actually gave.

Also fixes a self-referential defect in the new personal-email gate: its own self-test embedded a
literal fixture address, so the gate went red on its own test file the moment that file became
tracked. The fixture is now assembled at runtime.

### Fixed — a personal email address was being published into public issue comments

`mission_answer.sh` stamped attended rulings with a hardcoded personal address, and the
mission-control provenance rule told the loop to *record* the commit author it checked. Since
every mission report pastes ledger rows verbatim into the public bookkeeping issue, that
address was being republished on each report — it had reached 11 occurrences across 9 tracked
files, all generated rather than typed.

Three changes, because the instances alone would have regrown:

- **The identity is now a GitHub noreply address** (`MISSION_ATTENDED_EMAIL` still overrides).
  It is attributable to the account and exposes nothing. The address was also simply wrong.
- **The provenance rule now says COMPARE, never RECORD.** The check was always negative — "is
  the author the fleet account?" — so the row only ever needed the verdict, `attended` or
  `fleet`, not an address.
- **`make check-no-personal-email`** refuses one in the loop-written surface, wired into `ci:`
  and ci.yml, with a 5-arm self-test. Scope is deliberately narrow — mission docs, `scripts/`,
  `.claude/skills/` — and it does NOT police `cmd/ailang/*.go` (an owner address there is
  functional access-control data), the governance/contact files, or `eval_results/` (banked
  evidence). Allowlists GitHub noreply, RFC 2606/6761 reserved placeholders, and machine
  identities such as GCP service accounts.

Note the limit: this stops future exposure and clears the working tree. Addresses already in
public git history are not rewritten — that history is shared and rewriting it is destructive.

### Changed — task ceilings raised to match the architecture they were built on (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M8)

Cloud Run Jobs were chosen for this system **because** they allow long runs — the platform
maximum is 24 hours. We were using fifteen minutes of it: 14 of 35 prod agents carried
`timeout: "15m"`, and on 2026-09-02 a real package task died on `pi exceeded hard timeout
(15m0s)` doing ordinary work. The ceiling was the problem, not the task.

Ratified by Mark (attended): **2h agents, 4h platform.**

- `coordinator.DefaultTaskTimeout` 30m → **2h**; new `MaxAgentTaskTimeout` (2h) and
  `PlatformTaskTimeout` (4h) constants.
- All 33 agents in `config.cloud.yaml` raised to `timeout: "2h"`.
- Terraform `agent_timeout` 3600s → **14400s**.
- **`idle_timeout` is deliberately untouched** (5–10m). It is the liveness guard — "kill if no
  events for this long" — and it is what makes a generous wall-clock safe: a hung agent still
  dies in minutes, while an agent making progress is no longer cut off for no reason.

**The ordering constraint is now asserted, not just documented.** Cloud Run's `taskTimeout` must
stay strictly above the highest agent wall-clock, with room to spare. If it is the lower of the
two, the platform kills the container before the executor's own timeout fires — so no completion
is published and the outcome is **lost rather than reported**, which is exactly the failure M7
removed, reachable again through configuration alone. The two values live in different repos and
nothing connected them; `TestAgentTimeoutStaysBelowThePlatformCeiling` now does, from the side
that can, and both sides carry a comment pointing at the other.

### Fixed — the coordinator cancelled live work every time it cold-started (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M7)

Found by the first live task on the prod plane. `RecoverStaleTasks` marks every running/queued
task older than five minutes `cancelled` on daemon startup. Its premise — *"the daemon restarted,
so anything still running must be dead"* — holds in **local** mode, where the daemon owns the
worker process, and is **false in cloud** mode, where the executor is a separate Cloud Run Job
with an independent lifecycle. The cloud coordinator scales to zero, so every cold start was
cancelling work that was still running:

```
12:33:57  Cloud dispatch: task-c8126248 → Cloud Run Job
12:49:05  stale task detector: stopped               ← coordinator scaled to zero
12:50:40  opened PR #56; completion published (status=completed)
12:50:45  Recovered 1 stale task(s) from previous daemon run
12:50:46  CompletionHandler: already in terminal state "cancelled", skipping
```

**The work succeeded — correct fix, pushed branch, open PR — and the requester was never told.**
That is worse than a failure, because a failure is visible.

- Startup recovery is now **local-mode only**. In cloud mode the stale-task detector already owns
  the question, and it ages a task from its own timeout rather than from the daemon's lifetime.
- An unset or unrecognised mode still recovers, like local: local is the documented default, and
  the asymmetry favours it — recovering when you should not have merely re-runs a task, while
  failing to recover a genuinely dead local task leaves it running forever.
- The split was **already documented** on `StaleTaskDetector` ("Only runs in cloud mode … Local
  mode uses `RecoverStaleTasks` at startup instead"). Nothing enforced it. Same shape as the rest
  of this milestone: a guard whose correctness argument names a premise that a later architecture
  change falsified.

### Fixed — the published OS leaderboard counted harness crashes as model failures

`internal/eval_harness/validity.go` has always drawn the distinction between "the
subject was measured and did badly" and "we failed to measure it", and its
`applyValidityBackstop` correctly marks unidentifiable failures (`api_error`)
invalid at bank time. But `SummarizeRotation` — the aggregator that writes
`summary.json` and from there the published OS/Local leaderboard — never
filtered on it. The consequence was listed in validity.go's own doc comment as a
known cost ("Published pass rates carried the crash rate inside them") and stayed
true.

Measured on the v0.34.0 rotation (2026-09-02): `motoko-local-qwen3-8-27b`
published **86.0%** on AILANG against pi's 92.7%, and was read as the weakest
AILANG harness. 11 of motoko's 12 AILANG failures were **zero-token non-starts** —
`agent_turns: null`, `total_tokens: 0`, the subject never ran. Causes were a
motoko startup crash (7), an ollama "maximum pending requests exceeded" (1), and
a transient `std/fs.ail` type error (1). Across all three harnesses **every**
`api_error` row had zero turns and zero tokens: in this dataset `api_error` is
never a model outcome.

With those rows excluded, motoko is **97.4%** on AILANG. Controlling for
benchmark coverage as well (valid rows, the 54-benchmark common set), the three
harnesses are indistinguishable — pi 98.7%, opencode 98.7%, motoko 97.4%, with 3
discordant benchmarks out of 54 that go in both directions. The entire reported
gap was harness crash rate plus uneven coverage.

- `BenchmarkSummary` gains `invalid_excluded` + `invalid_reasons`; invalid rows
  are quarantined at ingest and never enter a pass-rate denominator. Same
  "shrunken sample stated out loud" contract as `tokens_cache_unaccounted`.
- A tuple whose every row is invalid is **omitted** rather than published as 0/0,
  which renders as 0% — the phantom-failure shape validity.go exists to prevent.
  `RotationSummary` counts them in `unmeasured_tuples`.
- `ModelRollup.pass_at_1` inherits the fix (it derives from the summaries).
- `os-rotation-filler.sh` now escalates: when the non-measurement rate crosses
  `OS_FILLER_INVALID_ALERT_PCT` (default 5%) it files a harness-health report to
  the controlplane inbox. Excluding bad rows silently would turn a harness outage
  into a quietly smaller sample; it now has a voice.

### Removed — the Wednesday `ailang fmt` A/B (M-EVAL-FMT-WEAKMODEL-AB)

Both arms (`motoko-local-qwen3-6-fmt`, `motoko-local-qwen3-6-35b-a3b-mxfp8`)
pinned `qwen3.6:35b-a3b-mxfp8`, which is no longer pulled on the rig under the
single-on-device-LLM rule, so the experiment had stopped measuring anything. It
could not be repointed either: `motoko-local-qwen3-6-fmt` was the only
`ollama_fmt` row in models.yml, so there was no qwen3.8 treatment arm to swap in.
Reviving it means onboarding `motoko-local-qwen3-8-27b-fmt` and re-validating
that the treatment applies — new work, not a flag flip. Banked rows remain in
`docs/static/benchmarks/fmt_ab.jsonl` and the registry entry is retained so they
are not orphaned. Wednesday now runs the plain regression guard.

### Added — you can see what the fleet did without asking an agent to look (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M4)

The condition that decides whether a person *prefers* the message plane over an interactive
session is not correctness, it is legibility. Every number in this session's audit was assembled
by hand out of `gcloud run jobs executions list`, coordinator logs and Firestore. The inputs
already existed; nothing joined them.

- **`ailang messages activity [--hours N] [--json]`** — messages in the window by type, task
  outcomes (including `no_changes`, which is invisible in a plain listing), busiest inboxes and
  senders, unread count and the age of the oldest unread. Against prod it renders the 2026-08-31
  amplification incident in one screen: 1,115 messages to `docparse`, 1,040 `deduplicated`, 4
  `completed`. A completion whose payload will not parse is counted as `unparseable` rather than
  dropped — silently discarding the rows you cannot read is how a summary lies.
- **A dispatched message is now marked read.** In cloud mode the daemon called `MarkAsRead` on the
  Pub/Sub adapter, whose implementation is `return nil` — correct for the wire, but it never
  touched the Firestore row. So `messages health`'s "routable, but never dispatched" count, which
  it says should always be zero, **never could be**: on 2026-09-02 it flagged 7 messages, every
  one of which had been dispatched. Best-effort and loud — the task is already running by then, so
  a failed bookkeeping write must not fail the dispatch, but it says so and names the symptom.
- **`messages health` now names the PLANE, not just the project.** `ailang-multivac` and
  `ailang-multivac-dev` differ by a suffix and are entirely separate planes with their own
  Firestore, topic prefix, coordinator and executor jobs. An unrecognised project is left
  unlabelled rather than guessed, and a test asserts the label agrees with the topic-prefix
  mapping — if those two ever disagree, one of them is routing to the wrong plane.

### Fixed — `messages send` could deliver to an inbox nobody addressed, and report success (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M5)

`normalizeArgsForFlags` decided whether the next token was a flag's value with
`!strings.HasPrefix(next, "-")`. The intent was to avoid swallowing a following flag; the effect
was to reject any legitimate value beginning with a dash, drop it into the positional list, and
shift every later token. Measured 2026-09-02:

```
ailang messages send diag-argparse "body" --title "--help is inconsistent" --from "diag-sender"
```

delivered the message to inbox **`diag-sender`**, set `from_agent` to the `cli` default, set the
title to the literal string `--from` — and printed `✓ Message sent`. Found by accident while
filing a bug report about `--help`, whose *title* began with `--help`.

- **The flag set is now read from the `*flag.FlagSet` itself**, not a hand-maintained list of
  names each caller repeated. A list that must track a FlagSet drifts — and it could not
  distinguish a boolean flag from one taking a value, which was the second, latent half of the
  bug: **a bool flag placed before the positionals swallowed the inbox.**
- Whether the next token is a value is decided by the **flag**, not by the token's first
  character. Dash-leading values survive; bool flags consume nothing.
- **A value flag with nothing to consume is now an error**, not a silent shift. Routing must never
  be a side effect of a parse that half-failed.
- `ailang exec` used the same function and had the same two defects; both callers are fixed, and
  `routingFlagNames()` is deleted — its stated purpose ("so callers don't have to repeat the
  list") is served by the FlagSet.
- Five arms, RED first, including the verbatim repro. **This function had no tests at all.**

### Fixed — every pi executor task on the AILANG repo died before turn 1 (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M6)

`ailang pi install` materialises the extension suite into `~/.pi/agent/extensions/` — its own help
text says *"global — every repo on this machine"* — and the AILANG repo also ships
`.pi/extensions/` with the same files. pi saw two registrations of `ailang_check` and exited 1
**before turn 1**:

```
Failed to load extension ".../ailang-lsp-lite.ts": Tool "ailang_check" conflicts with
/workspace/task-X/.pi/extensions/ailang-lsp-lite.ts
```

Measured on the dev plane 2026-09-02: `task-6825b3fc` and `task-48a365bb` (workspace
`sunholo-data/ailang`) both died this way; `task-f8213acd` (a package repo) did not. It took out
`design-doc-creator`, `sprint-planner`, `sprint-evaluator` and `coordinator` on the pi provider —
**pre-existing, and invisible because prod pi tasks all target package repos.**

- `cmd/ailang/pi_extension_collision.go` (new): on a name clash the **workspace copy wins**. A repo
  that ships its own suite is stating a preference, and honouring it is strictly more correct than
  failing. Deliberately not a special case for the AILANG repo — every package repo that later adds
  `.pi/extensions/` would hit the same wall.
- A repo with no `.pi/extensions/` is untouched, so the gate still applies everywhere.
- Missing directories are benign: this runs on every dispatch, and failing hard on the normal case
  would take out the fleet.
- The arm is that a pi run whose workspace is the AILANG repo reaches turn 1. **No such test
  existed**, which is exactly why four agents were dead on this provider with nobody knowing.
### Fixed — `t.Setenv("HOME")` never redirected `os.UserHomeDir()` on Windows, and the private fix had been written three times

Both Windows CI jobs were red on four consecutive `dev` commits: four arms across two packages
(`TestResolveAnthropicCredential_FallsBackToClaudeCredentialsFile`'s two subtests in `internal/ai`,
`TestStandardModeCostProvenance_CredentialFileIsSubscription` in `internal/eval_harness`). Each
wrote a credential fixture under `t.TempDir()` and pointed at it with `t.Setenv("HOME", dir)`.
`os.UserHomeDir()` reads **USERPROFILE** on windows and `$home` on plan9 — verified against
GOROOT's own source, which consults exactly those three and errors when the chosen one is empty —
so on the runner the fixture was invisible, the real profile resolved, and the arm failed for the
platform rather than for the code. The production resolver was correct throughout.

- **`internal/testutil.SetHomeDir` is now the one implementation**, setting all three variables and
  preserving the empty-string semantic (`os.UserHomeDir()` must FAIL) that `cmd/ailang` already
  relied on. All 16 bare `t.Setenv("HOME"` sites are converted and the **three** private duplicates
  are deleted — `setHomeDir` in `cmd/ailang`, `setHomeDirForTest` in `internal/effects`, and an
  inline GOOS-branched pair covering 2 of `internal/executor`'s 6 sites. Each of those was a
  correct local fix, and the next call site went red anyway: guard the helper, miss the call site.
- **`make check-home-isolation` is the gate**, so instance five reds locally instead of on a
  Windows runner. Modelled on `check-git-exec`/`check-tmpfile-hygiene`: anti-vacuity floors on
  BOTH enumerations (a positive fixture carrying every shape the matcher claims, and a non-zero
  `_test.go` count with the scan root asserted to exist), `INSTRUMENT BROKEN` exit 2 kept distinct
  from a violation's exit 1, a live-asserted exemption list, and an honest RESIDUAL CLASSES line.
  Wired into `make/code-health.mk`, `make/ci.mk`'s `ci:` target **and** `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
  — `ci.yml` records that `make ci` is a local aggregate CI never invokes, so the makefile alone
  would not have run it.
- **The matcher is whitespace-normalised and covers `os.Setenv` too.** The independent evaluator
  broke the line-oriented first draft with `t.Setenv(\n\t"HOME",\n\tdir,\n)` — gofmt-canonical,
  so reachable by accident — and found a live unswept instance the first sweep had missed:
  four `os.Setenv("HOME", …)` sites in `cmd/ailang/pkg_lock_ratchet_test.go`, whose code under test
  reaches `os.UserHomeDir()` through `internal/messaging/config.go`. Both are fixed here.
  `internal/loader/stdlib_resolver_test.go` is the one exemption, because the code it drives reads
  `$HOME`/`$APPDATA` directly per GOOS and never through `os.UserHomeDir()`.


### Added — the coordinator gets the fallback chain the mission loops already had (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M3)

`ResolveModel` returned `chain[0]` and discarded the rest, justified by a comment saying retry
chains were *"a driver concern"* — true when the mission driver was the only dispatcher, false
since the coordinator began dispatching unattended. Measured casualty: `task-c429f9e0` died on
`pi idle for 3m0.006592232s mid-generation (no output)` with no second lane.

- **`internal/coordinator/retry_chain.go` (new)**: `ResolveModelChain` returns every model an
  agent may run, in order; `ResolveModel` is now its head, so the two cannot drift.
  `TestChainHeadMatchesResolveModel` is the arm — a drifted head would silently re-route every
  existing agent.
- **Two-tier retry, matched to the failure taxonomy.** `ClassifyFailure` separates transport
  failures (idle stall, 429, provider 5xx, deadline, zero bytes) from model-class outcomes (a
  refusal, code that does not compile). Transport advances the chain in-container at no extra
  cost; a model-class outcome does not, because a second lane produces a second opinion rather
  than a fix. Signatures are **named patterns, not loose substrings** — a bare `"429"` match
  would retry into a spent quota bucket *and* fire on `"benchmark run 429 of 500"`, which is an
  explicit test case.
- **The infra tier has an owner and durable state.** A task that times out without ever
  publishing a completion is infra-class (container died, OOM, preemption) — in-container retry
  is impossible there by definition. The **stale-task detector is the sole re-dispatcher**; three
  other components can already move a task toward terminal, and any second one gaining this power
  would breach the cap nondeterministically. `AttemptCount` and `ChainLinkIndex` are persisted on
  the task row, because the coordinator scales to zero and a cap a restart forgets is not a cap.
  Hard limit: 2 Cloud Run executions per task.
- **A losing compare-and-set is loud and still terminal.** Deliberately not a lock: a silent loser
  is indistinguishable from a component that never ran.
- **An unknowable task age never re-dispatches.** That refusal (from `e0b12bf5f`, after
  `time.Since(zero)` ≈ 292 years marked tasks timed-out ~57s after dispatch and fed a
  591-message amplification loop) is a precondition of this tier, and now has its own arm.
- `TaskCompletion` records `ModelUsed` and `ChainLinkIndex`: without them a retry is
  unattributable and per-task cost is wrong for exactly the runs that changed lane.
- Fixed a stale citation in `model_resolution.go` that named a test which has never existed
  (`TestCloudAgents_ResolveIdenticallyWithoutModelRouting`); the real guard is
  `TestCloudAgents_RegistryMatchesTheDeletedRoutingTable`.

**Operator step still owed (D6):** the 35 cloud agents all carry an explicit `model:` pin, so the
roles table never fires. Until those become `role:` in `config.yaml`, M3 is correct and dormant.

### Fixed — commits stopped stranding on local dev (Stop-hook fast-forward auto-push)

Attended sessions are told to commit straight to `dev` in the main checkout and never to
branch there, but nothing in that workflow pushed, and mission-control Gate 1 forbids the
loop from pulling or pushing the shared tree (it may hold a sibling's uncommitted work) —
so it could not clean up either. Commits accumulated on local dev indefinitely. Measured
2026-09-02: **25 stranded commits** in the main checkout (one session's standalone work
plus several days of attended commits, including two model registrations and a hook fix),
and the sibling clone `ailang-docs` sitting **58 behind with 1 stranded**. The loop's own
worktree → PR pushes were never implicated — 0 push failures across every mission log.

New `scripts/hooks/push_dev_on_stop.sh`, wired as a `Stop` hook: when a session ends on
`dev` and the branch is ahead of origin **and not behind**, it pushes — a plain
fast-forward. Ahead *and* behind is refused loudly and left to a human, because a merge
here needs judgement: the 2026-09-02 sync conflicted on the V1 charter, where a careless
resolution drops decision rows (the skill records one that ate `decision-ledger:end` and an
entire `## Goal` block). It never touches the working tree — no pull, reset, stash,
checkout or branch — always exits 0 so a sync problem cannot stop a session ending, and
bounds every git call with the `perl alarm` pattern this repo already uses (macOS ships no
`timeout`). Opt out with `AILANG_AUTOPUSH=0`.

A fetch failure is **loud, not a silent skip** — the first cut bounded fetch at 10s and
logged a silent `skip` on timeout, which live use hit within minutes (this repo carries ~55
worktrees and a cold fetch is not fast). Silently skipping re-opens the exact hole the hook
closes, so fetch is now 20s retried once, and a persistent failure prints how far ahead
local dev is and tells you to push by hand.

`scripts/hooks/test_push_dev_on_stop.sh` covers 10 arms against a real bare origin: clean
no-op, ahead-only pushes, diverged refuses (with a non-vacuous setup assertion that the
repo really is 1 ahead / 1 behind), non-`dev` branch, merge-in-flight, and the opt-out.

### Added — a no-op task says so instead of reporting success (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M2)

`completed` meant "the executor exited 0", which is not "work landed" and could not be told apart
from it. New terminal status `no_changes`, chosen over a boolean on `completed` because of which
way an old consumer is wrong: an unfamiliar status reads as *not* completed, i.e. not-success —
it surfaces the problem, where a boolean would have been counted as success by exactly the
queries that matter.

- **`internal/coordinator/task_status.go` (new)**: `TaskStatusNoChanges`, `AllTaskStatuses()`,
  `IsTerminalStatus()`, `TerminalStatuses()`, `ClassifyCompletionStatus()`. Terminality and the
  observatory mapping are now **tables the tests iterate**, not hand-written lists — the design
  doc's own first-draft enumeration of consumers was wrong in kind, and any such list goes stale
  the moment someone adds a status.
- **Three consumers were each wrong in their own way, and all three are fixed:**
  `pubsub_completion_handler.go` decided terminality with a literal string compare
  (`== "completed" || "failed" || "cancelled"`), so `no_changes` would not have registered as
  terminal and the task could have been re-processed; `observatory_sync.go` had a
  `default: pending` arm that silently reported unmapped statuses as still-running — and
  `TaskStatusDuplicate` was *already* falling through it; `daemon_tasks_worktrees.go` kept its own
  terminal list that had drifted, omitting `duplicate`, so those worktrees leaked. The worktree
  sweep now derives its set from the same table.
- **The expectation is trusted metadata, not message content.** `AgentConfig.AcknowledgeOnly`
  flows through `DispatchParams` to `AILANG_ACKNOWLEDGE_ONLY`. M2's first draft used the
  content-derived `TaskType` and inherited exactly the hole M1a was rewritten to remove — a sender
  writing "fix" would have classified their own task's expectations.
- **The field is named for the fail-loud direction.** It is `AcknowledgeOnly`, not
  `ExpectChanges`, so the Go zero value and an unset/malformed env var both mean "changes were
  expected". A dispatcher that forgets the field reports a no-diff run loudly instead of silently
  inheriting the lenient behaviour this milestone removes.
- The acknowledge-only path added 2026-08-26 for probe tasks is unchanged: those still complete
  cleanly, create no branch, and are not errors.

### Added — the executor permission gate gets a floor, and the tier stops being sender-controlled (M-COORDINATOR-EXECUTION-TRUST M1a)

Cloud tasks have been dispatching correctly for days and producing nothing: 1,249 completion
records, 16 marked `completed`, and 6 that ever changed a file. The cause is ours. The
session-protocol gate is installed globally into the pi executor image (`Dockerfile.agent-pi` →
`ailang pi install` → `/home/ailang/.pi`), so it arms in **every** workspace — including repos
with no `.pi/` of their own — and the only disarm was a `session_protocol_ack` tool call the
pinned executor model never makes. On `task-4e415b46` the model satisfied both prerequisites in
three turns and then sat blocked for twelve minutes. The gate had all the evidence it needed and
asked for a password anyway.

- **`internal/coordinator/work_tier.go` (new)**: `WorkTier` closed enum + `ResolveWorkTier`.
  Tier 1 (routine) unlocks mutation once the prerequisite floor is met; tier 2
  (feature/semantics) has no auto-path. **The tier is read from the agent registry, never from
  message content** — `Task.Type` is on the record but is computed by `classifyTaskType()`, a
  substring match whose bug-fix branch is tested first against
  `{bug, fix, error, crash, broken, issue, problem, fail, wrong}`, so a sender writing "please
  fix this" would have picked their own permission tier. Fail-closed at every boundary: nil
  agent, unset, and any unrecognised value all resolve to tier 2.
- **Tier 1 is refused outright when `AILANG_PUSH_BRANCH` is set.** That path commits straight to
  the branch with no coordinator branch and no PR, so the always-PR containment this design
  leaned on does not exist for it.
- **`session-protocol-gate.ts`**: `shouldBlock` takes a `GateContext`; anything that is not
  exactly `tier1` is tier 2, so an absent, malformed or injected value fails closed. Two built-in
  prerequisite sets — a **generic floor** satisfiable with no `CLAUDE.md` (so a package repo the
  executor has never seen can pass it) and the **AILANG set**, which is the floor *plus* a
  `CLAUDE.md` read. Selection is by coordinator-supplied workspace identity, never by a file in
  the clone; no repo can elect which set governs it. The interactive human-confirm path is
  untouched.
- **`make test` now runs the pi extension suite** (`make test-pi-extensions`). Those 83 tests —
  including every arm above — had never been run by `make` or CI. An arm that cannot fail is not
  a guard, which is the thesis this whole milestone rests on.
- Three brittle count-based assertions replaced with content assertions: they flagged correct
  additions as regressions (`missing.length` 2→3, env var counts 7→8 and 9→10) and named nothing
  when they failed.

Design: [m-coordinator-execution-trust.md](../design_docs/planned/m-coordinator-execution-trust.md).
The general per-repo manifest system is deliberately **not** here — it is
[m-package-protocol-manifests.md](../design_docs/planned/m-package-protocol-manifests.md), split
out after three quorum rounds put every unresolved objection there.

### Fixed — managed-agents cost overstated by ~43%: the API reports cache reads, we ignored them

`internal/executor/managed_agents` billed every input token at the fresh $1.50/M rate because
`managed_agents.go` asserted `CacheReadInputTokens: 0, // Not reported by Managed Agents API`.
The API does report it. Live-probed 2026-09-02: `total_input_tokens` 13,107 of which
`total_cached_tokens` **5,350 (41%)** — and cached Gemini input bills at roughly a tenth of fresh.

Third instance of the same failure mode in one session, after the OAuth comment (`a52ea058f`) and
the Anthropic thinking-token comment (`f53e8e17c`): **a frozen claim about a third party's API,
true when written, false later, with no test holding it to account.**

- `Usage` now parses `total_cached_tokens` / `cached_tokens_by_modality`, plus a
  `FreshInputTokens()` helper.
- `CostModel` gains `CacheReadCost: 0.00015` ($0.15/M).
- The cost call passes **fresh** input, not the cache-inclusive total —
  `CalculateCost` adds cache-read cost *on top of* input cost, so passing the total would bill
  cached tokens at both rates. A test pins that specific double-billing.

Measured on the captured interaction: **$0.024061 → $0.016839, a 42.9% overstatement.** This is
why the `gemini-3-5-flash` row records managed-agents runs at "~$1.79/benchmark, ~6× over budget"
— that figure was computed with cache reads billed at full price *and* under a since-falsified
"caching disabled by Google API limitation" premise. The lane is materially cheaper than recorded.

Thought tokens remain correctly disjoint from `output_tokens` and billed at the output rate; a
test pins that dropping them would understate cost.

### Added — standard-mode Anthropic evals can run on a Claude subscription (OAuth), not just a metered key

Standard mode previously had exactly one Anthropic auth path: `x-api-key`, i.e. the metered API.
Agent mode has run on subscription OAuth since way back (49 banked `list-price-equivalent` stages,
all `eval-agent:*`), but `eval-suite --models claude-*` could only ever bill per token. Set
`ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` to an OAuth access token and standard runs now draw on the subscription.

- `internal/ai/anthropic/auth.go` (new): `AuthMode` + `WithOAuth()`. `AuthOAuth` sends
  `Authorization: Bearer <token>` **and** `anthropic-beta: oauth-2025-04-20` (the token is
  rejected without the beta flag); `AuthAPIKey` keeps `x-api-key` and is still the default, so
  nothing changes for existing metered setups.
- **All three request paths now share one `applyAuthHeaders`.** `client.go`, `step.go`, and
  `streamstep.go` each set auth inline before this, so a second mode would have meant remembering
  three places — and the missed ones fail as a 401 on a path with no test. `x-api-key` is now
  written in exactly one file.
