reaching loop, so `expires: "not-a-date"` rode out as `[allowlisted]` at rc=0.

Module-level findings now report as `[NOT allowlisted]`, `[allowlisted]`, or
`[allowlisted, EXPIRED <date>]`, sharing one `classifyEntry` helper and one clock with the reaching
path so the two cannot drift on the boundary. A malformed module-only expiry is reported and exits
2 before any success output. Per `#703`'s ratified decision these findings remain **non-gating**:
an expired module-only entry is surfaced and the process still exits 0.

The exit-code decision moved out of `main` into a testable `decide`, because the non-gating
invariant cannot be pinned through the per-class helpers — they are never handed module-only IDs.
An earlier version of the test asserted `classifyReaching(nil, …)` returned empty, which passes for
any implementation; measured, it stayed green under a mutant that added **every** finding to the
blocking set. The replacement arms red on that mutant.

### Fixed — the `ailang.toml` rollback net gated on validity, so it never engaged for a parseable-but-invalid manifest

`ailang install` writes its dependency line by scanning `ailang.toml` as text, and
`writeManifestChecked` is the net that bounds that scanner's open-ended blind spots: it re-parses
after every write and rolls back anything that would leave the manifest less parseable than it
found it.

Its "was this parseable before?" probe was `pkg.LoadManifest` — TOML parse **plus full semantic
`Validate`**. So a manifest that is perfectly good TOML but fails validation (missing `edition`, a
one-level `name`, missing `version`) counted as *already broken*, the post-write re-check was
skipped, and the call returned nil. Measured on two fixtures differing by exactly one line
(`edition = "1"`): without it, a duplicate dependency key was **written to disk** and the command
printed `✓ Added`; with it, the write was refused and rolled back. A hand-written manifest missing
`edition` is exactly the sort of project where someone runs `ailang install`, so `#720`'s framing —
that none of the known scanner gaps ships corruption — did not hold for that whole class.

Fixed by adding `pkg.ParseManifestFile` (read + `toml.Unmarshal`, no `Validate`) and pointing both
probes at it. The net now engages for every manifest that **parses**.

Three scanner gaps closed alongside, all reproduced first-party against controls that replaced
cleanly:

- `openMultilineString` counted `"""` occurrences, so `notes = 'contains """ triple double
  quotes'` read as an open multi-line string and the scanner skipped the real `[dependencies]`
  section, appending a second table. It now walks the line tracking single-line basic/literal
  string state and only recognises a delimiter outside one.
- A quoted table header `["dependencies"]` — semantically identical to `[dependencies]` — was
  unrecognised. `tableHeaderName` now accepts bare, basic-quoted and literal-quoted single-segment
  headers, and deliberately refuses `[[dependencies]]` and dotted headers.
- `stripLineComment` had no escape awareness, so `"a\"#b" = "1.0"` truncated at the `#`.

Appending to a manifest that did **not** parse beforehand no longer prints an unqualified
`✓ Added`; it warns, names the file and the parse error, and leaves the exit status unchanged. The
test helper `countDependencyKey` — which exists to be an independent control — had grown the same
blind spots as the code under test and was repaired without calling the production helpers.

Guarded by a twelve-shape corpus test that runs the real install path twice per shape and asserts
the manifest still parses with exactly one dependency key and one `[dependencies]` table, with an
anti-vacuity floor so the enumeration cannot silently shrink.


### Fixed — the SessionStart hook held its own stdout open, refusing 1 in 4 mission fires

`scripts/hooks/session_start.sh` ended with `ailang docs embed-warmup --quiet --timeout 3m &`,
commented "(non-blocking)". That is true of the *script* and false of every *consumer that
captures its stdout*: a backgrounded child inherits the stdout descriptor, so a `$(...)`-style
capture cannot see EOF until the **child** exits, however promptly the script itself `exit 0`s.
Claude Code captures hook stdout — that is how the banner reaches a session — so the hook was
held open for as long as the warmup ran, bounded only by the warmup's own `--timeout 3m` (180s)
against the mission driver's 120s per-model probe cap.

That is the whole of the fleet's long-standing *"probe timed out after 120s — captured output:
`''`"* refusal: empty because `claude -p` emits nothing until it completes. Every model in the
preference list fails identically, so the driver concludes `NO usable model in prefs … Refusing`
and burns the fire. **`quota-limited` had never once fired** in either driver log, so these
refusals were never about quota.

Measured 2026-08-14: capture arm **8,433ms** vs redirected arm **8ms** vs **237ms** with the
background child removed; one real hook capture took **96,377ms** against a warm-cache
`embed-warmup` of ~1.2s. Refusals per fire: v1 **47/186**, motoko **6/11**, and world — the one
mission whose checkout has no `.claude/settings.json`, hence no hooks at all — **0/89**.
Each timed-out probe also *orphans* its warmup (verified reparented to `ppid=1`), so the next
probe adds another GPU tenant: up to six per fire.

Fixed by redirecting the child (`>/dev/null 2>&1 &`). Guarded by
`tools/launchd/test_hook_stdout.sh`, wired into `make test-launchd-drivers` (already a CI gate),
which stubs a slow warmup and asserts the captured hook still returns promptly — verified to go
red when the redirect is removed, with a control asserting the hook actually reaches the warmup
line (`session_start.sh` early-returns on the no-unread-messages path, which made the first draft
of the test pass against the mutant).
### Fixed — package dependency upserts and manifest parse diagnostics (#718)

Repeated `ailang install` and duplicate `init package --deps` declarations now
replace the existing `[dependencies]` entry in place instead of emitting duplicate
TOML keys. The writer handles a header carrying a trailing comment, a header that
is the file's last line, keys inside multi-line strings, literal-quoted keys, and
CRLF manifests; because it edits TOML as text, it also re-parses afterwards and
rolls the write back rather than leaving a manifest less parseable than it found
it. Package compilation additionally surfaces malformed `ailang.toml` and existing
malformed `ailang.lock` files with their paths and underlying parse errors; bare
projects and packages that have not generated a lock file retain legacy behavior.

### Fixed — clean handler exits now complete successfully (#706)

`exit(0)` from serve-api routes, A2A tasks, and MCP tools now produces each
protocol's existing unit-shaped success response. Non-zero exits remain errors
with their existing response shapes and messages.

### Fixed — the motoko mission loop had no recovery watcher, so a refused fire cost 12 hours

`mission-recovery.sh` has been mission-parameterized since it was written (#7d8db911f) — every
path derives from `MISSION_NAME`, and `TARGET_LABEL` falls out of it. But only **one** instance
was ever installed, and its plist pins `MISSION_NAME=v1`. So `dev.ailang.mission-motoko` wrote
its `.blocked` marker into a directory nothing was watching.

Measured 2026-08-14: `~/.ailang/state/mission-motoko.blocked` had sat untouched for **26711s
(7.4h)** while `/tmp/ailang-mission-recovery.log` showed an unbroken run of `mission v1 blocked`
lines. Three of the six motoko fires from 08-12 to 08-14 refused — 08-13 00:13 and 08-14 01:07 on
the probe stall, 08-12 09:13 on the trust dialog.

**Recovery matters more here than for v1, not less.** `mission-motoko`'s `StartInterval` is
43200s (12h) against `mission-control`'s 5400s (90m), so one refused fire costs a full half-day
and the loop silently halves its own cadence — 1 iteration/day against a scheduled 2. The stall
itself is transient and fleet-wide, not a motoko fault: v1 hit the identical empty-output probe
timeout at 05:34 on 08-14 and was green by 05:54, *because something kickstarted it*.

Fix is the missing plist only — `dev.ailang.mission-recovery-motoko.plist`, `MISSION_NAME=motoko`,
240s poll, 20-min cooldown, `RunAtLoad=false`. No script change was needed or made.

Verified live rather than by inspection: the first poll logged
`mission motoko blocked for 26711s — kickstarting dev.ailang.mission-motoko`, the mission
restarted (pid 70407), and its pin line confirmed the retry ran current code. A refused motoko
fire now costs ~20 minutes instead of 12 hours.

Two residuals, both declared rather than left implicit. The script's `SELF_KILL_SWITCH`
(`mission-recovery.disabled`) is **not** namespaced, so it silences both watchers; the per-mission
switch (`mission-motoko.disabled`) is the one that stops just this loop, and it is checked first
and still wins. And the two jobs share `/tmp/ailang-mission-recovery.log` — lines carry the
mission name, so this is legible, but it is a shared append target.
### Added — opt-in remote read for the `chains` view commands (#698 part 1, `D-15`)

`ailang chains` has always read the LOCAL SQLite observatory, while the *write* half has been able
to dual-write to a remote one since `#697`. That asymmetry made the design's own Primary Goal —
following a whole mission loop across providers — unreachable by any shipped tool.

`openChainsReadBackend` (`cmd/ailang/chains_read_backend.go`) closes it for the `Backend`-shaped
read commands, mirroring the write half's three-part shape: an explicit `--remote <mode>` beats
`AILANG_CHAINS_READ`, which beats the local default. **Local remains the default** — a caller that
passes nothing sees no change. 12 helper signatures widened from `*observatory.SQLiteBackend` to
`observatory.Backend`, and 13 read sites moved onto the resolver.

### Changed — the surfaces that cannot go remote now say so out loud

Scope is deliberately narrowed to `view` (Mark's ruling `D-15`, 2026-08-14): the ratified freeze
item said *every* `chains`/`eval-*` consumer would inherit the option, and the code refuted that
for the `eval-*` half. Firestore stores `eval_assessment` as an opaque JSON string, so
`QueryEvalResults`' six `json_extract` filters cannot be served remotely without a schema change —
a porting problem, not the wiring problem the doc described.

So, per Critical Principle 2 (no silent fallbacks), each of these **errors** rather than quietly
reading local when remote is requested:

- `chains live` (raw SQL via `Store().DB()`), `chains journey` and `chains find --task`
  (`GetChainJourney` / `GetTaskSpanSummary` are `*SQLiteBackend`-only), and
  `chains stats --cost-per-verified-success` (`Store()` is not on `Backend`).
- All 13 `eval-*` commands plus `eval-chains` subcommands, naming `D-15` and `#698` part 1 so the
  first person who wants remote eval read produces a **dated, attributable signal** instead of an
  assumed requirement. Deliberate asymmetry: an explicit `--remote` is a hard error, while merely
  having `AILANG_CHAINS_READ` exported produces a loud warning and proceeds locally — hard-failing
  the env would make the variable unexportable.

### Fixed — Firestore `GetMissionRollups` returned `nil, nil` instead of refusing

It was the only such stub in `internal/storage/firestore/observatory_chains.go` (26 methods), inert
only because nothing could reach it remotely. The moment `chains stats --by-mission` accepted
`--remote gcp` it would have printed "no missions" for a store it never queried — a Critical
Principle 2 violation **this change itself would have created**, so this change closes it.

### Fixed — `exit()` in an embedded module no longer panics the host (#691)

`exit()` raises a `*eval.EvalExitCode` sentinel panic (`internal/effects/io.go:145`). The CLI's
two consumers recover it — the single-file path (`cmd/ailang/main_run_exec.go`) and the batch
path (`recoverBatchItemExit`, added by #607). `internal/embed`, the sanctioned host → compiler
bridge, did **not**: `Engine.Call` and `Engine.CallPreserveFloats` called
`runtime.CallEntrypoint` directly, so an embedded module calling `exit()` took the **host
process** down with a raw Go stack. For a long-lived host (the dashboard/observatory) that is a
crash, not an exit code. Reproduced first-party before the fix, frame-for-frame as filed.

This is the same defect class as #607, one layer down — the repo's recurring
guard-the-helper-miss-the-call-site shape. `runtime.CallEntrypoint` itself is deliberately left
panic-based: the CLI *needs* the sentinel to reach its own recover so it can map onto `os.Exit`,
so the shared primitive stays neutral and each consumer supplies its own contract.

**Contract decision.** An embedding host has no process exit to map onto, so `exit(N)` is now
surfaced as a typed `*embed.ExitError{Code: N}` from `Call`, `CallPreserveFloats` and
(transitively) `CallJSON`. `exit(0)` is reported as an `ExitError` too, **not** as a nil error —
unlike the batch runner, which owns a process and can treat `exit(0)` as success. A nil error
here would be indistinguishable from a function that returned unit normally, discarding
information the caller cannot recover; hosts wanting batch semantics branch on `Code == 0`.

Two residuals are declared in code rather than left implicit: `Engine.Eval` is UNINFORMATIVE
(GAP-5 makes standalone expressions unreachable — its existing tests are skipped), and
`GetCallValue`/`GetCallValueN` hand the evaluator's own handles to the host unwrapped, which is
harmless for the in-evaluator use (the panic unwinds into the enclosing `Call`) but not for a
host that invokes them outside one.

Guarded by 8 arms in `internal/embed/exit_test.go`, both call sites pinned separately. Mutation
drill, one neutering mutation per branch, each asserted LANDED (sha256) and BUILDS (`go build`
rc=0): the exit-mapping, re-panic and passthrough branches each red only their own arms, and
unwiring either call site leaves the *other* site's arm passing. Inverse arm: with both guards
removed and the new tests deleted, `internal/embed` is **rc=0 / 60 PASS / 0 FAIL** — the defect
shipped entirely undetected by the existing suite.

### Security — project toolchain go 1.26.5 → 1.26.6 (7 stdlib advisories)

The `govulncheck` gate went red on `dev` with **7 unallowlisted findings**, all of them Go
**standard library** advisories and all reporting the same `fixed` version, `1.26.6`:

| ID | CVE | Package |
|---|---|---|
| GO-2026-5026 | CVE-2026-39821 | `net/http` (x/net/idna punycode) |
| GO-2026-5972 | CVE-2026-33818 | `encoding/asn1` |
| GO-2026-6088 | CVE-2026-56859 | `encoding/xml` |
| GO-2026-6089 | CVE-2026-56853 | `net/http` |
| GO-2026-6090 | CVE-2026-56862 | `crypto/tls` |
| GO-2026-6091 | CVE-2026-56858 | `html/template` |
| GO-2026-6218 | CVE-2026-56860 | `net/url` |

Every one is reported **reachable** from AILANG's own call graph (`net/http.Do`,
`crypto/tls.Handshake`, `net/url.Parse`, `encoding/xml.Token`, …), so this is a real upgrade
rather than an allowlist case — no `.govulncheck-allow.yml` entry was added. The bump in fact
clears an **eighth** stdlib advisory the gate never counted, `GO-2026-5942` (SVCB/HTTPS RR parse
panic in `x/net/dns/dnsmessage`, also `fixed: 1.26.6`); it is invisible to the "7" because it
reaches no traced function — see the caveat below.

Bumped the toolchain at **16 patch-pinned sites**: the `go` directive in `go.mod`, all **14**
workflow `go-version` pins (`build.yml`, `ci.yml` ×6, `release.yml` ×4, `docusaurus-deploy.yml`,
`eval-weekly.yml`, `test-game-codegen.yml`), and the hardcoded `go 1.26.5` in the external-module
fixture at `serveapi/serveapi_external_test.go:95`. "16 sites" counts *patch pins* only — the 8
`docker/Dockerfile.*` files reference the floating `golang:1.26-bookworm` tag, which tracks the
latest patch on its own and needs no edit.

The fixture edit is **drift hygiene, not a required fix**, and an earlier draft of this entry
overstated it. Measured: reverting only that literal, with the active toolchain at 1.26.6
(what `actions/setup-go` installs), leaves the test `rc=0` — outcome-identical to the unmutated
arm. The `requires go >= X` failure is real but needs the *active* toolchain to be below the
requirement, which cannot happen here because all 14 workflow pins move in lockstep with
`go.mod`. It is still worth doing: the literal was predicted to drift in
[#586](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/586) (NB-3), and this is that prediction
coming true.

Measured before and after on the same tree: `govulncheck -format json ./... | govulncheck-filter`
→ **7 unallowlisted, rc=1** on 1.26.5, and **0 unallowlisted / 8 allowlisted-and-unexpired,
rc=0** on 1.26.6. The 8 that remain are the pre-existing Ollama entries, unchanged, none expiring
before 2026-10-29.

⚠ **Caveat found while verifying this, filed separately:** `govulncheck-filter` only considers
findings that reach a traced *function*, so a module-level finding is reported in **neither** the
unallowlisted list nor the allowlisted count — it is silently dropped. Four are being dropped
today, and one of them, `GO-2026-5750` (`CVE-2026-7020`, Ollama path traversal, no upstream fix),
is a real published advisory that is **not** in the allowlist. Unchanged by this PR and present
identically before it.

### Tests — telemetry remote-write guard coverage

Added deterministic mutation-killing coverage for pinned stage IDs and for four reachable
remote-write validation branches. The fifth reported branch, Firestore eval-assessment marshal
failure, remains explicitly unreachable while `EvalAssessment` contains only JSON-marshalable
scalar fields; a type guard now makes any future violation visible.

### Added — mission iterations can dual-write to a remote observatory

`ailang chains post-iteration` wrote to one hardcoded local SQLite store. A node that sets
`AILANG_CHAINS_CLOUD=gcp` (or passes `--cloud gcp`) now writes each iteration to its local store
**and** to a remote one. The node is a parameter throughout: nothing names a particular machine,
and with the variable unset behaviour is exactly what it was.

- **Same identity in both stores.** `ChainCreateRequest`/`StageCreateRequest` accept an optional
  `ID`, and the first write backfills the post with the ids it used, so the remote copy carries the
  same chain and stage ids. Without that the remote record is an island — a span carrying the local
  chain id could never join it.
- **Never blocks.** An unreachable or misconfigured remote is a loud stderr notice plus a buffered
  retry, and the command still exits 0. Verified against a real broken config (`gcp` with no
  project), not by reading the code.
- **Each target keeps its own bounded spool.** Sharing one would let a long cloud outage evict
  local posts that were only waiting on a locked database. Both keep the existing 100-entry / 1 MiB
  caps, so a persistent outage cannot grow either without limit.
- Backend selection reuses the `AILANG_STORAGE` resolver via `storage.NewBackendsForMode` — the
  same switch, taking the mode as a parameter, rather than a second local/gcp/hybrid selector. A
  remote target that resolves to this node's *own* store is rejected with an explanation instead of
  quietly writing to itself.

`UpdateStageEvalAssessment` moved onto the `observatory.Backend` interface (implemented for
Firestore, which is what the deployed observatories run) so a remotely-posted stage carries its
model — without it, cloud-side cost classification cannot resolve a rate.

### Fixed — mission iterations reported $0.0000 while spending real money

A mission iteration spans Anthropic, OpenAI and OpenRouter. Read back, `mission:v1/iter-190` showed
four stages **all `pending`**, its two OpenRouter quorum stages recording **$0.0570 and $0.0507 at
zero tokens**, and a chain total of **$0.0000** against $0.1077 actually spent. Two defects with
different owners, which is why fixing "mission accounting" as one thing would have patched one and
missed the other:

- **Writer-side (status).** `PostIteration` created each stage and never transitioned it, so every
  stage kept `CreateStage`'s `pending` default forever. `IterationStage` now carries a `status`
  from the existing stage vocabulary (`pending`/`running`/`awaiting_approval`/`completed`/`failed`)
  and the stage is transitioned. An unknown value is rejected rather than coerced — coercion would
  report an outcome the caller never claimed.
- **Caller-side (tokens).** The writer already forwarded `tokens_in`/`tokens_out`; the zeros came
  from the poster. The mission-control skill now supplies per-stage token counts, and
  `ailang chains post-iteration` echoes the totals it recorded plus a stderr notice naming how many
  stages posted no status — so a payload that forgot them is visible at the call site rather than
  weeks later in a cost rollup.

Stage costs and tokens now aggregate into the chain total, and a chain whose stages all reported a
terminal outcome closes as `completed` — or `failed` if any stage failed. **A failed stage reads
back `failed`**: blanket-completing every stage would satisfy "nothing is pending" while hiding the
failures the record exists to surface, so that is pinned by a regression test built from the real
iter-190 shape (4 stages, 3 providers, two carrying cost with zero tokens). Omitting `status`
remains valid and behaves exactly as before — the skill and the CLI ship independently.

### Fixed — OpenRouter spans could not find their mission chain

`convertSpan` populated `ChainID` only from `ailang.chain_id`; OpenRouter's Broadcast delivers our
chain id as `session.id`, so every broadcast span landed unattached. Rather than add a parallel
session→chain map, the resolver reuses the linkage that already exists — `sessions` rows already
carry `chain_id` and `stage_id` — via `LookupChainBySessionID` on all five backends, including the
Firestore one that live Broadcast actually hits. It is strictly a fallback: an explicit
`ailang.chain_id` still wins, and a `session.id` with no matching row resolves to empty without
erroring, so **Claude Code spans, which own that attribute today, resolve exactly as they did**.

### Fixed — `exit()` in one batch item killed the whole batch run (#607)

`ailang run --batch` promises per-item isolation: it runs the entrypoint once per input, counts
failures, and prints `Batch complete: X/Y succeeded`. It did not deliver that when an item called
`exit()`.

`exit()` raises a `*eval.EvalExitCode` sentinel panic (`internal/effects/io.go`). The single-file
run path recovers that sentinel and turns it into a clean `os.Exit`; `executeBatchItem` called
`executeModuleEntrypoint` directly with **no recover**, so the sentinel unwound through the batch
loop and out of `main`. The process died with **rc=2** and a raw Go stack trace, and every
remaining input was silently skipped — reported from a 2,500-file PDF batch job, where one bad
file aborted the run.

The recover already existed; one call site did not have it. Now `exit(N)` with `N != 0` fails
**that item** (the loop reports it and continues to the next input), `exit(0)` counts as a
success, and non-exit panics are re-raised unchanged so a genuine crash stays loud.

Before: `[1/2]` panics, rc=2, Go stack, `[2/2]` never runs.
After: `[1/2]` reports `program called exit(1)`, `[2/2]` runs, `Batch complete: 1/2 succeeded`, rc=1.

Batch mode had no regression tests at all; it has five now, covering all four recover branches
(including the re-panic arm, which no `.ail` fixture can reach) plus a single-file control that
distinguishes a batch-mode regression from one in the shared `exit()` mechanism.

### Fixed — the stdlib interface-freeze gate had been dead since v0.0.12

`make verify-stdlib` failed on a clean tree and had done so for ~33 minor versions. It was not
catching a regression; it could not run at all. Three independent breakages, each sufficient on
its own:

1. **Wrong path.** `tools/verify-stdlib.sh` hardcoded `STDLIB_DIR=stdlib/std`. The stdlib lives at
   `std/`; `stdlib/` does not exist.
2. **`ailang iface` could not read ANY std module.** The loader routes any path starting with
   `std/` to the stdlib resolver — correct for an import (`std/io`), but the same branch is hit
   when a std/ *file* is the entry, so `std/io.ail` reached `validateModuleName` as a module NAME
   and the `.` in `.ail` failed the `[a-zA-Z0-9_/-]` guard. The embedded fallback then compounded
   it by looking for `io.ail.ail`. **`ailang check std/io.ail` was broken too — you could not
   type-check your own stdlib.** Fixed by stripping one trailing `.ail` before resolution, which
   cannot mask a real module because `.` is not a legal module-name character.
3. **Failure was invisible.** `set -e` plus `ailang iface … 2>/dev/null | grep -A 10000 '^{'` meant
   a failing `iface` produced no diagnostic and killed the script via `grep`'s exit status. "Gate
   is red" and "gate is broken" looked identical from outside — which is why nobody noticed.

Also fixed, found on the way:

- **Coverage was 5 of 45 modules** (`io list option result string`, hardcoded). Now derived from
  the filesystem, so a new std module is *uncovered and red* rather than silently unwatched.
- **The scripts verified the wrong binary** — bare `ailang` from PATH, not the working tree. Now
  `./bin/ailang` with a PATH fallback, matching `tools/verify_examples.sh`.
- **Stale goldens are now removed** when a module disappears, instead of being verified forever.
- Shared plumbing lives in `tools/stdlib-iface-lib.sh` so freeze and verify cannot drift apart and
  report a script difference as an interface change.

**Goldens re-frozen at 45 modules.** The old ones dated from *Release v0.0.12* (2026-05-22) and
recorded `"module": "stdlib/std/io"` with 3 functions; `std/io` now exports 8, `std/list` went
10 → 30+, and constructors gained arity (`Some` → `Some(a)`). That is legitimate evolution across
33 versions, not regression — but re-freezing **accepts** whatever is current, so it is called out
here rather than done silently, and `freeze-stdlib.sh` now says so at the top.

**`make verify-stdlib-selftest`** proves the gate fails on a real interface change: it injects an
export into `std/option`, then asserts non-zero exit, that the changed module is *named*, and that
the diff shows the new symbol. A green gate is not evidence it works — this is. (`verify-stdlib` is
now wired into `make ci`; the self-test follows the existing convention of staying out of CI.)


### Added — `std/math.bitwiseOr`, a function the teaching prompt has promised since v0.11.4

**The models were following our own instruction.** `cmd/ailang/prompts/v0.16.5.md:1136` tells every
eval model, verbatim:

```
-- Note: bitwise OR (|) is not an operator (conflicts with ADT syntax).
-- Use bitwiseOr(a, b) from std/math instead.
```

`bitwiseOr` was never implemented. Every model that followed the prompt failed with
`IMP010: symbol 'bitwiseOr' not exported by 'std/math'` — the **sole** failure in
`dense_operator_program` for every model tested (gemini-3-flash, gpt5-4-mini, or-deepseek-v4-pro),
and the single blemish on an otherwise-clean 22/23 smoke run. That benchmark asks for
`(12 & 10) | (4 ^ 6)`, so it could not be solved as instructed. Same class as the `fmt` episode:
a claim of ours, believed by the models, wrong for months.

- **`export pure func bitwiseOr(a: int, b: int) -> int`** in `std/math`, implemented in pure AILANG
  via De Morgan (`~(~a & ~b)`) — the operators it needs already exist, so no builtin and no parser
  change. Verified at runtime: `12|10=14`, `0|0=0`, `255|256=511`, `-1|5=-1`, `7|7=7`.
- **`dense_operator_program` is now solvable** — a reference solution using
  `bitwiseOr(12 & 10, 4 ^ 6)` produces byte-exact expected output (`44/10/9/true`).
- **`examples/bitwise_or.ail`** covers the operator set plus the function, registered in the
  examples manifest.

**`|` is deliberately NOT becoming an operator.** `internal/lexer/token.go`'s precedence table
records the exclusion — *"(BITWISE_OR `|` — reserved, not an operator; use bitwiseOr())"* — because
a single pipe already belongs to ADT declarations (`type T = A | B`) and pattern alternatives. The
gap was never a missing operator; it was the missing function the language already advertised.

An audit of all 198 symbols the current prompt tells models to import from `std/*` found
`bitwiseOr` was the **only** genuine gap. The other 27 initially flagged were false positives of the
search — ADT constructors (`Some`, `Ok`, `JString`) and `_`-prefixed builtins (`_xml_parse`,
`_zip_readEntry`) exported by other mechanisms; each confirmed compiling with `ailang check`.

  Compiler Stops* + *MOTHER*) to the bibliography; BibTeX citation no longer
  hard-codes a version.
- **Stale facts fixed**: README install-pin example (v0.30.0 → v0.33.1);
  implementation-status hand-maintained "recent versions" list (frozen at
  v0.6.1/Dec-2025) dropped in favor of the auto-generated design-docs page.
- **Removed** (superseded; content survives in design_docs/ + git history):
  `guides/evaluation/{migration-guide,go-implementation,baseline-tests}.md`,
  `guides/consumer-contract-v0.5.md`.
- **Slimmed**: `agent-integration` (hand-maintained syntax card and frozen
  Oct-2025 success rates removed — the teaching prompt and dashboard own
  those), `claude-code-integration` (503→~120 lines, now a bridge-concept
  page routing to the canonical CLI/hooks/coordinator docs),
  `state-system-workflow` (marketing sections cut),
  `semantic-caching-vs-vectordb` (9 verbose sections → table + 2 examples),

### Added `or-qwen3-8-27b` — OpenRouter quality screen for a rig upgrade candidate (no user-facing change)

- **Qwen3.8 27B clears the screen against the rig incumbent's OpenRouter twin.** Core tier,
  standard mode, ailang+python, 3 trials, 270 runs, $5.34 metered. AILANG **94.2%** (65/69)
  vs `or-qwen3-6-35b-a3b` **78.3%** (54/69) = **+15.9pp**; Python 93.9% vs 89.4%. Across 45
  matched benchmark×lang cells: 12 wins / 3 losses / 30 ties (sign test p=0.035), and
  trial-level McNemar 20 vs 6 discordant (**p=0.0094**). The gain is concentrated in AILANG
  and specifically in *compile-time* correctness — 2 compile errors vs the control's 8.
- **Screened hosted, on purpose: the rig cannot run it.** `ollama pull` returns HTTP 412 for
  every qwen3.8 tag (MLX *and* GGUF) — support landed in ollama 0.32.12 and the rig is on
  0.32.1. Running both arms on OpenRouter keeps the comparison free of an ollama-version
  confound.
- **This is NOT a promotion to the rotation.** qwen3.8 is **dense 27B** — there is no `a3b`
  variant — against the incumbent's 35B/**3B-active** MoE, i.e. 9x the active params on a
  bandwidth-bound box, where timeouts are already the top local failure mode (42 of 80
  failures in the v0.33.1 bank). The screen measures quality only; on-device throughput is
  unmeasured. Promotion additionally requires the ollama upgrade, which moves the incumbent's
  baseline (0.32.10 flips the `repeat_penalty` default 1.1 → 1.0, 0.32.4 changes Qwen3 MoE
  decoding, 0.32.6 changes the `/v1` streaming wire format) and so needs a qwen3.6
  re-baseline on both sides of it.
- Cost cap on the new entry is **price-scaled, not matched** ($1.00 vs the control's $0.30):
  at $3.20/M output vs $1.00/M, an equal dollar cap would have given the candidate ~3.2x less
  token headroom and let the cap, not capability, decide the screen.

### Eval cost accuracy — the pricing gate now covers vendor-direct rows (no user-facing language or CLI change)

- **Added Gemini 3.7 Flash** (`gemini-3-7-flash`, released 2026-08-13) and **gave it the
  `extended_suite` Flash-tier slot over `gemini-3-6-flash`**, which drops to opt-in as the
  longitudinal anchor (the role `gemini-3-5-flash` got when 3.6 displaced it 22 days
  earlier). Vertex GA name `gemini-3.7-flash` verified against the global endpoint on
  project `ailang-dev` (HTTP 200, `modelVersion` echoed, `finishReason: STOP`); the
  `-preview`, `gemini-3-7-flash`, `-flash-lite` and `3.7-pro` variants all 404. Thinks by
  default (552 thinking vs 464 content tokens on a bare prompt), `max_output_tokens: 65536`
  established by API rejection at 131072.
- **The swap is justified by cost and recency, NOT capability.** Pass rates are
  indistinguishable — smoke 23/23 = 23/23, core 23/23 = 23/23 (both at ceiling, so that
  tier ranks nothing), stretch 24/25 = 24/25. The two stretch benchmarks they split on were
  de-flaked at N=3 and both proved flaky for *both* models (`emit_exact_bytes_varied` 3.6
  1/3 vs 3.7 2/3; `run_length_encode` 3.6 1/3 vs 3.7 0/3 — 2/6 each, dead even). The N=1
  reading that 3.7 fixes `emit_exact_bytes_varied`, the standing failure of the Flash line,
  was **retracted** before it reached the annotation. What decides it: identical price
  ($0.75/$3.75 both, since the promo covers 3.6 too) and **42–48% fewer thinking tokens**
  (core 34,405 vs 59,250; stretch 71,468 vs 137,376), making 3.7 12–19% cheaper — $0.925 vs
  $1.147 on stretch, the tier release baselines actually run. No truncation on either
  (`finish=length` 0/50), so the 65536 ceiling is not confounding the comparison.
  `--tier frontier` was NOT measured; the 3.5→3.6 swap had it and this one does not.
- **Corrected five vendor-direct rows** whose prices no gate could see:
  `gemini-3-6-flash` was $1.50/$7.50 per 1M after Google extended a 50% introductory
  cut across the Flash line (2x overstatement); `opencode-haiku`,
  `pi-claude-haiku-4-5` and `motoko-claude-haiku-4-5` were still on Claude Haiku
  **3.5's** $0.25/$1.25 while billing Haiku 4.5 at $1.00/$5.00 (**4x understatement**,
  on exactly the rows that exist to compare harness cost); `opencode-gemini-2.5-flash`
  carried gemini-3-flash's rates. A sixth, `gemini-3-flash-preview`, had **no pricing
  block at all** and so banked real Vertex spend as $0.00.
  Banked results are NOT recomputed, per the precedent set below.
- **Widened the offline CI gates from `provider: "openrouter"` to every billed
  provider** (`TestModels_PricingIsSlugConsistent`, `TestModels_PricingIsPlausible`;
  both renamed). The openrouter-only scope was never a considered choice, and it is
  what hid all six rows above. `ollama` stays out: those 22 rows are legitimately $0.
  Rows are now keyed by provider **and** api_name, since the same model reached
  through a reseller legitimately costs something different.
- **Added `pricing.expires` / `pricing.next`** — a schema for rates known in advance
  to change on a date, with `TestModels_PricingScheduleIsHonoured` enforcing it
  offline. Both Gemini Flash rows are on introductory pricing that **doubles on
  2027-01-01**; before this that reversion lived only in a YAML comment, so on New
  Year's Day every Gemini Flash cost figure would have silently halved with nothing
  going red. Verified by backdating an expiry and watching the gate fail.
- **Extended `make verify-model-pricing` to cross-check google/openai/anthropic rows**
  against OpenRouter's listing of the same model (47 of 51 rows map; the 4 that don't
  are reported, not guessed at). Two designs were rejected on measurement and the
  reasons are recorded in the tool's package comment: a checked-in expected-price
  table only restates the number models.yml already holds, and a staleness date wide
  enough not to nag (90 days) would have sailed past the Gemini miss, which happened
  23 days after that row was last verified.
- **These findings are ADVISORY (exit 0; `--strict` promotes them).** OpenRouter runs
  its own promotions — measured 2026-08-13, it listed `google/gemini-3.7-flash` at
  exactly half Google's published rate, and gpt-5.6-terra/luna and claude-sonnet-5 at
  0.50x/0.50x/0.67x. We bill these models direct, so the vendor's page is the
  authority and OpenRouter is a second opinion. Failing the build on a disagreement
  you are supposed to ignore is how a checker gets ignored. What IS hard-failed for
  vendor rows is decidable offline and sits in CI instead.

### Eval cost accuracy — OpenRouter pricing drift (no user-facing language or CLI change)

- **Corrected 23 of 39 `provider: "openrouter"` rows in `internal/eval_harness/models.yml`**
  against the live OpenRouter models API (audited 2026-08-13). Drift ran from 0.89x to
  3.67x and in **both** directions, so it was not a uniform bias that comparisons could
  absorb: an understated rate silently flatters a model in every cost-per-pass table, an
  overstated one penalises it. Worst cases: `or-deepseek-v3` output 3.67x understated,
  `or-qwen-2-5-72b` input 3.0x, and `or-deepseek-v4-pro` / `opencode-or-deepseek-v4-pro`
  ~2.7x — the last because the row had been holding the price of what is now a *different*
  slug (`deepseek/deepseek-v4-pro-0813`, the newer GA snapshot at $0.435/$0.87 per 1M).
- **Banked results are NOT recomputed.** A banked cost measures what a run cost under the
  rates in force at the time; recomputing it at today's rate would fabricate a figure no
  one was ever charged. Affected baselines get annotated, following the v0.30.0 cost
  invalidation precedent and `m-eval-measurement-contract.md` ("the existing data stays
  as-is, only annotated"). Cross-model cost comparisons that span the correction date
  should be treated as incomparable rather than reconciled.
- **Added `make verify-model-pricing`** (`tools/verify-model-pricing`) — diffs every
  openrouter row against the live API, reports the drift table with per-row factors, and
  exits 1 on drift / 2 when the check *could not run*. The exit-2 distinction is the point:
  "prices are correct" and "I could not find out" are different answers. Deliberately **not**
  in `make ci`, which must not go red because a third party's API is down. Supports
  `--json` and `STRICT=1` (also fail on withdrawn slugs).
- **Added `TestModels_OpenRouterPricingIsSlugConsistent`** — the offline half of the gate,
  which *does* run in CI. Rows sharing an `api_name` must share a price: OpenRouter bills
  the slug and has no idea which harness sent the request, so when `or-glm-5`,
  `opencode-or-glm-5` and `motoko-glm-5` disagree, the harness-lift comparison those rows
  exist to enable is reading a bookkeeping artifact. The audit found 4 such splits, worst
  being `google/gemma-4-26b-a4b-it` with three different prices across four rows — none of
  them the live price. Also added `TestModels_OpenRouterPricingIsPlausible` (no negative
  rates; no non-free row priced at $0, which would report real spend as free).
- Aligned `cache_read_per_1k` across the four `deepseek-v4-flash-0731` rows (only
  `pi-or-deepseek-v4-flash` had declared it). The remaining ~30 rows still declare no cache
  rate, which bills cache reads at the **full input rate** — an overstatement, the safe
  direction, and now reported continuously by `verify-model-pricing` rather than left
  invisible.
- Flagged two rows whose slugs no longer resolve on the API — `motoko-or-qwen3-5-35b-a3b`
  (`qwen/qwen3.5-35b-a3b-20260224`, dated snapshot withdrawn) and `or-laguna-xs-2`
  (`poolside/laguna-xs.2:free`). These are *reachability* defects, not pricing ones; both
  are left un-repointed on purpose, since choosing a replacement revision is a
  model-selection decision rather than a drift correction.
- Documented the pricing policy in the `models.yml` header: `pricing:` is the single source
  of truth, `notes:` prose is dated narrative that must not be rewritten to match new
  prices, and `description:` must not embed a rate at all (it has nowhere to carry a
  verified-on date). Hand-verification decays fast — `or-glm-5-2` carried "verified live
  2026-08-06" and its output rate was already 1.79x stale seven days later.

### Mission infrastructure (no user-facing language or CLI change)

- The mission loop's sprint-planner now defaults to the ChatGPT-subscription codex
  lane instead of opus, so opus stays controller-only. The configured default is not
  the effective lane: a new `tools/launchd/derive-planner-lane.sh` reads the picked
  design doc and fails **closed** to opus unless the doc declares
  `**Planner-Lane**: codex-ok` and every path in its Files section is inside a narrow
  infrastructure allowlist. Rollback is one commented line in
  `~/.config/ailang/mission-<name>.env`. Design docs gain an optional
  `**Planner-Lane**` header field. See
  `design_docs/implemented/v1_0_0/m-planner-codex-lane.md`.

### SECURITY — the prelude's `println` bypassed the capability system

- **`println` reached the runtime by two paths that disagreed on enforcement.** Bare
  `println` (prelude-injected, the *documented* form — "println is in prelude, no import
  needed") was registered in `eval.registerBuiltins` as a plain builtin that wrote to stdout
  via `fmt` directly. `import std/io (println)` routes through `_io_println` →
  `effects.Call` → `RequireCap`. Both spellings declare the same effect (`! {IO}`) and both
  pass effect checking, but only the imported one consulted the capability set. A program
  could therefore perform IO with the **wrong** capabilities, or with **none at all** —
  `--caps` was evadable by simply not importing `std/io`, which defeats the point of a
  capability system.

  | form | `--caps IO` | `--caps Declassify` | no caps |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `import std/io (println)` | prints | blocked | blocked |
  | bare `println` (before) | prints | **prints** | **prints** |
  | bare `println` (after) | prints | blocked | blocked |

- **Fix**: prelude builtins that perform a real effect now gate on the same capability the
  `std/io` path does. `registerBuiltins` takes the evaluator so the closure reads
  `effContext` at call time (`SetEffContext` runs later), asserting a local `capRequirer`
  interface — the existing `budgetChargeScoper` pattern, which avoids the
  `eval`↔`effects` import cycle. Evaluators with no effect context (REPL, `TypedEvaluator`,
  unit tests) are unchanged; they never granted capabilities in the first place.
- **Regression test** `TestPreludeAndImportedPrintln_SameCapabilityGate` pins the invariant
  across **both** resolution paths × {no caps, wrong cap, correct cap}. Verified to FAIL on
  the unfixed binary with `CAPABILITY BYPASS: bare performed IO with caps []`, and only on
  the bare path — the imported path was always correct.
- **Found via an eval, and it had corrupted an eval result.** `benchmarks/prompt_injection.yml`
  declared `caps: ["Declassify"]` while its task says "prints 1" with `expected_stdout: 1`.
  IO was never granted, so the benchmark **only passed for solutions using the evading bare
  form** and FAILED correct code that imported `std/io`. In the qwen3.8-vs-qwen3.6 on-device
  A/B, qwen3.8 wrote the imported form and was scored `runtime_error`; qwen3.6 passed by
  using the bypass. Caps list corrected to `["Declassify", "IO"]`.
- **Audited the rest, and it is NOT systemic**: of 91 benchmarks, 90 already grant IO and
  `prompt_injection` was the only mismatch; 0 examples in `examples/manifest.json` (195) use
  bare `println` without IO. One test needed updating — `TestPreludeDocs_ForwardCompileProbe`
  ran its probe without caps and passed only because of this bug; it now passes `--caps IO`,
  since it tests prelude *resolution*, not capability enforcement.

### The token WORK gate now covers local evals (it only ever covered cloud)

- **`budgets.max_tokens_per_bench` was on 7 models, all cloud, and 0 of 27 local (ollama)
  entries.** Local pricing is `0/0`, so `ResolvedMaxCostUSD` returns 0 and there is no cost
  gate; with no token gate either, **`hard_timeout_secs` was the only gate on every local
  model** — despite `models.go:96` stating wall-clock is "a safety net for hung connections,
  not a cost proxy". The `--max-tokens-per-bench` flag's own help text reads "thrash
  detection **for free local models**", so the instrument was built for this case and never
  pointed at it. This extends the #545 policy (Mark, 2026-07-31: free lanes "gate on TOKENS
  alone — equal work, and no spend to control") to the third free lane.
- **All 27 local entries now carry `max_tokens_per_bench: 3000000`** — the same ceiling the
  cloud lanes use, so a mixed cohort gets equal work — **and `hard_timeout_secs: 3600`**,
  demoting wall-clock to the hung-connection safety net it is documented to be. Seven entries
  were raised from 600/2400s; nine had no budgets block at all and were falling through to the
  600s default.
- **Measured impact — the wrong unit was inverting a result.** Re-scoring the 2026-08-17
  qwen3.8-vs-qwen3.6 on-device A/B (117 runs) under each gate:

  | arm | ungated | 600s wall-clock | 1.5M tokens |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | opencode-qwen3-8-27b | 27/27 | **23/27** | **27/27** |
  | pi-qwen3-8-27b | 29/29 | 28/29 | 29/29 |
  | opencode-qwen3-6 (control) | 24/31 | 24/31 | 24/31 |
  | pi-qwen3-6 (control) | 30/30 | 29/30 | 29/30 |

  The time gate killed 4 passing qwen3.8 runs; the token gate killed none, while still
  aborting the one genuine runaway (a 4.69M-token pi-qwen3.6 run). qwen3.8 in fact uses
  **fewer** tokens than qwen3.6 (median 177k vs 217k on opencode) — it does less work per
  benchmark and merely costs more wall-clock per unit of work on a bandwidth-bound box.
  Charging that to the model produced a false "qwen3.8 is unaffordable on opencode" reading.
- Duplicate-`budgets` hazard noted for anyone scripting this file: entries that already had a
  block need the key **merged in**, not a second block appended. A duplicate mapping key is
  accepted by the runtime loader (last one wins, silently dropping the original
  `max_cost_usd`/`hard_timeout_secs`) and only caught by the stricter test loader.

## [v0.33.1] - 2026-08-13

### Fixed — CI flakes, systemically rather than one red run at a time

A red `dev` outranks the mission queue every time it happens, and the same few failure shapes kept
producing them. Rather than patch each flake, this establishes one gating convention across the Go
test suite: bounded waits instead of unbounded ones, a default-deny egress boundary so a test cannot
silently depend on the live network, and a pure-Go lint for the known offenders.

Five milestones, AC1–AC12 re-verified out-of-sandbox. Closes #583, #494, #509, #587 (CI flakes) and
#561 (local-only network dependency).

One decision is deliberately left open and asserted *as* open by the acceptance criteria: AILANG's
own `Net` effect stays **outside** the egress boundary for now, because `internal/effects` builds its
transports by hand with `Proxy == nil`. Routing those through the proxy is a production-runtime
change, not a test-only one, and is queued as its own design item rather than riding in on a sprint
scoped and reviewed as test infrastructure.

Design doc: [`m-ci-flake-systemic-fix.md`](../design_docs/implemented/v0_33_1/m-ci-flake-systemic-fix.md)

### Fixed — the OTLP/JSON receiver corrupted every trace and span ID it accepted

OTLP/JSON encodes `traceId`/`spanId` as **hex**. `protojson` correctly implements the proto3 JSON
mapping, which encodes `bytes` as **base64**. The observatory fed spec-compliant OTLP/JSON bodies
straight to `protojson`, so every JSON-ingested ID was base64-decoded from its hex text: a 32-char
(16-byte) trace ID was stored as 24 bytes, rendering as 48 hex chars. The endpoint answered
`HTTP 200 {"partialSuccess":{}}` throughout.

Discovered when OpenRouter Broadcast was pointed at production on 2026-08-13: **38 of 38 spans
arrived with 48-char trace IDs**, which also settled that OpenRouter sends OTLP/JSON rather than
protobuf. Trace IDs are the join key for `ailang chains`, so corrupted ones fragment a trace into
singletons and never match a protobuf-sourced span.

It shipped because **nothing tested the decode path** — every existing receiver test handed
`convertSpan` a raw `[]byte` ID and never reached `protojson` or the HTTP handler.

- `normalizeOTLPJSONIDs` rewrites `traceId`/`spanId`/`parentSpanId` (both spellings) from hex to the
  base64 `protojson` expects, keeping one decoder and confining the spec deviation to one function.
  Applied to traces, logs, and metrics, including their content-type-sniffing fallbacks.
- Hex is tried unconditionally rather than sniffed: every 32-char hex string is also valid base64, so
  sniffing has no principled tiebreak.
- Malformed IDs now return a typed **400** and write **no row**, instead of storing garbage under a 200.
- `migrate_v18` repairs existing rows. The corruption is lossless — base64 decoding is injective at
  fixed length — so `base64encode(unhexlify(stored))` recovers the original exactly. Length-guarded,
  hence idempotent; it never deletes, and a repaired ID that would collide with an existing row is
  skipped and reported rather than aborting the migration.
- `CurrentSchemaVersion` added, so a new migration stops reddening unrelated version assertions.

### Added — OTLP ingest can require a shared secret

`/v1/traces`, `/v1/logs` and `/v1/metrics` are publicly reachable on Cloud Run and were
unauthenticated. `AILANG_OTLP_INGEST_TOKEN` now gates them, accepted as `X-AILANG-Ingest-Token` or
`Authorization: Bearer`, compared in constant time.

**Unset or empty = auth disabled**, which is how it ships: Broadcast already streams to production
and the rig posts to `localhost:1957` with no credential, so an enforcing default would break both on
deploy. Enabling it requires the Cloud Run env var *and* the matching header on every producer.
Wrapping happens inside `RegisterRoutes`, so the auth cannot widen to the dashboard UI or the
`/api/observatory/*` reads.

### Added — OpenRouter requests carry Broadcast correlation IDs

`ai.Correlation` (`user`, `session_id`, `trace`) rides on `ai.Request` and is stamped onto all three
OpenRouter build sites — `chat.go` via the struct, `step.go`/`streamstep.go` via the existing
`marshalStepBodyWithExtras` splice. Standard-mode eval runs now tag each request with the chain ID as
`session_id` and `{trace_name, benchmark, tier}` as `trace`, so a broadcast trace joins back to the
run that caused it.

A request setting no correlation is **byte-identical** to before, asserted separately for both body
shapes. Over-cap values (`user` > 128, `session_id` > 256) are rejected before dispatch rather than
truncated — a shortened correlation ID is still well-formed and joins to nothing.

### Fixed — the eval charged the model for the harness's own failures

An audit of `eval_results/baselines/v0.32.0/standard` (877 AILANG rows, 213 failures) found
**47 failures — 22% — that are not model capability failures**: 36 from `docx`/`markdown_reimplement`
sitting at 0% because `input_files` never reaches the standard-mode prompt, 6 from AI-caps
benchmarks run without `--ai`, 5 from an AILANG interpreter dictionary crash. All of them bank
as `compile_error`/`runtime_error` and read downstream as model weakness.

The reason nothing catches this: `CategorizeError` sees only three booleans
(`compileOk/runtimeOk/stdoutOk`) and cannot know *whose* fault a failure was, while
`applyValidityBackstop` quarantines **only** `api_error`. Its comment asserts "every identifiable
MODEL failure has its own category" — false, and inverted from its own stated intent of charging
unknown failures to us and never to the model. Net effect: **4 of 877 rows carry
`validity.valid=false`**.

- **`--caps AI` without `--ai` is fixed at both call sites.** The runner granted the AI capability
  and configured no handler, so all three benchmarks declaring `caps: ["AI", ...]` failed with
  "no AI model configured" and were charged to the model. Now wires `--ai-stub` — deterministic
  by design, because byte-exact grading cannot depend on a second model's response. This does not
  make those three pass: they are `tier: vision` with prose `expected_stdout` placeholders. It
  makes the remaining failure honestly attributable to the spec.
- **New `output_format` error category** separates "solved it, ignored the output contract" from
  "could not solve it". Still a FAIL — byte-exact stdout remains the determinism contract.
  `CategorizeError` is unchanged; the refinement lives in a new `CategorizeErrorWithOutput`, so no
  existing reader of `error_category` shifts meaning.
- **The detector is deliberately conservative, and that is the feature.** Over-matching would
  reclassify wrong answers as cosmetic and inflate capability — the mirror of the bug being fixed.
  It requires equal line counts *and* a label terminated by `:` or `=`, because a naive suffix test
  reads produced `13` as a labelled `3`. It reclassifies **0 of 72** `logic_error` rows in the
  v0.32.0 baseline.

Design docs: `design_docs/planned/m-eval-failure-attribution.md` (producer side) and
`design_docs/planned/m-eval-w8-harness-errors-as-capability-failures.md` (consumer side, **split
out of `m-eval-validity-discipline`** so W8 can route without waiting on the disputed W9). The two
cover disjoint populations: W8's target rows concentrate in agent/motoko rotations (147 invalid
tree-wide, 80 in `motoko_full_core_matrix`) where it delivers alone, while standard-mode baselines
need the producer half first.

Two claims from the originating analysis were **refuted before landing** and are recorded as such:
`IMP010` is models hallucinating stdlib APIs (`bitwiseOr`, `max`, `contains`, `Map`, `Array` exist
nowhere in `std/`), not an export gap; and a claimed 17 "correct values, extra decoration" rows are
genuine wrong answers — `contract_rle_roundtrip` expects a final `true` and produced `1q`, matched
only because a loose heuristic found `true` on an earlier line.

### Fixed — models were being throttled outside the eval harness

Policy, stated once: **outside evals, models run at full ability.** Equalised headroom is an
EVAL device — it makes token counts comparable between models
(`TestModels_CloudHeadroomEqualised`). Nothing else has that excuse, and three paths were
quietly capping thinking anyway. Reasoning tokens bill against the output budget on
OpenRouter and Gemini, so every one of these was a thinking throttle wearing a max-tokens
costume.

- **The pi lane ran at 16384 while the registry declared 65536.** pi has no max-tokens flag:
  it reads `maxTokens` from `~/.pi/agent/models.json` and falls back to 16384 (plus
  `contextWindow` 128000 and `reasoning: false`) for any model that omits it. All four of our
  OpenRouter models were registered as bare `{"id": "..."}`, so the mission's executor
  fallback lane and every pi agent-mode eval ran 4x under the declared budget — with
  DeepSeek V4 Flash 0731's default-on thinking eating the same 16384. This is the mechanism
  behind the executor's three `"stopReason":"length"` failures; two directives were written
  to prevent it by prompting, which could never have worked. `buildPiArgs` cannot forward
  `task.MaxOutputTokens` (no flag exists), so the invariant is now asserted where it can be:
  `tools/pi-extensions/models.mission.json` is the canonical copy and
  `TestPiModelsConfigMatchesRegistry` fails if it drifts from models.yml. The fix has a trap
  — `reasoning: true` alone makes pi send `reasoning: {effort: "none"}`, *disabling* thinking
  — documented in `tools/pi-extensions/README.md` and neutralised via `thinkingLevelMap`.
  Verified live: `stopReason: stop` with a `thinking` block present.
- **Quorum reviewers were capped at a hardcoded 16384** (`internal/mission/quorum`). That
  number replaced a 4096 cap after a live quorum silently degraded to N-1 — gemini-3-1-pro
  hit `finishReason=MAX_TOKENS` mid-verdict — and left the same trap set one octave up. Now
  sourced from the reviewer's declared `max_output_tokens` (65536 for gemini-3-1-pro, 4x the
  old ceiling), and an undeclared budget is a hard refusal rather than a silent fall back to
  the handler's 4096 default.
- **The coordinator's Gemini provider hardcoded 4096** on every research/doc task
  (`internal/coordinator/provider_gemini.go`). Now unset, which omits `maxOutputTokens`
  entirely and leaves the model's own server-side ceiling.
- `reasoning_effort` from models.yml reached standard mode but was dropped on the agent
  path — an entry could declare a dial that never left the registry. Now forwarded to pi as
  `--thinking` (`executor.Task.ReasoningEffort`), with an unknown level a loud pre-dispatch
  error. Unset stays unset: no reasoning control is sent, which is NOT the same as `off`.

**Local (ollama) models, second pass — the three harnesses are now aligned, and two of the
three numbers we thought we had were wrong.** The first pass deferred this as "VRAM-bound,
moving it moves a baseline". Measured instead of assumed: opencode's per-model
`max_tokens: 4096` is **not reaching the wire** (banked sessions show qwen3.6 outputs to
31,703 tokens and `length` finishes at exactly 32,000), and motoko was on **32768**, not
8192 — the 8192 belonged to the `pi-`/`opencode-` rows. So models.yml disagreed with itself:
32768 on `motoko-local-*` and 8192 on `pi-`/`opencode-*`, same model, same wire.

  Aligned at **32768** — the value the lane with the most banked local data already used, so
  motoko does not move, opencode moves ~2%, and only pi actually changes (16384 → 32768).
  `TestPiModelsConfigMatchesRegistry` now covers ollama rows and fails on registry
  self-disagreement. ⚠️ Baseline boundary **2026-08-13**: local qwen rows banked earlier ran
  on a per-harness budget and are not comparable across harnesses. `gemma4:26b` is unchanged
  (not established as a heavy reasoner in our data); `gemma4:26b-ailang`'s 4096 is genuinely
  deliberate — it matches `num_predict` in its Modelfile — and is untouched.

- **`num_ctx` was pinned to 8192 on the AILANG ollama path, below the prompts we send.**
  Both option maps in `internal/ai/ollama` hardcoded it ("Reasonable context window",
  2026-05-05, written before the local-model rotation existed). `num_ctx` bounds prompt AND
  generation together, while the harness's agentic prompts for these models measure
  **28k–44k tokens** (banked opencode sessions, first step-finish) — so ollama context-shifted
  and the model answered having seen a fraction of the task, while the same map asked for up
  to 32768 tokens of generation inside that 8192. Now omitted by default, letting ollama size
  context from the model (measured 262144), which is what the `/v1` lanes already get;
  `AILANG_OLLAMA_NUM_CTX` pins it when VRAM demands. Scope, so it is not over-read: motoko's
  **tool-calling turns never came through here** (they route to `/v1`); what did is
  `Generate`, tool-less chat, and the legacy native tool path — including motoko's
  `compaction_ai` summaries, i.e. the calls whose whole job is to digest a long context.

### Security

- **GitHub-sourced work is now authorised by AUTHOR, not just by label — and the webhook
  fails closed.** Audit prompted by the wave of sock-puppet supply-chain attacks on open
  source. Three findings, all on the path from a public issue to autonomous execution:

  1. **`import-github` had no author check at all** — it filtered on labels only
     ([messages_github.go](cmd/ailang/messages_github.go)), while
     `.github/ISSUE_TEMPLATE/bug_report.yml` **auto-applies `bug`** (and
     `feature_request.yml` `enhancement`) for *any* user, no write access required. So
     "only collaborators can label" was never true. Meanwhile `handleWebhookOpened`'s
     chain runs `syncRepoIssues` → `pollAndProcessTasks` → **`executeTaskQueue`**.
     Fixed with `GitHubConfig.TrustedAuthors` + `IsTrustedAuthor` (case-insensitive —
     GitHub logins are). Untrusted issues still import, because dropping public feedback
     is a worse failure; they import **inert**: no category (so the auto-triage router
     can't promote them), no inbox routing, and a `github-untrusted:<login>` sender.
     Default is `[expected_user]`, which is the sole author of all 51 open issues, so
     nothing in flight changes.
  2. **Sender identity was spoofable from the issue title.** `fromAgent` was parsed from
     the `[agent-name]` prefix, so an outsider titling an issue `[mission-world] …`
     imported as that sibling mission. The prefix is now honoured only for trusted authors.
  3. **The webhook signature check was fail-open** — `if secret != ""`, so an unset
     `GITHUB_WEBHOOK_SECRET` skipped verification entirely and accepted unsigned POSTs.
     Now 503s without a secret, and the route isn't registered at all in that state.
     (It is set on the live Cloud Run coordinator, so this is defence against a future
     misconfiguration, not an open hole being closed.)

  Worth stating precisely, because it drove the design: the HMAC authenticates the
  **transport** (the request really came from GitHub), never the **principal** — GitHub
  signs webhooks for issues opened by anyone on a public repo. Author authorisation has
  to happen at import, which is where it now lives.

- **ailang#611: the executor fallback chain is real, and both missions route identically.**
  Ratified since 2026-08-06 ("codex as default but deepseek to be replacement when codex out
  of quota"), but NEITHER driver implemented it: the codex and pi pre-flight loops were
  independent and each degraded straight to a hardcoded `opus`. Deepseek was reachable only by
  hard-pinning it in a mission env file — which made it the **default**, the opposite of the
  policy. V1 had exactly such a pin and had been running deepseek-first for days.
  Implemented by retargeting the codex loop's fallback to the chain's next link rather than
  adding a third probe loop: the pi loop already runs immediately after, so a `pi:*` handoff is
  probed there and degrades to opus on its own failure. Per-role tail overridable via
  `MISSION_<ROLE>_FALLBACK`.
  **World needed more than a retarget** — its driver had *no pi loop at all*, so a `pi:*` value
  would have been exported unprobed, the exact defect `mission-world.env` warned about. The
  role-generic pi pre-flight is ported there too, its 48-line hand-written stopgap deleted as
  that block itself instructed, and `tools/pi-extensions/` copied across so the skill's
  `$REPO/tools/pi-extensions/...` invocation resolves under World's checkout.
  Verified on BOTH drivers, all three links: codex healthy -> codex; codex unusable -> probed
  `pi:…:floor`; both unusable -> opus. Plus a live two-layer containment check inside World's
  own checkout (bash write to `$HOME` -> `Operation not permitted`).
  **Deliberately NOT made consistent:** World keeps `MISSION_PLANNER_MODEL=opus` where V1 uses
  codex. That is a per-mission choice, not drift, and a wholesale driver copy would have
  silently changed World's planner routing.

- **The token KPI now counts UNCACHED work only (Mark, 2026-08-11) — counting cache reads
  penalised the behaviour we want.** A cache read costs ~20% of a fresh token, so charging
  it as a whole one made a run that caches well look *more* expensive than one that does
  not. AILANG was hit hardest, because its large teaching prompt is the most cacheable
  object in the system: measured, `total_tokens` read **3.9x** the real work on an AILANG
  run at a 75% hit rate (2.7-3.8x on Python). `rotation_summary`'s `tokens_pass_mean` /
  `tokens_fail_mean` — the tokens-to-pass family that became primary once pass/fail
  saturated — now use `RunMetrics.FreshTokens()` = uncached input + output + reasoning.
  On a live run: **30,998** fresh vs the ~93,500 the blended metric would have reported.

  **The trap this is built around:** agent rows banked before 2026-08-11 have no cache
  fields, and `omitempty` makes absent indistinguishable from zero. Subtracting naively
  would score every historical row as 100% fresh, inflating it and manufacturing an
  improvement out of a schema change — the baseline-shift confound this charter keeps
  getting caught by. So a new `cache_accounted` flag marks rows whose split is genuinely
  known (set for EVERY agent run now, including real zeros like a local ollama model with
  no cache), `FreshTokens()` refuses unaccounted rows rather than guessing, and
  `tokens_cache_unaccounted` reports how many trials were dropped — a shrunken sample
  stated out loud instead of silently.

  Comparability: token KPIs are NOT comparable across the boundary. Pre-2026-08-11 agent
  baselines report blended tokens; post rows report fresh, and mixed cohorts will show a
  non-zero `tokens_cache_unaccounted`. Cost, pass rate and best-of-N are unaffected.

- **Agent-mode cache tokens are banked, and cache reads are finally priced — agent cost
  has been understated ~2x, not the ~25-30% the code claimed.** `RunMetrics` has carried
  `cache_read_input_tokens` since v0.31.0, but **only the standard-mode repair path ever
  set it**; every agent row omitted it, so the agent cache hit rate was unmeasurable from
  our own data — the exact gap v0.31.0 set out to close, left open on the agent side.
  Worse, agent mode folded cache reads INTO `input_tokens` for reporting while costing the
  cache-exclusive value, so `input_tokens` and `cost_usd` described different quantities
  and neither could be derived from the other.
  Now: `Pricing.CacheReadPer1K` (`cache_read_per_1k`) plus
  `CalculateCostForModelWithCache`, and agent mode banks the disjoint split.
  **A model with no declared cache rate bills cache reads at the FULL input rate** — an
  overstatement is visible in a budget, whereas $0 hides both the spend and a broken cache.
  Measured on the first re-run: hit rates **75.2% / 75.0%** (AILANG) and **65.5% / 76.1%**
  (Python), and the same 4-run suite reprices **$0.0070 -> $0.0143**.

- **pi/OpenRouter lane price-pinned with `:floor`, and the rate card re-measured.**
  `models.yml` hardcoded $0.09/M input; OpenRouter's endpoints API shows **26 hosts
  serving this model at $0.08-0.14/M with cache reads at $0.016-0.028/M and context
  ceilings from 131k to 1,048k**. A bare model id load-balances across all of them, so the
  banked rate was a guess and the context window was nondeterministic. `:floor`
  (`provider.sort=price`) makes routing deterministic and selects DeepInfra, which the
  rate card now tracks exactly.
  **Honest limits:** `:floor` is a price SORT, not a hard pin — if the cheapest host
  changes, routing moves. A hard pin needs `provider.only`, which pi's `ProviderConfig`
  cannot express (only `baseUrl`/`apiKey`/`api`/`models`/`headers`, and OpenRouter uses
  headers only for `x-anthropic-beta`). And pinning is **not** demonstrated to improve
  caching: it was adopted for rate-card and context-window determinism.
  Applied to the pi entry and `mission-v1.env` only; the motoko/opencode twins on the same
  model are unmeasured and unchanged.

  **A claim of mine this corrects:** two identical back-to-back pi *invocations* cached 0
  of ~27.7k tokens, and I read that as "caching is broken". It is not — reconstructing the
  pre-change run from its own cost shows **~83% of input was already cache-served** inside
  each agent run. Cross-invocation caching is a different, far less valuable thing than
  within-run caching, and the eval loop only needs the latter.

- **`scripts/mission_directives.sh` — the mission directive allowlist is now enforced in
  code, not only in prose.** The control that decides whose GitHub comment can steer an
  autonomous loop was a `jq ... select(.author.login == "…")` written into the
  mission-control skill's instructions: correct, but one paraphrase deep. The script
  takes the allowlist as jq *data* rather than interpolated program text, downcases both
  sides (GitHub logins are case-insensitive), and refuses on a set-but-empty allowlist —
  a loop that quietly stops seeing its human looks exactly like a human who stopped
  commenting. It deliberately does **not** write the watermark; the skill still does that
  after it has actually triaged, so a crash mid-triage cannot skip a directive.

  It also adds a guard the prose never had: **refuse if the allowlist contains the
  authenticated account.** Otherwise the loop could steer itself by commenting on its own
  bookkeeping issue, and every downstream guard would read that as a human instruction.

  Verified with a positive control, because a zero here proves nothing on its own: `#559`
  yields **2** directives out of 69 comments — the same count iteration 168 recorded via
  the inline jq — while `#635`'s single bot-authored comment is correctly excluded (0).
  Plus case-insensitive match, `--since` filtering (2 → 0), set-but-empty refusal, and
  the self-direction guard all exercised. `bash -n` clean under the rig's bash 3.2.57,
  `shellcheck -S error` clean. The mission-control skill now calls it instead of hand-rolling
  the `gh | jq` pipeline, and says so explicitly.

- **External-origin inbox messages never auto-outrank the mission queue.** Cross-mission
  messages already had this carve-out; imported GitHub issues did not — and the importer
  turns issues into inbox messages, where Gate 0 says "a genuine regression or human
  directive OUTRANKS the queue". Combined with public issue templates auto-applying
  `bug`/`enhancement`, that was the last prompt-level path from an arbitrary account to
  this loop's priorities. Messages sent by `github-untrusted:*` are now explicitly public
  feedback: read, never obeyed, never the pick, never unparking anything; if substantive
  they enter the queue on their own live-repro evidence rather than on the strength of the
  request. The prefix is machine-set by the importer, not read from the issue title, so it
  cannot be spoofed by titling an issue `[mission-world] …`.

- **`dev.ailang.mission-recovery` proved itself in production hours after shipping.**
  2026-08-10: 15:51:38 every controller probe timed out and the loop refused; 15:53:31 the
  watcher saw the `.blocked` marker at 113s and kickstarted; 16:04:23 an iteration started
  (on Fable, after the opus probes timed out again). **~13 minutes instead of 90** — about
  77 minutes of loop time reclaimed on its first real firing, and the marker cleared
  correctly on the successful probe.

- **The local coordinator binds `127.0.0.1` instead of `0.0.0.0`.** Cloud Run requires
  `0.0.0.0`; the local daemon does not, and binding every interface exposed `/health`
  plus the API-key surface to the LAN and tailnet for no reason. Mode-aware, with
  `COORDINATOR_BIND_ADDR` to override. Takes effect on daemon restart.

  Not a finding, and corrected here because an earlier read of mine said otherwise: the
  local daemon never served `/github/webhook` at all — it is registered only under
  `COORDINATOR_MODE=cloud`, verified by a live `404` on `:8765`.

### Fixed

- **Fixed-length list patterns now enforce exact arity in the bytecode VM (#505).** Closed
  patterns such as `[x]` and `[a, b]` no longer match longer lists and silently discard the
  remaining elements. Tailed patterns (`[x, ...rest]`) and `::` cons patterns retain their
  intentional “at least n” behavior.

- **Strict `take` after `flatMap` or an allocating `map` no longer has to materialise every
  unvisited output.** `std/list` now exposes the existing fused `takeFlatMap(n, f, xs)` and
  `takeMap(n, f, xs)` builtins, with the strict-evaluation limitation and remaining memory
  bounds documented. `/usr/bin/time -l` release evidence for both rewritten reproductions —
  the #617 `flatMap` case and the allocating-`f` `map` case:

  | Form | Peak RSS | Wall time |
  |---|---:|---:|
  | V1 unfused `take(5, flatMap(f, xs))` | 425 MB | 81.9 s |
  | V2 fused `takeFlatMap(5, f, xs)` | 89 MB | 0.06 s |
  | V26 unfused `take(5, map(f, xs))`, allocating `f` | 559 MB | 18.78 s |
  | V25 fused `takeMap(5, f, xs)`, same `f` | 101 MB | 0.08 s |

  Both pairs compute byte-identical output. A *non-allocating* scalar `f` does not amplify
  (V7), which is why the `map` half is stated as conditional on `f` allocating rather than as
  a blanket rule. The fused form removes outputs for unvisited inputs; it does not cap source
  residency or a single large `f(x)`. The non-blocking `LIST_TAKE_AFTER_FLATMAP` check-time note
  now detects direct `take(n, flatMap(f, xs))` calls and suggests `takeFlatMap(n, f, xs)`; fused,
  `take`-after-`map`, and `sortBy` calls remain quiet. These exports landed without the currently
  stale `make verify-stdlib` path; repairing
  that separate gate remains owned by `m-verify-stdlib-stale-path`.

- **Structural contract generators now exercise record, ADT, tuple, and unit parameters**
  ([#517](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/517), Lane B1). Across the contracts corpus,
  **87 previously-never-executed properties in 15 examples now run**, reducing vacuous skips from
  111 to 24. Eight files flip from exit 1 to exit 0: `access_control.ail`, `cross_function.ail`,
  `nested_record_verify.ail`, `park.ail`, `record_adt_cycle_verify.ail`,
  `record_adt_sort_verify.ail`, `record_discovery_verify.ail`, and
  `record_pattern_verify.ail`. The 24 remaining skips are deliberately outside B1: imported types
  in `cross_module_types.ail` and `inbox_v2_app.ail`, plus refined `string<email>` parameters in
  the prompt-injection examples. Those require imported-type resolution and refinement-aware/B2
  generation respectively.

- **A contract property test could pass on a value the harness had silently
  invented.** When the generator produced a value kind the splicer had no arm
  for, `valueToLiteral` quietly substituted `()` and the property was then
  checked against that fabricated unit — so the run reported a pass for an input
  it never actually tested. The splicer now **refuses**, returning an error, and
  all three property paths (`ensures`, `requires`, `forall`) turn a refusal into
  a `StatusFail` naming the offending Go type. Deliberately **never a skip**: a
  skip would put the vacuous hole straight back. This is Lane B1 milestone M2 of
  [#517](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/517), and it is the second
  half of the mandated ordering — the splice arms from M1 had to exist first,
  or every unit-typed value would have started failing.

  Making the refusal *observable* needed a small production change, which is
  worth naming because it is the point rather than a side effect: the four
  call-site branches were unreachable from any test, since every generator
  reachable today yields a kind M1 already splices — and they stay unreachable
  after the rest of Lane B1, so they would have been permanently defensive code
  nothing protects. `Runner` therefore gains an injectable generator seam, and
  each branch now has a test that reds when the branch is neutered. One of the
  five does not: the shrink path's guard is shadowed by the adjacent
  `EvaluateExpression` error branch, so it is **declared redundant in the code**
  with the measurement, and the implicit contract it rests on — that evaluating a
  nil expression errors rather than panics — is pinned by its own test, which
  reds if that ever changes.

### Added

- **Opt-in streaming for Ollama's OpenAI-compatible `/v1` tool-calling path, with a
  mandatory total-time bound** — milestone M2 of
  [#618](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/618). Set
  `AILANG_OLLAMA_V1_STREAM=1` to route `Step`'s `/v1` call through
  `openai.StreamStep`, wrapped in the idle/TTFT/hard-deadline reader landed by M1.
  A stalled stream now fails in seconds with a typed `ErrIdleTimeout` /
  `ErrTTFTTimeout` instead of burning the whole 300s client cap, and a stream that
  emits keep-alives forever is bounded by a hard deadline (`ErrStreamDeadlineExceeded`)
  rather than resetting the idle timer indefinitely. **Default OFF and byte-identical
  when unset** — with the flag absent the request carries no `stream`, no
  `stream_options` and no `text/event-stream` `Accept`, pinned by a test that captures
  the raw wire body.

  Two semantics worth knowing. `AILANG_OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_SEC` now means different
  things per path: on the legacy buffered path `0` still means "uncapped", while on the
  streaming path `0` or a negative value is a **configuration error**
  (`ErrStreamDeadlineInvalid`) refused before any request leaves — a stream with no
  total bound is the failure this milestone exists to remove. And the streaming branch
  derives its deadline from the context captured *before* the buffered path's 300s
  wrap; deriving it after would have silently capped every stream at 300s and shipped
  the feature inert.

- **The opt-in Ollama `/v1` streaming path now has full response parity with the
  buffered path** — milestone M3 of
  [#618](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/618). Streaming a Qwen3
  thinking model previously discarded two things the buffered path keeps:
  `Response.Reasoning` (the model's thinking text) and Hermes tool-call recovery
  (lifting a `<tool_call>` block the model emitted inside its reasoning when no
  native `tool_calls` were produced). Without this, turning the flag on would have
  traded a timeout bug for motoko's dominant **disengagement** failure mode. The
  streaming branch now accumulates thinking deltas via a non-nil `onChunk`, sets
  `resp.Reasoning`, and runs the identical zero-native-tool-calls recovery as the
  buffered path (`openai.ExtractHermesToolCalls`, now exported). Pinned by a
  three-case streamed-≡-buffered parity table asserting `Text`, `ToolCalls` (order,
  IDs, assembled arguments), `FinishReason` and `Reasoning` are byte-identical.

- **The rig now opts into Ollama `/v1` streaming, and the 1800s timeout stopgap is
  gone** — milestone M4 of
  [#618](https://github.com/sunholo-data/ailang/issues/618), which closes the
  sprint. Both eval launchd plists (`dev.ailang.os-rotation-filler`,
  `dev.ailang.nightly-eval`) drop the 2026-08-07 `AILANG_OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_SEC=1800`
  pin and set `AILANG_OLLAMA_V1_STREAM=1` in the same edit. Removing the pin is
  **mandatory, not tidy-up**: flag-on, that same variable stops being an HTTP client
  timeout and becomes the stream's hard deadline, and 1800s sits *below* the computed
  worst-case legitimate request (~2941s) — leaving it would have re-created the
  `took=4m59.97` failure at a larger scale. Unset, the hard deadline defaults to
  3600s and hangs are caught by the sharper idle (120s) and TTFT (600s) windows.

  **The pin had a second delivery site, and the plist edit does not reach it.** On the
  rig, `AILANG_OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_SEC=1800` was *also* a launchd **user-domain
  global** (`launchctl setenv`), inherited by every job in the domain, invisible to
  any grep over the repo, and untouched by editing `EnvironmentVariables`. With both
  plists already clean, streamed requests still logged `hard_deadline_sec = 1800` and
  `effective_deadline_sec = 1800` rather than the 3600s default — caught only because
  `effective_deadline_sec` is **read back** from the request context instead of
  reported from the configured value. Clearing it needs
  `launchctl unsetenv AILANG_OLLAMA_HTTP_TIMEOUT_SEC`; audit with
  `launchctl getenv …` against a known-unset control name.

  Note the ordering, because the obvious cleanup order is the harmful one: the
  global is **load-bearing until the flag is on**. The repo plists are source — the
  installed copies under `~/Library/LaunchAgents/` are regular files updated by a
  manual `cp` + `launchctl load` — so with the flag not yet installed the buffered
  path runs, `ollamaV1Timeout()` falls back to **300s**, and the global is the only
  thing raising it. Install the flag-on plists **first**, clear the global
  **second**; the reverse order re-creates the very defect this milestone closes.
  Documented in `docs/docs/guides/debugging.md` and `.claude/rules/dev-workflow.md`.
